Chapter 7 Family, Couples and Group Therapies

In this chapter
  1. 7.1 The Family as a System: explain how general systems theory and cybernetics turned the family into the unit of treatment, and define homeostasis, feedback, circular causality, the identified patient and the family life cycle.
  2. 7.2 Families and Schizophrenia: From Blame to Expressed Emotion: describe the early theories that blamed families for schizophrenia, explain why they failed, and show how expressed emotion research led to family psychoeducation that prevents relapse.
  3. 7.3 Bowen's Family Systems Theory: explain Murray Bowen's eight concepts, his coaching style of therapy and the genogram, and tell the genogram apart from the ecomap and the sociogram.
  4. 7.4 Minuchin's Structural Family Therapy: describe subsystems, boundaries, enmeshment and disengagement, and the techniques of joining, enactment, unbalancing and restructuring.
  5. 7.5 Strategic, Brief and Systemic Therapies: Haley, the MRI and Milan: explain directives, paradox, ordeals and pretend techniques, first-order and second-order change, and the Milan team's hypothesising, circularity and neutrality.
  6. 7.6 Experiential and Contextual Family Therapies: describe Virginia Satir's communication stances and sculpting, Carl Whitaker's symbolic-experiential therapy and Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy's contextual therapy of loyalties and fairness.
  7. 7.7 Behavioural, Postmodern and Evidence-Based Family Work: explain behavioural family therapy and parent training, narrative and solution-focused family work, functional family therapy and multisystemic therapy, and the evidence, gender and culture questions family therapists face.
  8. 7.8 Couples Therapy: compare behavioural, integrative behavioural and emotionally focused couple therapy, and describe Gottman's research on what predicts divorce.
  9. 7.9 Sex Therapy: describe Masters and Johnson's programme and sensate focus, the squeeze and stop-start techniques, Helen Singer Kaplan's brief sex therapy and Annon's PLISSIT model.
  10. 7.10 Group Psychotherapy: trace the history of group therapy, explain Yalom's eleven therapeutic factors and the here-and-now, and describe the leader's tasks, group composition and the stages of group development.
  11. 7.11 Psychodrama, Encounter Groups, Self-Help and Therapeutic Communities: describe Moreno's psychodrama and sociometry, T-groups and encounter groups, Alcoholics Anonymous and other mutual-aid groups, milieu therapy and Maxwell Jones's therapeutic community.

Weightage. 28 of the 422 questions mapped in this book (6.6%) test this chapter, 11 of them from real past papers. 1 of its 11 sections is High yield.

In the early 1950s a British-born anthropologist named Gregory Bateson (1904 to 1980) was working at the Veterans Administration hospital in Palo Alto, California. Bateson had made his name far from any hospital: he had lived among the Iatmul people of New Guinea and, with his wife Margaret Mead, had photographed and filmed village life in Bali. What fascinated him was a puzzle about communication. Every message, he noticed, carries a second message about how the first one is to be taken. A friend who says "I'll kill you" with a grin is not making a threat; a parent who says "Go to bed" is also saying "I am in charge here". In 1952 Bateson started a research project on these layers of communication, and over the next two years he was joined by Jay Haley, a young student of communication, John Weakland, a chemical engineer turned anthropologist, and the psychiatrist Don Jackson. They listened to the talk of patients with schizophrenia, and then they began to watch those patients with their families.

One scene from their notes became the most famous anecdote in the history of family therapy. A young man who had fairly well recovered from an acute schizophrenic episode was visited in hospital by his mother. He was glad to see her and impulsively put his arm around her shoulders, whereupon she stiffened. He withdrew his arm, and she asked, "Don't you love me any more?" He blushed, and she said, "Dear, you must not be so easily embarrassed and afraid of your feelings." The young man could stay with her only a few minutes more. After she left, he assaulted an aide and was put in the tubs (the continuous baths then used to calm disturbed patients).

Look at the young man's position. If he moves towards his mother, her body tells him he is unwelcome. If he moves away, her words accuse him of not loving her. If he shows confusion, he is told that his feelings are the problem. Every move is wrong, he cannot point out the contradiction (she is his mother, and he is the patient), and he cannot walk away. In 1956 Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland published a paper called "Toward a theory of schizophrenia" in which they argued that a child who grows up inside such no-win situations, over and over, might learn the confused, metaphorical, guarded style of communication that we call schizophrenic. They called the trap a double bind.

The idea was bold, and it was mostly wrong as an account of what causes schizophrenia. But notice what it did. It moved the illness, at least in part, out of the young man's head and into the space between him and his mother. That move, from the individual to the relationship, is the foundation of everything in this chapter: family therapy, which treats the family as a living system; couples therapy, which treats the relationship between two partners; and group therapy, which uses a room full of strangers as a laboratory for relationships. Remember the young man and his mother; you will return to them at the end of the chapter, and you will see what became of the idea that began with them.

7.1 The Family as a SystemLower yield · 1 Q

For the first half of the twentieth century, psychotherapy meant a private conversation between two people: one patient and one therapist, behind a closed door. The patient's family was, if anything, an obstacle. Psychoanalysts in particular kept relatives at arm's length, because contact with them was thought to contaminate the transference (the patient's feelings about the analyst). Yet clinicians kept noticing things that the one-to-one model could not explain. A young woman who recovered well in hospital relapsed within weeks of going home. A boy's tantrums stopped, and his father promptly became depressed. A couple's quarrels vanished whenever their daughter refused to eat, and returned when she began eating again. It was as if the problem did not belong to one person at all, but moved around the family like water finding its level. This section explains the ideas that let therapists take that observation seriously.

From the consulting room to the family

Family therapy had several roots. The first was the child guidance clinic. From the 1920s, clinics in the United States and Britain treated troubled children with a division of labour: a psychiatrist saw the child, and a social worker saw the mother, usually separately and often with the unspoken assumption that the mother was the cause of the trouble. The second root was marriage counselling. The first marriage counselling centres opened in the 1930s, in Los Angeles under Paul Popenoe and in New York under Abraham and Hannah Stone, and at first each partner was usually seen by a different counsellor. Only in the 1950s did conjoint therapy, in which both partners (or all family members) are seen together by the same therapist, become common.

The third root was a handful of psychiatrists who simply started inviting the whole family in. In 1949 the British psychiatrist John Bowlby (1907 to 1990), better known for attachment theory, described holding a joint meeting with a troubled adolescent and his parents at the Tavistock Clinic in London. In 1951 the American psychologist John Elderkin Bell (1913 to 1995) began treating whole families as groups at Clark University in Massachusetts. And in New York the child psychiatrist and psychoanalyst Nathan Ackerman (1908 to 1971) began, in the 1940s and 1950s, to see entire families for both diagnosis and treatment, watching how they sat, who spoke for whom and who fell silent. Ackerman's book The Psychodynamics of Family Life (1958) and his warm, provocative style earned him the title "grandfather of family therapy", although he never wrote down a method that others could follow step by step.

The fourth and most important root was research on schizophrenia. During the 1950s at least four research teams, working independently, studied the families of patients with schizophrenia: Bateson's group in Palo Alto, Murray Bowen's at the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH), Theodore Lidz's at Yale and Lyman Wynne's, also at NIMH. You will meet each of them in Section 7.2. By 1962 the new field had its own journal, Family Process, founded by Ackerman and Don Jackson with Jay Haley as its first editor.

It helps to separate two terms at the start. Family therapy is any psychotherapeutic treatment of a family aimed at better psychological functioning; a psychoanalyst, a behaviour therapist or a person-centred counsellor can all do family therapy in their own style. Family systems therapy is the narrower family of approaches that treat the family as a system: they concentrate on the interactions between members, not the personalities of individuals, and they try to change the pattern rather than any one person. Most of this chapter is about family systems therapy, and to understand it you need to borrow some ideas from biology and engineering.

General systems theory and cybernetics

The key idea came from the Austrian biologist Ludwig von Bertalanffy (1901 to 1972). From the late 1930s onwards, and most fully in his book General System Theory (1968), von Bertalanffy argued that living things cannot be understood by taking them apart and studying the pieces one at a time. A cell, an organ, an organism, a family and a society are all systems: sets of parts in continuous interaction, organised so that the whole behaves in ways that no single part can explain. His general systems theory proposed a set of principles that apply to every system, whatever it is made of. Four of them matter for families.

  • Wholeness. The whole is more than the sum of its parts. You can interview a mother, a father and a son separately and still know nothing about what happens when the three of them sit down to dinner, because the family has properties (its rules, its alliances, its typical arguments) that exist only between people.
  • Interdependence. A change in any part changes the whole. When the eldest daughter leaves for college in Bengaluru, the whole household reorganises: the younger son gets her room, the mother loses her ally, the parents find themselves alone together at dinner.
  • Open systems. A closed system exchanges nothing with its surroundings and runs down; an open system, like every living thing and every family, constantly exchanges energy and information with its environment (school, work, relatives, neighbours, the media) and can grow more complex over time.
  • Equifinality. In an open system the same end state can be reached from different starting points and by different routes. Two families can arrive at the same problem (a teenager who will not go to school) by completely different histories, which is why systems therapists care more about the pattern that maintains a problem today than about how it began.

Systems are also nested in a hierarchy. Each family is part of a larger system (a neighbourhood, a caste or community, a town, a culture), and each member is a smaller system of organs and cells. A systems therapist always asks at which level a problem is best understood.

The second borrowed idea was cybernetics, the science of self-regulating systems and of communication and control in machines and animals, named by the American mathematician Norbert Wiener (1894 to 1964) in his book Cybernetics (1948). Wiener had worked during the Second World War on anti-aircraft fire control: aiming a gun at a moving plane by feeding information about the plane's course back into the aiming system, so that the aim was corrected continuously. The central concept is feedback: information about the output of a system that is fed back into the system and changes what it does next. Bateson attended the famous Macy conferences in New York at which Wiener and others developed these ideas, and he carried them straight into the study of families.

Feedback and homeostasis

Think of the thermostat on an air conditioner set to 24 degrees. When the room warms above 24, the thermostat switches the compressor on; when the room cools below 24, it switches it off. The room temperature wobbles but stays close to the set point. A system that holds itself steady in this way shows homeostasis, a term the physiologist Walter Cannon had used for the body's regulation of temperature and blood sugar. In 1957 Don Jackson (1920 to 1968) published a paper on "the question of family homeostasis", arguing that families too have set points. Each family has unwritten family rules about who may say what to whom, how close people may be, how anger is expressed and who makes decisions, and when a member breaks a rule the others act, often without realising it, to restore the old balance.

Two kinds of feedback do the work. Negative feedback reduces deviation and restores the previous state; it is the thermostat switching the compressor off. Positive feedback amplifies deviation and pushes the system further from where it was, as when a microphone held near a loudspeaker produces an ever louder howl. Here is the trap that every examiner sets: in systems language, "negative" and "positive" have nothing to do with bad and good. Suppose sixteen-year-old Priya comes home at midnight. If her father sits her down, listens and they agree on a curfew, the family returns to its usual pattern: that is negative feedback, and here it is helpful. If her father shouts, she stays out later, he shouts louder and she stops coming home at all, the deviation is being amplified: that is positive feedback, and here it is destructive. But positive feedback can also be the only route to healthy change, as when a family that has always avoided conflict is finally pushed into the open argument it needed.

Later writers gave the two tendencies names. Morphostasis is a system's tendency to keep its structure stable; morphogenesis is its capacity to change its structure and grow. A healthy family needs both: enough stability to give its members a secure base, and enough flexibility to reorganise when a baby is born, a grandparent moves in or a child grows up. Many family problems arise when a family clings to morphostasis at a moment when life demands morphogenesis, for example by treating a seventeen-year-old exactly as it treated him at seven.

Exam trap

Therapists who describe the family as a system with homeostasis, boundaries and feedback loops are applying general systems theory (Ludwig von Bertalanffy), together with Norbert Wiener's cybernetics. The distractors are operant conditioning (Skinner's learning theory, which explains behaviour by its consequences for one organism), psychosexual stage theory (Freud's account of individual development) and the medical model (which locates the illness inside one patient, the opposite of systems thinking). And remember: negative feedback restores the old balance, positive feedback amplifies change; neither word means good or bad.

Circular causality and the identified patient

Ordinary explanations are linear: A causes B. The husband drinks because his wife nags; the wife nags because her husband drinks. Each partner offers a perfectly sensible linear story in which the other is to blame. The systems therapist hears both stories and concludes that each is half of a circle. In circular causality each person's behaviour is at once a cause and an effect of the others' behaviour, so the question "who started it?" has no useful answer (Figure 7.1). The more she nags, the more he withdraws; the more he withdraws, the more she nags. The therapist's target is the loop itself, not either person.

Why does each partner see only half the circle? The answer came from Paul Watzlawick (1921 to 2007), Janet Beavin Bavelas and Don Jackson in Pragmatics of Human Communication (1967), a book that set out five axioms of communication drawn from the Palo Alto work. Three of them are worth knowing by heart. First, "one cannot not communicate": silence, turning away and refusing to answer are all messages. Second, every communication has a content aspect (what is said) and a relationship aspect (what the message implies about the relationship between the speakers); Bateson called these the report and the command. Third, the nature of a relationship depends on the partners' punctuation of the sequence of events: each divides the endless circle at a different point, so that his own behaviour appears as a reaction and the other's as the cause. ("I withdraw because you nag." "I nag because you withdraw.") The remaining two axioms distinguish digital (verbal) from analogic (non-verbal) communication, and symmetrical interaction, in which partners mirror each other (insult for insult, boast for boast), from complementary interaction, in which one partner's behaviour fits the other's (one leads, one follows). Bateson had described both patterns as early as 1936, in his book Naven about the Iatmul, under the name schismogenesis: the way symmetrical rivalry or complementary dominance can escalate until a relationship splits.

Circular thinking transforms how the family's problem member is seen. The person whom the family brings for treatment ("our son is the problem; please fix him") is called the identified patient (IP). To a systems therapist the identified patient is the symptom-bearer: the visible sign that the system is under strain, rather than the cause of the strain. Often the symptom even serves a function for the system. A twelve-year-old's stomach aches may keep his quarrelling parents united in worry about him; if the stomach aches stopped, the parents might have to face their marriage. Treating only the boy, the systems therapist predicts, will fail, or will succeed only for the symptom to reappear in someone else.

An early and influential description of this process came from the sociologists Ezra Vogel (1930 to 2020) and Norman Bell in their 1960 paper "The emotionally disturbed child as the family scapegoat". In families they studied, the parents' unresolved tensions were displaced onto one child, who was chosen for some characteristic that already set him apart (he resembled a disliked relative, he was slower, or the child was a girl when a boy was wanted). The scapegoat carried the family's conflicts; by blaming and worrying about him, the parents could avoid confronting each other, and the family held together at his expense. Scapegoating is a classic example of a symptom that stabilises a system.

Finally, circular thinking shifts attention from content to process. The content of a family argument is what it is about (money, homework, the mother-in-law). The process is how it unfolds: who speaks first, who interrupts, who sides with whom, who goes silent, how it ends. Family systems therapists watch the process, because the same process tends to repeat whatever the content, and changing it changes many arguments at once.

Exam trap

The identified patient is the family member presented as the problem; systems therapists see that person as the symptom-bearer of a troubled system, not its cause. The scapegoat (Vogel and Bell, 1960) is a child onto whom parental conflict is displaced. Do not confuse circular causality (a systems idea about mutual cause and effect) with circular questioning (a Milan interviewing technique, Section 7.5).

Subsystems, boundaries and triangles

Three more structural words appear in every school of family therapy, so it is worth meeting them here before each school gives them its own twist. A subsystem is a smaller unit within the family that carries out particular functions: the spousal (husband and wife) subsystem, the parental subsystem (whoever raises the children, which in an Indian joint family may include a grandmother), the sibling subsystem, and each individual. A two-person unit is a dyad; a three-person unit is a triad. A boundary, in the family therapist's sense, is the set of invisible rules that defines who participates in a subsystem and how: whether the children may interrupt the parents' discussions about money, whether a grandmother may overrule the mother about bedtime, whether a teenager's diary is private. Boundaries can be too loose, too tight or about right, and Salvador Minuchin built a whole therapy around them (Section 7.4).

The third word is the triangle. Two-person relationships are unstable under stress. When a husband and wife are anxious with each other, one of them is likely to pull in a third person (a child, a parent, a friend, a lover, even a therapist) to absorb the tension: the wife complains to her son about his father, or the husband spends his evenings with his mother. This drawing-in of a third person to manage the tension between two is triangulation, and Murray Bowen made it central to his theory (Section 7.3). A coalition is an alliance of two members against a third; when it crosses generations (a mother and son allied against the father) it undermines the parental hierarchy and is a classic target of both structural and strategic therapists.

The family life cycle

Families are not only structures; they move through time. Just as an individual passes through developmental stages, a family passes through a sequence of stages, each of which demands that it reorganise. The sociologist Evelyn Duvall described an eight-stage family life cycle in 1957, but the version family therapists use comes from two Bowen-trained therapists in New York, Betty Carter (1929 to 2012) and Monica McGoldrick (born 1943), in their book The Family Life Cycle (1980) and its later editions, The Changing Family Life Cycle (1989) and The Expanded Family Life Cycle (1999). Carter and McGoldrick described six stages of the middle-class American family life cycle, each with a key emotional task, summarised in Table 7.1. Their central claim is that symptoms cluster at the transitions between stages, when the old rules no longer fit and the new ones have not yet been worked out. A young adult develops panic attacks in the month before leaving home; a mother becomes depressed when her last child leaves; a man starts drinking in the year after he retires.

Table 7.1 Carter and McGoldrick's family life cycle and the key emotional task of each stage (stage names as in the later editions of their book).
StageKey emotional taskTypical changes required
1 Leaving home: single young adultsAccepting emotional and financial responsibility for oneselfSeparating from the family of origin; work; close peer relationships
2 Joining of families through marriage: the new coupleCommitment to a new systemForming the marital system; realigning ties with both extended families and friends
3 Families with young childrenAccepting new members into the systemMaking room for children; sharing child care, money and housework; grandparent roles
4 Families with adolescentsMaking boundaries more flexible for children's independence and grandparents' frailtyLetting adolescents move in and out; refocusing on mid-life marriage and careers
5 Launching children and moving onAccepting many exits from and entries into the familyRenegotiating the couple as a dyad; adult-to-adult relations with grown children; in-laws and grandchildren; dealing with the death of parents
6 Families in later lifeAccepting the shift of generational rolesCoping with physical decline, retirement, loss of spouse and peers; making room for the wisdom of the elderly

Carter and McGoldrick added a useful distinction between two kinds of stress. Horizontal stressors move forward through time: the predictable transitions of the life cycle and unpredictable events such as an accident, a job loss or an early death. Vertical stressors come down the generations: patterns of relating, family secrets, legacies, expectations and anxieties that are passed from grandparents to parents to children. When a horizontal stressor (a daughter's marriage) lands on a family already loaded with a vertical one (three generations in which daughters who marry are cut off from their mothers), the strain can be enormous. Jay Haley, whose strategic therapy you will meet in Section 7.5, organised much of his work around the same idea: in Uncommon Therapy (1973) he presented the hypnotherapist Milton Erickson's cases as interventions at stuck points in the family life cycle, and in Leaving Home (1980) he focused on the young adult who cannot launch.

Indian context

Carter and McGoldrick's stages describe the middle-class American nuclear family, and they need translation in India. In many Indian families "leaving home" never happens in the American sense; a son may live with his parents all his life, and the key task of young adulthood is to take on adult responsibility within the family rather than outside it. "The joining of families through marriage" is literal: in an arranged marriage two families are joined, and the new couple often lives inside the husband's parental household, so the first task is not forming a couple alone but fitting a daughter-in-law into an existing system with its own hierarchy. Several Indian family therapists have proposed modified stages that give weight to the joint family, to the mother-in-law and daughter-in-law relationship and to the care of elderly parents by grown sons. The principle survives translation: symptoms cluster at transitions, and the therapist's first question is "what change is this family going through right now?"

Clinical link

A therapist who does not understand a family's culture will misread it. Closeness that looks like intrusion by one culture's standards (a mother who still feeds her adult son, a family that makes every decision together, a daughter-in-law who defers to her mother-in-law) may be ordinary, supportive and expected in another. Poor awareness of a family's culture and customary practices leads to wrong assessments and misinterpretations: normal behaviour is labelled pathological, the family feels judged, and the therapeutic alliance suffers. This is why Monica McGoldrick and her colleagues edited Ethnicity and Family Therapy (1982) and why cultural humility is now part of every family therapy training.

7.2 Families and Schizophrenia: From Blame to Expressed EmotionHigh yield · 8 Qs

The first serious family research asked the most painful question a family can be asked: did you cause your child's illness? Between the late 1940s and the early 1960s several brilliant clinicians answered, in effect, yes. Their theories have not survived as accounts of what causes schizophrenia, and they did real harm to a generation of parents who were told, or who quietly assumed, that their mistakes had produced a devastating disorder. Yet the same line of research, once it changed its question from "what causes schizophrenia?" to "what makes a person with schizophrenia relapse?", produced one of the best-replicated findings in clinical psychology and one of its most useful treatments. This section tells that story from blame to help. (The disorder itself, its symptoms and its biological causes, is covered in the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

The schizophrenogenic mother

Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (1889 to 1957) was a German-born psychoanalyst who worked at Chestnut Lodge, a private hospital near Washington, and who was famous for treating patients with schizophrenia by intensive psychotherapy at a time when most psychiatrists thought them unreachable. (One of her patients, Joanne Greenberg, later wrote a novel about her treatment, I Never Promised You a Rose Garden.) In a 1948 paper Fromm-Reichmann wrote that the schizophrenic patient was "painfully distrustful and resentful of other people, due to the severe early warp and rejection he encountered in important people of his infancy and childhood, as a rule, mainly in a schizophrenogenic mother." The phrase schizophrenogenic mother (literally, the schizophrenia-producing mother) came to mean a mother who was cold, dominating, rejecting and at the same time overprotective, insecure and anxious.

The idea spread rapidly, because it fitted the psychoanalytic assumption that severe disorders begin in the earliest relationships. It collapsed because no one could ever show that mothers of people with schizophrenia were different, in these ways, from other mothers before their children became ill. Any differences that were observed could equally be effects of living with a severely ill child rather than causes. Meanwhile twin and adoption studies were building the case for a strong genetic contribution. Today the schizophrenogenic mother is regarded as discredited, and the episode is taught as a warning: a theory that blames families without evidence adds guilt to grief. A parallel theory blamed the "refrigerator mother" for autism, an idea associated with Leo Kanner's early descriptions of the parents of autistic children and popularised by Bruno Bettelheim's book The Empty Fortress (1967); it too has been abandoned.

Bateson's double bind

Bateson's group approached the same question from communication theory rather than psychoanalysis. Their 1956 paper defined a double bind as a situation with the following ingredients:

  1. Two or more persons, one of whom is the "victim" and another (usually a parent, often the mother) on whom the victim depends.
  2. Repeated experience, so that the double bind becomes a habitual expectation rather than a single traumatic event.
  3. A primary negative injunction, such as "Do not do that, or I will punish you."
  4. A secondary injunction that conflicts with the first at a more abstract level, usually conveyed non-verbally by posture, tone or gesture ("Do not see this as punishment"; "Do not question my love").
  5. A tertiary negative injunction that prohibits the victim from escaping the field: he cannot leave the relationship, and he cannot comment on the contradiction.
  6. Eventually the complete set of ingredients is no longer needed; almost any part of the pattern is enough to trigger panic or rage in the victim.

Apply this to the opening story. The mother's stiffening body says "Do not come close"; her words say "You should come close, and if you do not, you do not love me"; her final remark says "Do not notice the contradiction; the problem is your feelings". The son depends on her, he is in hospital, and he cannot say "Mother, you stiffened when I hugged you." Bateson's group used the episode as an illustration of the symptomatic double bind, the double bind as a supposed cause of schizophrenic symptoms, and suggested that a child raised on such messages would learn never to trust the literal meaning of any message and to speak in the guarded, metaphorical way that looked like schizophrenic thought disorder. The theory drew on the philosophers Bertrand Russell and Alfred North Whitehead's theory of logical types: a message about a message (a metacommunication) belongs to a higher logical level than the message itself, and confusion arises when the two levels contradict each other.

How did the theory fare? As a cause of schizophrenia, badly. Research teams could not reliably identify double binds, could not show that they were more common in families of people with schizophrenia than in other families, and could not show that they came before rather than after the illness. Double binds, it turned out, occur in all families, and in classrooms and offices too ("Be spontaneous!" is a double bind). But the concept was enormously fertile for therapy. It taught therapists to listen for the relationship message underneath the content, it led directly to the idea that therapists could use paradox to break such binds (the "therapeutic double bind" of the strategic therapists, Section 7.5), and the Palo Alto circle that produced it went on to shape the Mental Research Institute (founded by Don Jackson in 1959), the strategic school and the whole communications approach to family therapy.

Exam trap

"One parent conveys two conflicting messages to the child at the same time" is double bind communication, proposed by Gregory Bateson and colleagues (Don Jackson, Jay Haley, John Weakland) in 1956 as an early family-communication theory of schizophrenia (not of phobias, depression or autism). The bait options are deliberately vague ("faulty communication") or belong elsewhere ("social skills deficits", "conflict resolution"). The mother-and-son hospital story illustrates the symptomatic double bind, not a "cybernetic system" or "homeostatic system" (general systems ideas, Section 7.1) or an "incongruent family dyad" (incongruence is Satir's and Rogers's word, Section 7.6). First-rank symptoms are Kurt Schneider's, not Bateson's; dementia praecox is Emil Kraepelin's; Aaron Beck is cognitive therapy (Chapter 5). An option printed as "Wayne" in such questions is almost certainly Lyman Wynne, the researcher of pseudomutuality (below), who has nothing to do with first-rank symptoms either.

Marital schism, marital skew and pseudomutuality

At Yale, the psychiatrist Theodore Lidz (1910 to 2001) and his colleagues studied the families of hospitalised young people with schizophrenia in great depth, and in 1957 (Lidz, Cornelison, Fleck and Terry) described two kinds of disturbed marriage. In marital schism the parents are in chronic open conflict, each preoccupied with his or her own problems, each undermining the worth of the other and competing for the children's sympathy and loyalty. The child is caught: pleasing one parent means betraying the other, and a father who despises the mother may fear that the child will turn out like her. In marital skew there is little open conflict, because one parent's serious disturbance dominates the home while the other parent passively accepts it and pretends that everything is normal. The children are left to make sense of a reality that the family officially denies.

At NIMH, Lyman Wynne (1923 to 2007) and his colleagues (Wynne, Ryckoff, Day and Hirsch, 1958) described pseudomutuality: a family facade of harmony, closeness and agreement that hides emotional distance and, more importantly, leaves no room for any member to develop a separate identity. Divergence is felt as a threat to the whole family, so it is never allowed to surface. Wynne pictured the boundary of such a family as a rubber fence: it stretches to include whatever fits the family's picture of itself and snaps back to exclude whatever does not, so the child can neither leave nor be fully known. Its mirror image, pseudohostility, is a surface of constant bickering that hides the same underlying fusion. Wynne later worked with the psychologist Margaret Thaler Singer (1921 to 2003) on communication deviance, a measurable tendency of parents to communicate in vague, fragmented or confusing ways, which proved one of the more durable findings of this era.

Table 7.2 brings the early family theories of schizophrenia together.

Table 7.2 Early family theories of schizophrenia.
ConceptWho and whenCore ideaStatus today
Schizophrenogenic motherFrieda Fromm-Reichmann, 1948A cold, dominating, rejecting yet overprotective mother causes schizophreniaDiscredited
Double bindBateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland, 1956Repeated contradictory messages at two levels, with no escape and no comment allowedNot a cause; seminal for communication and strategic therapies
Marital schism and marital skewTheodore Lidz and colleagues, 1957Open warring parents who compete for the child; or one dominant disturbed parent and a passive partner who pretends all is wellNot a cause; descriptive
PseudomutualityLyman Wynne and colleagues, 1958A false front of harmony that hides distance and forbids a separate identity (the rubber fence)Not a cause; led to communication deviance research
Expressed emotionGeorge Brown and colleagues, 1950s to 1972; Vaughn and Leff, 1976Critical, hostile or over-involved relatives raise the risk of relapseRobust predictor of relapse; basis of family psychoeducation

Why did all these causal theories fail? Partly because they were built on small numbers of families seen after the illness had begun, with no comparison groups and no way of separating cause from effect. Partly because they ignored genetics. And the most convincing modern evidence suggests that family environment matters in a different way: in the Finnish adoptive family study led by Pekka Tienari, children who were adopted away from mothers with schizophrenia were at raised risk of schizophrenia-spectrum disorders mainly when they were raised in adoptive families rated as disturbed. Genetic vulnerability and family environment interacted; neither alone told the story. That conclusion fits the diathesis-stress model taught in Chapter 1, and it prepares the ground for the research that did survive.

Expressed emotion

The research that survived began in London, with a simple observation by the sociologist George Brown (1930 to 2026). In 1959 he followed men with schizophrenia who had been discharged from hospital and found, contrary to what anyone expected, that those who went home to live with their parents or wives relapsed more often than those who went to live in lodgings or with brothers and sisters. Something about close family life seemed to be risky. With colleagues, Brown developed a long, semi-structured interview with a key relative, the Camberwell Family Interview (CFI; Brown and Rutter, 1966), named after the district of south London where the research was done. The relative is interviewed alone, usually soon after the patient's admission, about the past few months at home; the interview is audio-recorded, and trained raters code not only what the relative says but how it is said: tone of voice, speed, emphasis.

From these ratings came the construct of expressed emotion (EE): the emotional climate of a home as revealed by the attitudes that a key relative expresses about the patient. In the definitive form of the index, developed by Brown, James Birley and John Wing (1923 to 2010) in 1972, a home is rated high in EE on the basis of three components:

  • Critical comments: statements that clearly express dislike or disapproval of something the patient does, judged by content or by tone ("He just lies in bed all day; he is plain lazy").
  • Hostility: criticism generalised from what the patient does to who the patient is, or outright rejection ("He has always been a selfish person; I wish he had never come back").
  • Emotional over-involvement (EOI): exaggerated, intrusive, self-sacrificing or overprotective concern, dramatic distress, and an inability to let the patient do anything alone ("I haven't left the house since he got ill; I sleep in his room").

The CFI also rates warmth and positive remarks, but these are not part of the high-EE index. In Christine Vaughn and Julian Leff's version, a relative is rated high in EE if he or she makes six or more critical comments in the interview, shows any hostility, or shows marked emotional over-involvement. Brown, Birley and Wing (1972) confirmed that patients returning to high-EE homes were far more likely to relapse within nine months than those returning to low-EE homes. Then came the study that made the finding famous.

Key study: Vaughn and Leff (1976)

Question. Does the expressed emotion of relatives predict relapse in schizophrenia, and can anything protect a patient living in a high-EE home? Method. Christine Vaughn and Julian Leff (1938 to 2021), working at the Institute of Psychiatry in London, used a shortened Camberwell Family Interview to rate the relatives of patients with schizophrenia admitted to hospital, and then followed the patients for nine months after discharge, recording whether they relapsed, whether they took maintenance antipsychotic medication, and how many hours a week they spent in face-to-face contact with their relatives. They also studied a group of patients with depression. Results. About half of the patients who returned to high-EE homes relapsed, compared with roughly one in eight of those in low-EE homes. Within high-EE homes two factors were protective: regular medication, and less than 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact with the high-EE relative. Patients who had neither protection did worst of all; nearly all of them relapsed (Figure 7.2). Depressed patients were also sensitive to criticism, relapsing at even lower levels of critical comments. Conclusion. The emotional climate at home predicts relapse in schizophrenia independently of medication, and its effect can be buffered by medication and by reduced contact. Criticism. The study was correlational: high EE might partly reflect a more severely ill or more difficult patient. Later work showed that the influence runs in both directions.

The finding has been replicated around the world. A meta-analysis by Ronald Butzlaff and Jill Hooley (1998), pooling 27 studies, confirmed that high EE is a robust predictor of relapse in schizophrenia, and showed something unexpected: EE predicts relapse even more strongly in mood disorders and eating disorders. Hooley's own research explained part of the mechanism. High-EE relatives, especially critical ones, tend to believe that the patient's symptoms are under the patient's control ("he could get up if he wanted to"), whereas low-EE relatives see the same behaviour as part of the illness. Criticism follows from blame. The relationship is also bidirectional: patients who are more disturbed or more irritating provoke more criticism, and criticism in turn raises stress and the risk of relapse. EE has even been measured in hospital staff, with similar effects.

Three things about EE are tested again and again. First, high EE predicts relapse, not the onset of schizophrenia, not negative symptoms and not violence. Second, the three components are critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement; warmth is rated but is not one of them. Third, because high EE is a stressor that acts through contact, one of the simplest ways to manage it, when the emotional climate cannot be changed quickly, is to reduce the hours of face-to-face contact: a day-care centre, a sheltered job, a club, separate meal times or a hostel place for part of the week.

Indian context

India has played an important part in EE research. In the 1980s Narendra Nath Wig, Julian Leff and colleagues measured EE among the relatives of patients with schizophrenia in and around Chandigarh as part of the World Health Organization's work on the outcome of schizophrenia. Indian relatives, and rural relatives in particular, showed much lower rates of high EE than British or Anglo-American relatives (Janis Jenkins and Marvin Karno's 1992 review compared Indian, Mexican-American, British and Anglo-American samples, with Indian families lowest). Where EE was high, it still predicted relapse. This may be one reason why the WHO studies found a better course of schizophrenia in developing countries. But be careful in applying the ratings: an Indian mother who feeds her ill son by hand and accompanies him everywhere may look "over-involved" by a London scoring manual and be entirely ordinary and supportive in her own culture.

Family psychoeducation

If the emotional climate at home predicts relapse, can changing it prevent relapse? From the late 1970s several groups set out to find out, and the answer was a clear yes. The resulting treatments are known together as family psychoeducation or family intervention for psychosis: structured work with the patient's relatives that combines education about the illness with training in coping, communication and problem solving. Notice the change of attitude. The family is no longer the cause to be treated but an ally to be informed and supported; the first message of every programme is that the family did not cause the illness.

Leff and his colleagues ran the first controlled trial in London (Leff, Kuipers, Berkowitz, Eberlein-Vries and Sturgeon, 1982). Patients living in high-EE homes were all kept on medication; half of the families also received education sessions at home, a fortnightly relatives' group in which low-EE relatives modelled calmer ways of coping, and family sessions aimed at lowering criticism and reducing contact. At nine months about 9 percent of the patients in the intervention group had relapsed, against about half of the controls. In California, Ian Falloon and his colleagues developed behavioural family management, a home-based programme that teaches the whole family, patient included, about schizophrenia and its treatment, and then trains communication skills (expressing positive feelings, making positive requests, expressing negative feelings constructively, active listening) and a six-step method of structured problem solving. In their 1982 trial relapse over nine months was about 6 percent with family management against about 44 percent with individual management. At Pittsburgh, Carol Anderson, David Reiss and Gerard Hogarty (1935 to 2006), in the programme described in their book Schizophrenia and the Family (1986), began with a one-day "survival skills workshop" for families, teaching them about schizophrenia, its course, its biology and its treatment, followed by regular family sessions lasting a year or more; and William McFarlane developed multiple-family groups in which several families learn together and support one another.

The programmes differ in style, but they share a set of ingredients:

  • Education about the illness, its treatment and its course, given in plain language, with the explicit message that the family is not to blame.
  • Support for regular medication and for recognising the early warning signs of relapse, with a written plan for what to do when they appear.
  • Communication training, especially in expressing negative feelings and requests without criticism or hostility.
  • Structured problem solving for the practical stresses of daily life.
  • Reducing face-to-face contact where the climate remains tense, and helping relatives look after their own health and social lives, which lowers over-involvement.
  • A long enough course (guidelines such as those of the UK's National Institute for Health and Care Excellence ask for at least ten planned sessions over three months to a year) and, where possible, the patient's own participation.

Systematic reviews, including those of the Cochrane Collaboration, have confirmed that family intervention reduces relapse and readmission and improves adherence to medication, and clinical guidelines in many countries recommend it for every family in close contact with a person with schizophrenia. The benefits fade if the intervention stops early, which is why it is usually offered as a long-term part of care (Chapter 9 places it within psychosocial rehabilitation).

Indian context

Indian psychiatry involved families long before Western family therapy did. In the 1950s Dr Vidya Sagar at the Amritsar Mental Hospital invited the relatives of patients to live in tents on the hospital grounds and share in their care, learning about the illness through group meetings; the family members became co-therapists. Because most Indian patients with severe mental illness live with their families, and because families provide most of the care in a country with very few professionals, psychoeducation for caregivers is one of the most practical interventions an Indian clinical psychologist can offer.

Exam trap

Expressed emotion means a family environment with emotionally loaded communication patterns of a critical, hostile or over-involved nature; it is not the patient's difficulty in talking about feelings, not a family full of positive comments, and not a lack of emotion. High EE predicts relapse of psychotic illness, not onset, negative symptoms or violence, and "poor treatment outcome" is too vague to be the best answer. Its three components are critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement; warmth and positive regard are not among them. Reducing contact hours is a strategy for managing high EE, not for interpersonal violence, parasuicide risk or stubbornness in children. Names to match: George Brown (the original London research), Brown, Birley and Wing (1972), Christine Vaughn and Julian Leff (1976, the 35-hour finding), the Camberwell Family Interview.

Clinical link

EE research gives you a ready-made formulation for a family visit. Listen for criticism ("he is lazy"), hostility ("he has always been like this"), and over-involvement ("I cannot leave him for a minute"). Then ask what the relative believes about the cause of the behaviour; criticism usually rests on the belief that the patient could control it. Explaining negative symptoms as part of the illness, not laziness, is often the single most effective thing you can do for a critical relative.

Scan Your Brain

  4. The family member brought for treatment as "the problem" is the patient.
  5. The principle that the same outcome can be reached from different starting points is .

7.3 Bowen's Family Systems TheoryMedium yield · 2 Qs

Imagine two family therapists at work in the 1970s. In one room an energetic man with an Argentinian accent is on his feet, moving a mother's chair away from her daughter, telling a father to take charge, joking with a teenager one minute and challenging her the next. You will meet him in Section 7.4. In the other room a quiet man from Tennessee sits very still. He asks a wife, in a level voice, "What is your theory about why your husband goes silent when you raise the subject of his mother?" When the husband begins to answer his wife angrily, the therapist turns to him and asks him to speak to the therapist instead. Nobody cries, nobody shouts, and the session looks almost boring. Yet the quiet man believed that what he was doing went deeper than anything happening next door, because he was not trying to rearrange the family in front of him. He was trying to help each person become, slowly, more of a self. His name was Murray Bowen, and of all the founders of family therapy he was the one most interested in theory. This section explains that theory, the calm style of therapy that grew from it, and its most famous tool, the genogram.

Murray Bowen and the family as an emotional unit

Murray Bowen (1913 to 1990) was born in the small town of Waverly, Tennessee, took his medical degree at the University of Tennessee in 1937 and served as an army doctor during the Second World War, where his interest moved from surgery to psychiatry. From 1946 to 1954 he trained and worked at the Menninger Clinic in Topeka, Kansas, then the leading psychoanalytic hospital in the United States, and there he became fascinated by the intense, symbiotic attachment between some mothers and their children with schizophrenia. In 1954 he moved to the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH) near Washington, and set up one of the strangest research projects in the history of psychiatry. Whole families were admitted to a research ward and lived there for months at a time alongside their young adult son or daughter with schizophrenia: at first mothers with their children, later fathers and brothers and sisters as well. Bowen and his staff could watch, day and night, what no clinician seeing a patient for fifty minutes a week could ever see.

What they saw changed Bowen's thinking. The intensity he had noticed between mother and child was not a two-person affair; the father was part of it, usually by keeping his distance, and the whole family behaved like a single emotional organism. When one member became anxious, the anxiety passed through the others like a current. Bowen first called this stuck-together quality the undifferentiated family ego mass, a phrase he later dropped. He also noticed that the same processes, in milder form, could be seen in ordinary families, including his own. In 1959 he moved to Georgetown University in Washington, where he worked for the rest of his life and where his Georgetown Family Center later became the Bowen Center for the Study of the Family. His two main books are Family Therapy in Clinical Practice (1978), a collection of his papers, and Family Evaluation (1988), written with his colleague Michael Kerr.

One episode shows what kind of man he was. In 1967 the psychologist James Framo (1922 to 2001) organised a national conference in Philadelphia to bring family researchers and family therapists together. Bowen was expected to present an update on his NIMH research. Instead he stood up and described, in detail, his own efforts over several years to change his position in his own family of origin back in Tennessee: the visits home, the letters he wrote to stir up old triangles, the way he stayed calm when his relatives reacted. The audience was stunned. A senior psychiatrist had made his own family the case study, and the message was clear: a therapist cannot help a family become less fused than he is himself. When Framo published the conference papers in 1972, Bowen's paper appeared under the name "Anonymous". From then on, working on one's own family became the heart of Bowenian training.

Bowen built his theory on a few assumptions. Every family is an emotional system, and the emotional system, which humans share with other animals, runs much of our behaviour automatically. Against it stands the intellectual system, the capacity to think, reason and choose. Each family also balances two life forces, a pull towards togetherness (closeness, approval, being one of us) and a pull towards individuality (being a separate person directed by one's own goals). When chronic anxiety, the sustained, background tension of a family under strain, rises, togetherness takes over, people react rather than think, and the family's automatic emotional patterns come to the surface. Out of these assumptions Bowen developed eight interlocking concepts, which the rest of this section takes one at a time. The first six had been set out by the mid-1960s (in a 1966 paper); emotional cutoff and societal regression were added in 1975. Because the theory traces how these patterns pass from grandparents to parents to children, Bowen's approach is also called multigenerational, intergenerational or transgenerational family therapy, and Bowenians often describe their work as family-of-origin therapy.

Exam trap

Bowen's family systems theory is not the same as Ludwig von Bertalanffy's general systems theory (Section 7.1). Bowen insisted that his theory grew out of his own observations of families and from biology, not from von Bertalanffy, and the two terms are a favourite pair of distractors. When an option says "transgenerational", "intergenerational" or "multigenerational" family therapy, think first of Bowen (Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy's contextual therapy, Section 7.6, is the other transgenerational school). The "undifferentiated family ego mass" is Bowen's early term; "pseudomutuality" and the "rubber fence" are Lyman Wynne's (Section 7.2).

Differentiation of self

The cornerstone of Bowen's theory is differentiation of self: the degree to which a person can separate thinking from feeling, and can remain a distinct self while staying emotionally connected to the people who matter. The definition has two halves, and you need both. The first half is intrapsychic, inside the person: can she notice her feelings, and yet choose to be guided by considered thought rather than be swept along by them? The second half is interpersonal, between people: can she hold her own views in a close relationship, without either giving them up to keep the peace or attacking and running away to protect them? The opposite of differentiation is fusion, in which thinking and feeling are merged inside the person and selves are merged between people, so that one person's mood instantly becomes another's.

A worked example makes the idea concrete. Meera, twenty-seven, is a software engineer in Bengaluru. Every Sunday her mother phones from Mysuru, and every Sunday the call ends in the same place: why is Meera not yet married, when all her cousins are? Consider three ways Meera might respond.

  • She gives in. She agrees to meet the prospective grooms her mother has lined up, though she has no wish to marry this year, because she cannot bear her mother's disappointment. Her decision is driven by her mother's feelings, not by her own thinking.
  • She explodes. She shouts that her mother is backward and controlling, hangs up, and stops taking the calls. This looks like independence, but it is just as driven by emotion as giving in; the mother's anxiety still decides what Meera does, only in reverse.
  • She stays in contact and states her position calmly: "I know you worry about me, Amma. I have thought about it, and I am not ready to marry this year. I would still like to talk every Sunday." When her mother cries, Meera does not shout and does not give in; she listens, says she understands, and holds her ground.

Only the third response is differentiated. Notice that differentiation is not about the content of the decision (Meera could equally have decided, after real thought, that she did want to marry) but about how the decision is made and how she stays in the relationship while making it. A calm statement of one's own beliefs and intentions, made without attacking or demanding that others change, is what Bowen called taking an I-position.

Bowen distinguished two kinds of self. The solid self is made of beliefs and principles that a person has arrived at by careful thought; it is not negotiable in relationships, and it does not change because someone is pleased or annoyed. The pseudo-self is made of positions borrowed from others or adopted under emotional pressure, to win approval or to oppose someone; it can be traded away in a relationship and it shifts with the emotional weather. The rebel who reflexively opposes his parents is, on this view, as poorly differentiated as the son who obeys them in everything; both are reacting, not choosing. Bowen also separated a person's basic level of differentiation, which is fairly fixed by the end of adolescence and changes only through long, deliberate effort, from the functional level, which rises and falls with anxiety and with relationships. In a marriage one partner may appear highly competent and the other helpless, because one has "borrowed" self from the other: the overfunctioning wife who runs everything and the underfunctioning husband who cannot decide anything are a reciprocal pair, and each keeps the other in place.

To convey how differentiation varies across people, Bowen described a theoretical scale of differentiation running from 0 to 100, divided into four broad quarters. It was a way of thinking, not a test with norms, and in later years he preferred describing people's functioning to giving them numbers. At the bottom of the scale, people live almost entirely in a world of feelings; they cannot distinguish feeling from fact, they spend their energy seeking love and approval or reacting against its absence, and under stress they are prone to severe and lasting problems. In the lower middle range people can think, but their thinking is easily captured by the emotional system; they are highly sensitive to approval, they say "I feel" when they mean "I think", and much of their self is pseudo-self. Higher up the scale people can hold a thought-out position under moderate stress, can be close without being fused and can disagree without cutting off. Bowen believed that people functioning above about 60 are a small minority of any society, and that the top of the scale is a theoretical ideal. Later researchers built questionnaires to measure the idea; the best known, the Differentiation of Self Inventory of Elizabeth Skowron and Myrna Friedlander (1998), has four subscales: emotional reactivity, I-position, emotional cutoff and fusion with others. Studies with it have generally found that lower differentiation goes with more anxiety, more psychological symptoms and more marital distress, which is broadly what Bowen predicted.

Indian context

Is differentiation just Western individualism in disguise? Many critics have asked this, and the question matters in India. The psychoanalyst Alan Roland, comparing patients in India, Japan and the United States in In Search of Self in India and Japan (1988), described the Indian "familial self": a self experienced through close, hierarchical family ties rather than in separation from them. Bowen himself insisted that differentiation is not the same as independence or distance; the person who moves far away and stops calling is, in his terms, poorly differentiated. Read this way, the concept travels well. An Indian son who lives with his parents all his life can be highly differentiated if he can disagree with his father calmly, keep his own counsel and still be a warm and dutiful son; a young woman who leaves for Canada after a bitter quarrel and never speaks to her family again is not.

Exam trap

Differentiation of self (Bowen) means separating thinking from feeling and keeping a self within close relationships. It does not mean distance, independence or rebellion; the emotionally cut-off person is poorly differentiated. Do not confuse it with Margaret Mahler's separation-individuation (the infant's psychological birth, Section 3.4), with Minuchin's enmeshment (a property of family boundaries, Section 7.4) or with Wynne's pseudomutuality (Section 7.2). Fusion, solid self, pseudo-self and the I-position are all Bowen's words.

Triangles and the nuclear family emotional process

Section 7.1 introduced triangulation: the drawing in of a third person to absorb the tension between two. For Bowen this was not an occasional event but a law of emotional systems. A two-person relationship is stable only while it is calm. As anxiety rises, the more uncomfortable partner pulls in a third person, and the tension spreads across three relationships instead of being trapped in one. The triangle is therefore the smallest stable relationship system, and Bowen thought of families as networks of interlocking triangles: when one triangle can no longer hold the tension, it spills into the next, drawing in grandparents, in-laws, teachers, friends and, very often, the therapist.

Bowen added a subtle point about positions in a triangle. In calm periods there are two comfortable insiders and one uncomfortable outsider, and the outsider works to get closer to one of the others. When tension is high, the positions reverse: the outside position becomes the desirable one, and each person tries to get out and leave the other two to fight. Consider Arjun and Meera, now married with a ten-year-old son, Kabir. When Arjun starts staying late at the office, Meera, lonely and anxious, begins to confide her worries to Kabir. Kabir, flattered and burdened, becomes his mother's companion; Arjun, already uncomfortable, becomes the outsider. When Kabir starts having stomach aches on school mornings, both parents turn their worry towards him, and for a while they argue much less. The triangle has stabilised the marriage, at Kabir's expense. If Meera's mother now phones daily to criticise Arjun's long hours, a second, interlocking triangle (Meera, her mother, Arjun) begins to carry part of the load.

Triangles are not bad in themselves; every family has them, and once formed they persist. The problem is a triangle that becomes rigid, so that the same third person, usually a child, always carries the tension. The way out is detriangulation: a person caught in a triangle stays in emotional contact with both others while refusing to take sides, to carry messages or to become the go-between. If Kabir's grandmother, instead of passing on Meera's complaints to Arjun, says "That sounds like something you two need to talk about", she has detriangled herself.

Bowen's third concept describes how the anxiety of a single generation is expressed within the household. He originally called it the nuclear family emotional system; the Bowen Center now speaks of the nuclear family emotional process. Bowen observed that people tend to marry partners at about their own level of differentiation, so two poorly differentiated people form a highly fused couple, and the fusion has to go somewhere. It is absorbed in four basic patterns:

  1. Marital conflict. Each spouse externalises anxiety into the marriage; they blame each other, try to control each other and resist being controlled.
  2. Dysfunction in one spouse. One spouse pressures and the other yields; the yielding, underfunctioning partner absorbs the anxiety and may develop a physical illness, depression or a drinking problem.
  3. Impairment of one or more children. The parents focus their anxiety on a child, who becomes the symptom-bearer (the family projection process, explained next).
  4. Emotional distance. Partners distance from each other to lower the intensity, at the risk of becoming isolated; distance usually accompanies the other three patterns.

The more undifferentiated the couple and the higher the anxiety, the more intense the patterns. One of Bowen's students, Thomas Fogarty, described a familiar version of the distance pattern: the pursuer, who seeks closeness and talk when anxious, and the distancer, who seeks space, each driving the other further into his or her own move. Fogarty's advice to pursuers was blunt: never pursue a distancer. Stop chasing, attend to your own life, and the distancer, no longer fleeing, often comes closer.

Exam trap

Keep the triangles apart. Bowen's triangle is the basic unit of any emotional system, in which a third person is drawn in to lower anxiety between two. Minuchin's triangulation is narrower: a rigid pattern in which each parent demands that the child side with him or her against the other (Section 7.4). Jay Haley's perverse triangle is a cross-generational coalition that is denied (Section 7.5). Stephen Karpman's drama triangle of persecutor, rescuer and victim comes from transactional analysis (Section 3.9). The four patterns of the nuclear family emotional process are marital conflict, dysfunction in a spouse, impairment of a child and emotional distance.

Projection, transmission and cutoff across generations

The family projection process is the main route by which parents pass their own lack of differentiation to a child. The Bowen Center describes it in three steps: the parent focuses on a child out of fear that something is wrong with the child; the parent interprets the child's behaviour as confirming the fear; and the parent treats the child as if something really is wrong. The child, who is genuinely attentive to the parent's anxiety, gradually develops the problem that was feared. The child chosen is usually the one most emotionally attached to the parents: it may be the eldest son, the only daughter, a child born at a time of family crisis, or a child with a real illness. In Arjun and Meera's family it is Kabir. Meera's fear that he is fragile leads her to check on him constantly, she reads every quiet afternoon as a sign of trouble, and she keeps him home at the first stomach ache; Kabir learns that his body is the thing everyone watches. What the child inherits is less a specific symptom than a set of sensitivities: a heightened need for attention and approval, difficulty with expectations, a tendency to blame himself or others, and a feeling of being responsible for his parents' happiness. Siblings who are less caught in the projection grow up more mature, which is why Bowen expected brothers and sisters raised in the same home to differ in differentiation.

Extend the projection process across many generations and you have the multigenerational transmission process. In each generation, the child who is most involved in the projection emerges slightly less differentiated than his parents, while a sibling who is less involved emerges slightly more differentiated. Each of these children then marries someone at about the same level and repeats the process with the next generation. Over several generations, Bowen argued, one line of descent moves towards greater differentiation and stability, and another towards lower differentiation, more anxiety and more severe problems. In his early NIMH years Bowen even suggested that schizophrenia might be the product of several generations of declining differentiation, an idea no one now accepts as a cause of the disorder (Section 7.2 told what became of such theories). But the broader point survives: patterns of relating, and not only genes, are handed down, and you cannot understand the anxiety in a nuclear family without looking at the generations above it. That is why Bowen therapists always want to know about the grandparents.

How do people cope with unresolved fusion with their parents? Very often, Bowen saw, by cutting themselves off. Emotional cutoff is the reduction or elimination of emotional contact with one's family of origin in order to manage unresolved attachment. It can be physical, as when a son moves to another city or country and rarely visits, or purely emotional, as when a daughter lives in the same house but keeps every conversation polite, brief and away from anything that matters. Cutoff feels like freedom, but Bowen's central claim is that it solves nothing: the unresolved attachment travels with the person and resurfaces in new relationships, which tend to become intense and fused, because the cut-off person asks a spouse or a child to supply everything the original family no longer can. The more a family relies on cutoff, the more vulnerable its members are to problems. Picture a man who left his parents' home in Kanpur after a quarrel about his marriage and has not spoken to his father in seven years. He tells you he is "completely free of all that". Then you notice how desperately he needs his wife never to disagree with him.

The seventh concept, sibling position, Bowen borrowed from the Austrian-born psychologist Walter Toman (1920 to 2003), whose book Family Constellation (1961) proposed that a person's position among brothers and sisters shapes lasting characteristics: eldest children tend to take responsibility and lead, youngest children tend to follow and to expect others to take charge, and so on for the other positions, with differences according to the sex of the siblings. Toman's duplication theorem held that new relationships, including marriage, go most smoothly when they repeat the relationships of childhood. An elder brother of a younger sister who marries a younger sister of an elder brother fits his early pattern exactly; two eldest children who marry may compete over who is in charge (a rank conflict); a brother who grew up only with brothers, married to a sister who grew up only with sisters, may find the other sex unfamiliar (a sex conflict). Bowen used Toman's work, but he cared less about the literal birth order than about functional position. A first-born son who is the target of his parents' projection may function more like a helpless youngest child, while a second daughter steps into the role of the responsible eldest. Knowing the sibling positions of both spouses helps a Bowenian therapist predict how they will share power and which child is most likely to be drawn into the projection process.

The eighth concept, societal regression (the Bowen Center calls it the societal emotional process), extends the theory from families to whole societies. Bowen argued that a society under sustained chronic anxiety, from pressures such as rapid population growth, dwindling natural resources, economic strain or social upheaval, behaves like an anxious family: togetherness overwhelms individuality, decisions are made to relieve the anxiety of the moment rather than on principle, quick fixes replace long-term thinking, and people emphasise their rights more than their responsibilities. Bowen pointed to the way American society handled juvenile delinquency in the 1960s and 1970s as an example. Societies, like families, pass through progressive and regressive periods. Table 7.3 brings the eight concepts together.

Table 7.3 Murray Bowen's eight interlocking concepts.
ConceptCore ideaExample
Differentiation of selfSeparating thinking from feeling; holding a self within close relationshipsMeera states her view calmly and keeps calling her mother
TrianglesA third person is drawn in to absorb tension between two; the smallest stable systemMeera confides in Kabir about Arjun
Nuclear family emotional processA couple's fusion shows as marital conflict, spouse dysfunction, child impairment or distanceArjun withdraws to work; Meera turns to the child
Family projection processParents' anxiety is focused on one child, who develops the feared problemKabir's stomach aches become the family's focus
Multigenerational transmission processSmall differences in differentiation accumulate over generationsThree generations of distant fathers and anxious sons
Emotional cutoffUnresolved attachment is managed by physical or emotional distanceA son who has not spoken to his father for seven years
Sibling positionFunctional position among siblings shapes roles (from Walter Toman)Two eldest children married to each other compete to lead
Societal regression (societal emotional process)Chronically anxious societies regress, as anxious families doQuick-fix policies made to calm public anxiety
Exam trap

A question asking what Bowen family systems therapy emphasises wants differentiation of self, triangulation and multigenerational transmission. The bait options belong elsewhere: narrative externalisation is Michael White and David Epston's (Section 6.8), strategic paradox and prescribing the symptom belong to Haley and the Milan and MRI teams (Section 7.5), and exception questions belong to solution-focused brief therapy (Section 6.8). Sibling position comes from Walter Toman, not from Alfred Adler, although Adler also wrote about birth order (Section 3.8). Emotional cutoff is a sign of low differentiation, not high.

Therapy as coaching: the calm, detriangled therapist

Bowen's therapy follows directly from his theory, and it has two goals: to lower the anxiety in the system enough for symptoms to settle, and, more ambitiously, to raise each person's basic level of differentiation. Symptom relief alone he regarded as a modest achievement, because a family that is just as fused as before will produce a new symptom at the next crisis.

Who comes to therapy? Bowen rarely saw the symptomatic child. He preferred to work with the two parents, on the view that the child's problem was part of their emotional process, and very often with just one person, the most motivated and best differentiated member of the family. This sounds odd for a family therapist, but it follows from systems thinking: if one person in a triangle changes his own part, calmly and persistently, the other two must change in response. Family therapy, for Bowen, was defined by how the therapist thinks about the problem, not by how many people are in the room.

The therapist's own position is the most important tool. A family in distress will try, without knowing it, to pull the therapist into its triangles: a wife wants the therapist to agree that her husband is cold, a husband wants the therapist to see that his wife is unreasonable. Bowen's answer was to stay emotionally in contact with both while remaining outside their emotional field: calm, curious, neutral and low-key. He believed that if a couple can remain in contact with a third person who is not reacting to them, the fusion between them gradually loosens. To keep the emotional temperature down he usually had the spouses talk to him, not to each other; while one spoke, the other listened, and each heard the partner's thinking without having to defend against it. He asked process questions, questions about how the pattern works that invite thought rather than feeling: "What happens between you two when Kabir has a stomach ache?" "Who in your family worried most, when you were a child?" "What do you think you contribute to the distance?" He saw himself as a coach, in his own word: like a sports coach on the touchline, he helped clients understand their families and plan their own moves, but it was the client, not the coach, who went onto the field.

Several methods grew out of this stance. The therapist models and encourages the I-position. Clients are helped to design relationship experiments, deliberate changes in their own part of a pattern (a pursuer stops pursuing, a peacemaker stops carrying messages) and to observe what the system does in reply. Some Bowenians use displacement stories, films or stories about other families, so that a family can see its own process with less defensiveness. Above all, clients are coached to go home again: to visit the family of origin, to reopen contact with relatives they have cut off, to build a person-to-person relationship with each parent (talking one to one about each other's lives, rather than about third people), and to detriangle from old family triangles while staying in contact. Bowen warned that a differentiating move provokes a predictable reaction from the family: first "you are wrong", then "change back", and then "if you do not, here are the consequences". The person who expects this reaction, stays calm through it and neither attacks nor gives in usually finds that the family settles at a new level.

Return to Arjun, Meera and Kabir. A Bowenian therapist sees the parents, not Kabir. She draws a genogram with them (the next subsection) and finds that Meera's father was also a distant man who worked late, and that Meera, as an eldest daughter, was her own anxious mother's confidante. She asks Meera what she notices about the similarity, and asks Arjun what he does when Meera is anxious. Over some months she coaches Meera to take her worries about the marriage to Arjun rather than to Kabir, and to visit her mother and talk about her own childhood instead of about Arjun's faults. She coaches Arjun to stay in the room when Meera is upset rather than retreating to his laptop. Nobody works directly on Kabir's stomach aches. As his parents become a little less reactive and a little more of a couple, he is released from the triangle, and the aches fade.

Bowen's ideas were carried forward by a group of gifted students. Philip Guerin and Thomas Fogarty developed practical methods for working with triangles. Betty Carter and Monica McGoldrick, whom you met in Section 7.1, brought Bowenian thinking to the family life cycle and to the genogram. The rabbi and family therapist Edwin Friedman (1932 to 1996) applied the theory to churches and synagogues in Generation to Generation (1985), and the psychologist Harriet Lerner (born 1944) made it famous with general readers in The Dance of Anger (1985). As for evidence, Bowen theory has inspired a large correlational literature on differentiation but few controlled trials of Bowenian therapy, so its effectiveness rests more on clinical tradition than on outcome research.
