Critics have also argued that its prizing of reason over emotion and of the autonomous self reflects Western, middle-class and stereotypically masculine values, a criticism that feminist family therapists pressed hard (Section 7.7 takes up gender and culture).

Clinical link

Process questions are one of the most portable skills in this chapter. When a family meeting in an Indian clinic becomes heated, with a mother and grandmother talking over each other about a teenager, you do not need to win the argument or take a side. Slow things down, ask each person to speak to you, and ask questions about the pattern: "When Rahul refuses to eat, what does his mother do? And then what does his grandmother do? And then?" Thinking about a pattern lowers reactivity, and a calm, non-reactive clinician in the room is itself an intervention.

Exam trap

The calm, neutral coach who stays out of the family's triangles is Bowen. The active, directive stage director who joins the family and then rearranges it is Minuchin (Section 7.4). The therapist who gives directives and paradoxical tasks is Haley (Section 7.5). The playful, deliberately "crazy" therapist who uses his own spontaneity is Carl Whitaker (Section 7.6). Bowen often saw only the parents or a single family member; that is still family therapy in his sense.

The genogram: drawing and reading one

Bowen called it the family diagram. His students made it famous as the genogram: a diagram of a family across at least three generations that records who is in the family, the main facts about each person and, in its fullest form, the emotional relationships between them. Section 2.3 introduced the genogram as part of the case history and drew a simple one (Figure 2.2). Here you will meet it as a therapeutic tool, the way Bowenians use it. Its symbols were standardised by Monica McGoldrick and Randy Gerson in Genograms in Family Assessment (1985), and later editions, retitled Genograms: Assessment and Intervention, remain the standard reference.

McGoldrick and Gerson describe building a genogram in three layers: mapping the family structure, recording information about each member, and drawing in the relationships. The structural symbols are the ones you met in Section 2.3. A square is a male and a circle a female, with the current age written inside; the index person (the identified patient) has a double outline. A cross inside a symbol marks a death, usually with the year and the age at death. A horizontal line joins a married couple, with the year of marriage written on it; a dashed line marks a couple living together without marriage; one slash across the line means separation and two slashes mean divorce. Children hang from the couple's line in birth order, eldest on the left. A shaded symbol marks a condition such as depression or alcohol misuse, explained in a key, and the members of the current household may be enclosed by a dotted line.

The third layer is what makes a genogram Bowenian. Relationship lines between symbols show the quality of each important relationship: two parallel lines for a close relationship, three for a fused one, a dotted line for a distant one, a zigzag for conflict, three lines with a zigzag for a relationship that is fused and conflictual at once, and a line broken by a gap between two short bars for a cutoff. With these lines in place, triangles, cutoffs and repeating patterns literally become visible.

How do you draw one with a family? Start with the household and the presenting problem, then move up to each parent's family of origin, and keep going until you have three generations. Ask about the nodal events, the births, deaths, marriages, separations, migrations, illnesses and job losses, and get their dates. Then ask the relationship questions: Who was close to whom? Who does not speak to whom? Who did your mother turn to when she was worried? Many therapists draw the genogram on a whiteboard with the family, over the first two or three sessions, because the drawing is itself a conversation; families often discover links they had never put together. Figure 7.3 shows the genogram of a Mumbai family whose sixteen-year-old son, Rohan, has refused to go to school for most of the past year.

Reading a genogram is a skill. McGoldrick and Gerson suggest looking at the family's structure (who lives in the household, the sibling constellation), at life cycle fit (unusual ages or timing, such as a very late marriage or a child born just after a death), at patterns repeating across generations (in functioning, in relationships and in structure), at the coincidence of life events with symptoms, at relational patterns and triangles, and at balance and imbalance in the family. Apply these to Figure 7.3. Rohan stopped going to school within weeks of the death of his grandfather, Dada, in 2025, a coincidence of dates that invites questions about grief in a household where, it turns out, no one has mentioned Dada since the funeral. Rohan is fused with his mother Lata and distant from his father Suresh, and Suresh was equally distant from his own father: a pattern repeating across generations. Lata is in open conflict with her mother-in-law, who lives with the family, and has cut off her only brother, Vinod, after a quarrel over their father's property, so the person she turns to for closeness is her son. Her own father drank heavily, which may help explain why she learned to watch the men in her life anxiously. The triangle of Lata, Suresh and Rohan, interlocking with the triangle of Lata, Dadi and Suresh, now looks like the obvious place to begin, and Rohan's school refusal looks less like a problem inside one boy and more like a sign of a family under strain at a moment of loss.

Exam trap

The genogram belongs to Murray Bowen's family systems therapy, with standard symbols from McGoldrick and Gerson (1985); it is not a part of dialectical behaviour therapy (Chapter 5) or a projective test. Squares are males, circles are females, a double outline marks the index person, a cross marks a death, and relationship lines show closeness, fusion, distance, conflict and cutoff. Keep it apart from Minuchin's structural map, which shows the family's present organisation (boundaries, coalitions) rather than its history across generations (Section 7.4), and from Virginia Satir's family sculpting, in which members are physically posed to show relationships (Section 7.6).

The genogram, the ecomap and the sociogram

The genogram has two relatives that are easily confused with it, and both appeared briefly in Section 2.3. The first is the ecomap, developed in 1975 by the social worker Ann Hartman at the University of Michigan to help child welfare workers see families in their surroundings, and published in her 1978 paper "Diagrammatic assessment of family relationships". Where the genogram maps a family across time, the ecomap maps it across space: the household is drawn as a circle in the centre, and around it are circles for every system the family deals with, such as the school, the workplace, the temple, the extended family, the clinic, the tuition centre, the welfare office or the courts. Lines between the household and each circle show the quality of the connection: a heavy line for a strong, supportive link, a dashed line for a weak or tenuous one, and a hatched line for a stressful one, with arrows to show the direction in which energy, money or help flows. At a glance the ecomap shows where a family draws its support, where it is drained and how isolated it is. It is widely used in social work and community mental health for assessment and for planning which outside links to strengthen.

The second relative is the sociogram. It comes not from family therapy but from group psychology: the psychiatrist Jacob Levy Moreno, founder of psychodrama and of sociometry (the measurement of attractions and rejections within groups), introduced it in Who Shall Survive? (1934). Members of a group are asked sociometric questions such as "Whom would you choose to sit beside?" or "Whom would you most like to work with?", and their answers are drawn as circles joined by arrows. The diagram reveals who is a star (chosen by many), who is an isolate (chosen by none), which pairs choose each other, and where cliques, chains and rejections lie. Because it shows the actual lines of attraction, communication and avoidance in a group, both those people would admit to and those they would not, the sociogram captures a group's implicit and explicit interaction patterns. Section 7.11 tells Moreno's story and explains sociometry in full. Figure 7.4 sets an ecomap beside a sociogram, and Table 7.4 compares all three tools.

Table 7.4 The genogram, the ecomap and the sociogram compared.
FeatureGenogramEcomapSociogram
Who and whenBowen's family diagram; standardised by McGoldrick and Gerson, 1985Ann Hartman, 1975 (published 1978)Jacob Levy Moreno, 1934
Unit drawnA family across three or more generationsA household and the outside systems around itThe members of a group
What it showsStructure, events, illnesses and emotional relationships over timeStrong, weak and stressful links to school, work, services and kinChoices, rejections, stars, isolates, cliques: the group's interaction pattern
Home disciplineBowenian family therapy; case historySocial work; community assessmentSociometry; group work and psychodrama
Exam trap

"Which of the following captures implicit and explicit group communication and interaction patterns?" The answer is a sociogram (Moreno), because only the sociogram maps the interactions among the members of a group. A genogram maps a family across generations, an ecomap (Hartman) maps a family's links to outside systems, and a cognitive map is Edward Tolman's term for an animal's mental representation of a maze, which has nothing to do with groups or families. The key word that decides the question is "group".

7.4 Minuchin's Structural Family TherapyMedium yield · 5 Qs

Bowen asked families to think. Salvador Minuchin asked them to act. Where Bowen sat still and kept the emotional temperature low, Minuchin got up, moved chairs, interrupted, told a mother to stop answering for her son and a father to make his daughter sit down, then praised them both for doing it. Where Bowen looked back across three generations, Minuchin looked at the family in front of him, here and now, and asked one question: how is this family organised, and how does that organisation keep the problem alive? His answer, structural family therapy, became for a time the most widely practised model of family therapy in the world, and its vocabulary of boundaries, enmeshment and disengagement is now part of everyday clinical language. This section explains the ideas and the techniques, and Section 7.1's brief introduction to subsystems and boundaries is where it begins.

Salvador Minuchin: from Wiltwyck to Philadelphia

Salvador Minuchin (1921 to 2017) was born in San Salvador, a small town in the province of Entre Ríos in Argentina, into a family of Russian-Jewish immigrants. He qualified in medicine in Argentina, served as a doctor in the Israeli army during the war of 1948, and then went to New York, where he trained in child psychiatry under Nathan Ackerman, the "grandfather of family therapy" you met in Section 7.1. He returned to Israel to work with displaced Jewish children, and in 1954 came back to New York to train in psychoanalysis at the William Alanson White Institute, the centre of Harry Stack Sullivan's interpersonal psychoanalysis (Chapter 3).

The turning point came at the Wiltwyck School for Boys, a residential school in New York State for boys from the poorest neighbourhoods of New York City, most of them African American or Puerto Rican and most of them in trouble with the law. Working there as a psychiatrist in the late 1950s and early 1960s, Minuchin found that the talking therapies he had been trained in, designed for verbal, reflective, middle-class adults, did not reach these boys or their families. The families were not interested in insight; they were overwhelmed by poverty and by crises, they communicated through action more than words, and they were often organised in ways that the psychoanalytic textbooks did not describe. With colleagues including Braulio Montalvo, Minuchin began seeing whole families, built a one-way mirror so that colleagues could watch and consult, and invented concrete, active techniques: splitting the family into subgroups so that the parents could watch their children from behind the mirror, giving tasks to be done in the session, and changing who talked to whom. The project produced Families of the Slums (1967), written with Montalvo, Bernard Guerney, Bernice Rosman and Florence Schumer, which first described the two styles of family organisation that structural therapy is known for: the enmeshed family and the disengaged family.

In 1965 Minuchin became director of the Philadelphia Child Guidance Clinic, then a small clinic in a poor, largely African American neighbourhood. Over the next decade he turned it into one of the most famous family therapy centres in the world. Jay Haley, the strategic therapist you will meet in Section 7.5, joined him there in 1967, and the two learned a great deal from each other. Together they trained members of the local Black community, people with no formal qualifications in psychology, as family therapists, on the belief that they would join poor families better than middle-class professionals could. In Philadelphia Minuchin wrote Families and Family Therapy (1974), the book that set out structural theory and became one of the most widely read books in the field; with Rosman and the paediatrician Lester Baker he wrote Psychosomatic Families: Anorexia Nervosa in Context (1978); and with H. Charles Fishman he wrote Family Therapy Techniques (1981), a detailed manual of the methods. In 1981 he moved to New York and founded Family Studies, Inc., later renamed the Minuchin Center for the Family. In his later years he returned to his first concern, poor families and the social services that deal with them, in books such as Working with Families of the Poor (1998), written with his wife, the developmental psychologist Patricia Minuchin, and Jorge Colapinto. He died in Florida in 2017, aged 96.

The central idea of structural family therapy can be put in a sentence. A family's symptoms are maintained by the way the family is currently organised, so if the therapist changes the organisation, the symptom loses its function and fades. The therapist does not need to uncover the origins of the problem or give the family insight into it. He needs to see the structure, join the family, and then act, in the session, to change who relates to whom and how.

Family structure, subsystems and hierarchy

Minuchin defined family structure as the invisible set of functional demands that organises the ways in which family members interact. You cannot see a family's structure directly, any more than you can see the grammar of a language; you infer it from the patterns that repeat. If, every evening, the mother asks the son to do his homework, the son ignores her, the mother complains to the grandmother, and the grandmother tells the son he may finish it in the morning, then that sequence, repeated a hundred times, tells you who holds authority in the household, who is allied with whom and where the parent is weak. Minuchin said such patterns are maintained by two kinds of constraint. The generic constraints are the universal rules of family organisation, above all that there should be a power hierarchy in which parents and children have different levels of authority. The idiosyncratic constraints are the particular expectations of one family, built up over years of negotiation and accommodation until no one remembers where they came from ("Father is never disturbed after dinner"; "Big sister always takes the little one to school").

The family carries out its work through subsystems (Section 7.1), formed by generation, by sex, by interest or by function. Minuchin gave each of the main subsystems a job. The spouse subsystem exists to meet the couple's needs for each other; it requires complementarity and mutual accommodation, and it needs a boundary that protects it from intrusion by children, in-laws and work, because a couple who never has time or space alone stops being a couple and becomes only a pair of parents. The parental subsystem is the family's executive: it nurtures, guides and controls the children, and it may include a grandparent, an aunt or an older child as well as the parents. The sibling subsystem is a child's first peer group, where children learn to negotiate, cooperate, compete, make friends and handle rivalry. Each person belongs to several subsystems at once and plays a different role in each: the same woman is a wife, a mother, a daughter-in-law and an elder sister, and she must behave differently in each role.

For Minuchin, a well-functioning family has a clear hierarchy: the parents are in charge, they exercise their authority together, and older children have more responsibilities and privileges than younger ones. Many problems arise when the hierarchy is confused or inverted: a child who rules the household by tantrums, parents who undercut each other's decisions, a grandmother who overrules the mother in front of the children, or a child who has been elevated into a parent's confidant. Minuchin used the term parental child for a child who has been given parental authority, typically an eldest daughter in a large family or a single-parent household who looks after the younger children. This can work well when the authority is clearly delegated, limited and backed by the parent. It becomes a problem when the child carries responsibilities beyond her age, is given authority the parent does not support, or is cut off from her own childhood and peers. (Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy's related idea of parentification is explained in Section 7.6.)

Minuchin also stressed that a normal family is not a family without stress. Every family meets stress: from one member's contact with the outside world (a father loses his job), from the whole family's contact with it (a move to a new city), from developmental transitions (a child reaches adolescence, a grandparent comes to live with the family) and from particular problems (a child with a disability). A functional family responds by adjusting its structure: rules are renegotiated, boundaries loosen or tighten, roles shift. A dysfunctional family responds by holding the old structure even more rigidly, and the symptom appears at the point where the structure fails to adapt. This is the structural version of Section 7.1's lesson that symptoms cluster at life cycle transitions.

Exam trap

Structural family therapy is Salvador Minuchin's, and it focuses primarily on subsystems, boundaries (clear, diffuse, rigid) and hierarchy in the family's present organisation. The distractors each belong to another school: "multigenerational transmission of anxiety" is Bowen (Section 7.3), "cognitive distortions in family members" is cognitive therapy (Chapter 5), and "paradoxical injunctions" belong to the strategic and Milan therapists (Section 7.5). In the "who founded which school" questions: structural is Minuchin, multigenerational or intergenerational is Bowen, strategic is Jay Haley (with Cloe Madanes), and experiential is Virginia Satir and Carl Whitaker (Section 7.6).

Boundaries: clear, diffuse and rigid

Section 7.1 defined a boundary as the invisible rules that define who participates in a subsystem and how. Minuchin made the boundary his central concept. Its function is to protect the differentiation of each subsystem, so that the couple can be a couple, the parents can be parents and the children can be children, while still allowing contact and support to pass between them. Boundaries can be placed on a continuum (Table 7.5). At one end are rigid boundaries, which are overly restrictive and permit little contact between subsystems. At the other end are diffuse boundaries, which are blurred and permeable, so that members intrude freely into each other's business. In the middle lies the normal range of clear boundaries, which are firm enough to protect each subsystem's work and privacy and flexible enough to allow closeness, support and change.

A family organised around diffuse boundaries shows enmeshment. Its members are highly involved in each other's lives; they speak for each other, finish each other's sentences, know each other's every mood and treat any disagreement as disloyalty. The gain is a strong sense of belonging and mutual support. The cost is autonomy. Children in enmeshed families are slow to develop independent competence, because parents step in before they can struggle, and stress in one member reverberates instantly through all the others: when the daughter has a bad day at school, the mother cannot sleep and the father is irritable. Consider thirteen-year-old Kavya Iyer of Chennai. Her mother, Shalini, still sleeps in Kavya's room "because she has nightmares", answers every question addressed to Kavya, and checks her school bag, her phone and her diary. When Kavya is upset, Shalini is upset; when Kavya refuses to go to school, Shalini takes leave from work to stay home with her.

A family organised around rigid boundaries shows disengagement. Members function independently, go their own ways and rarely ask each other for help. The gain is autonomy; the cost is a lack of warmth, loyalty and support. In a disengaged family only a very high level of stress in one member brings a response from the others, so problems can go unnoticed for a long time: a son's depression comes to the parents' attention only when the school phones to say he has stopped attending. Kavya's father, Prakash, an engineer who leaves home at seven and returns at ten, is disengaged from the household. He knows little about his children's daily lives and is called in only as the "final authority" when Shalini cannot cope, at which point he shouts, and then withdraws again.

Notice two things about the Iyer family, because they hold for most families Minuchin described. First, enmeshment and disengagement are styles of transaction, not diagnoses. Most families have both enmeshed and disengaged subsystems, and the common pattern of an enmeshed mother and child with a disengaged, peripheral father is a classic example of both in one household. Second, only the extremes signal trouble. A mother may be closely involved with an infant, appropriately, and a father may be busy at work, appropriately; the question is whether the arrangement still fits the family's stage of life and allows each member to develop. Shalini's closeness would be ordinary with a three-year-old; with a thirteen-year-old who now refuses school, it no longer fits.

Table 7.5 Minuchin's continuum of boundaries.
BoundaryFamily styleWhat you seeGain and cost
RigidDisengagementLittle contact or interest; members go their own way; help comes only in a crisisAutonomy, at the cost of warmth, loyalty and support
ClearNormal rangeFirm but flexible rules; closeness with privacy; parents in charge togetherBelonging and autonomy, adjusted to the life stage
DiffuseEnmeshmentOver-involvement; members speak for each other; distress spreads instantlyBelonging and support, at the cost of autonomy and competence
Indian context

An Indian family in which a grandmother helps raise the children, a mother feeds her ten-year-old by hand, everyone shares a room and decisions are taken together can look "enmeshed" to a therapist trained on Western textbooks. It may be nothing of the kind. Minuchin himself insisted that a family must be judged against the norms of its own culture and its own stage of life. The structural questions that travel across cultures are not "how close are they?" but "is the hierarchy clear, so that the children know who is in charge?", "does the couple have any space of its own?" and "does the arrangement allow each child to grow in competence for his or her age?" In a joint family the most common structural problem is not closeness as such but a confused hierarchy, as when a mother-in-law and a daughter-in-law give the children contradictory instructions and the children learn to play one against the other. As Section 7.1 warned, a therapist who misreads the culture will make wrong assessments and lose the family.

Exam trap

Enmeshment (Minuchin) means diffuse boundaries with over-involvement, intrusion and loss of individual autonomy. Disengagement means rigid boundaries with little emotional contact. Clear boundaries are the healthy middle, so "healthy reciprocal closeness" describes clear boundaries, not enmeshment. "Pathological scapegoating of a single member" is Vogel and Bell's scapegoat (Section 7.1), and "a complete lack of communication" is not the definition of either style. Do not mix up the authors of similar-sounding ideas: enmeshment is Minuchin's, fusion and the undifferentiated family ego mass are Bowen's (Section 7.3), and pseudomutuality and the rubber fence are Wynne's (Section 7.2).

Alignments, coalitions and detouring

Boundaries tell you how close or far apart the parts of a family are. To describe who sides with whom, Minuchin used three further terms. An alignment is the joining or opposing of family members in carrying out any activity: the mother and daughter who always agree about what to cook, the father and son who always side together on cricket. Power is each member's relative influence on what happens. A coalition is an alignment of two members against a third. Coalitions between members of the same generation are ordinary (two brothers who gang up on their sister for a week); the dangerous kind is the cross-generational coalition, a stable alliance between a parent and a child, or a grandparent and a grandchild, against the other parent, because it destroys the parental hierarchy. Jay Haley's "perverse triangle" (Section 7.5) describes the same pattern from the strategic side.

In Families and Family Therapy Minuchin described three rigid triads in which children are used to manage the conflict between their parents:

  1. Triangulation. Each parent demands that the child side with him or her against the other, so that whenever the child sides with one parent he is automatically attacking the other. The child is paralysed, because every move is disloyal to someone. (Notice that this is a narrower meaning than Bowen's triangle, Section 7.3.)
  2. Stable coalition. One parent and the child form a fixed alliance against the other parent, who is pushed to the outside.
  3. Detouring. The parents submerge their conflict by uniting around the child. In detouring-attacking they define the child as bad and unite to control him ("our son is the problem; if he behaved we would be fine"). In detouring-supportive they define the child as sick or fragile and unite to protect him. Either way the child's problem holds the marriage together, which is why Minuchin expected a marital conflict to surface as soon as the child improved.

In the Iyer family, Shalini and Kavya form a stable coalition from which Prakash is excluded, while the paternal grandmother, Kamala, who lives with them, regularly sides with Kavya against her daughter-in-law ("Let the child rest; you push her too hard"). There are two coalitions crossing generations, and a mother who is undermined from both sides.

Structural maps

To keep track of all this, Minuchin drew a structural map: a quick, simple diagram of the family's organisation as the therapist sees it in the session. A structural map is not a family tree; it shows the present, not the past. Members are placed so that the generations are at different levels, with the parental subsystem above the children, and lines show the boundaries between subsystems and the relationships between members. Minuchin's conventions, set out in Families and Family Therapy, are reproduced in most textbooks: a dashed line for a clear boundary, a dotted line for a diffuse boundary and a solid line for a rigid boundary; a double line for affiliation and a triple line for overinvolvement; a line crossed by two short bars for conflict; a bracket to mark a coalition; and an arrow to show detouring. The map is a working hypothesis, drawn early in therapy, tested in the session and redrawn as the family changes. It also gives the therapist a target: a second map of how the family would be organised if it worked well.

Read the two maps in Figure 7.5 from left to right and you have the whole plan of therapy. The therapist must bring Prakash in from behind his rigid boundary, put him alongside Shalini as a parental team, move Kavya out of the parental level and back among the children with her brother Arun, loosen the fusion between mother and daughter into a clear boundary that leaves Kavya room to grow, and help the grandmother, Kamala, keep an honoured place in the family without overruling the parents. The next two subsections describe how a structural therapist does it.

Exam trap

Bowen's genogram maps a family's history across three generations; Minuchin's structural map maps its present organisation (subsystems, boundaries, coalitions). In the structural map a dotted line is a diffuse boundary (enmeshment), a solid line is a rigid boundary (disengagement) and a dashed line is a clear boundary; do not carry over the genogram's conventions, in which a dotted line means a distant relationship. Minuchin's triangulation (each parent demands that the child take his or her side) is one of three rigid triads; the other two are the stable coalition and detouring, which can be attacking (the "bad" child) or supportive (the "sick" child).

Psychosomatic families and the lunch session

Minuchin's most famous research began with a medical puzzle. At the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia there were children with diabetes whose blood sugar went out of control again and again, landing them in hospital with ketoacidosis (a dangerous build-up of acids in the blood), even though they took their insulin and followed their diets. In hospital they stabilised quickly; at home they crashed. Lester Baker, the paediatrician in charge, wondered whether something in the family was involved, and he and Minuchin set out to find out.

Key study: the family and the diabetic child (Philadelphia, 1970s)

Question. Can family conflict produce measurable physiological changes in a child with "brittle" diabetes? Method. Minuchin, Baker and their colleagues measured free fatty acids (FFA) in the blood, a chemical index of emotional arousal that, in a diabetic child, is one of the steps on the road to ketoacidosis. The parents were interviewed about stressful family issues while the child watched from behind a one-way mirror; then the child was brought into the room and drawn into the discussion. Results. The children's FFA levels rose as they watched their parents in conflict. In the families of the "psychosomatic" diabetic children, when the child entered the room and became involved, the child's FFA kept rising while the parents' FFA fell. The parents' stress had, quite literally, been passed through the child. Conclusion. A child's involvement in parental conflict can have physical consequences, and the child's symptom can relieve the parents. Criticism. The studies were small, and an association in a laboratory session does not show that family conflict causes the illness; the findings fitted the team's model rather than testing it against alternatives.

From this work and from families of children with severe asthma and anorexia nervosa, the Philadelphia team proposed a model of the psychosomatic family (Minuchin, Baker, Rosman, Liebman, Milman and Todd, 1975). Three conditions, each necessary but none sufficient alone, were thought to combine to produce and maintain severe psychosomatic symptoms in a child: a physiological vulnerability, the child's involvement in parental conflict, and a family organisation that encourages somatising. That organisation had four characteristics:

  • Enmeshment: diffuse boundaries, so that members are intensely involved in each other's lives and bodies.
  • Overprotectiveness: a high degree of concern for each other's welfare, which retards the child's autonomy and competence.
  • Rigidity: a commitment to keeping things as they are, so that the family cannot adapt at transitions such as adolescence.
  • Lack of conflict resolution: conflict is avoided, denied or detoured, never settled.

The sick child's symptom, on this view, protected the family from its unresolved conflicts, typically through detouring.

For anorexia nervosa the team devised a dramatic technique, the family lunch session, described by Bernice Rosman, Minuchin and Ronald Liebman in 1975. Early in treatment, often in the first or second session, the therapist has lunch with the whole family, food included, and the parents are asked to get their daughter to eat. In one version each parent tries in turn while the other stays out of the way, and the therapist watches how each one fails and how the other undermines the attempt; then the two are asked to do it together. The lunch is not really about the food. It is an enactment (the next subsection) designed to bring the family's structure into the room, to strengthen the parents as a team that can take charge, to move the conflict out of the daughter's body and into the open between family members, and to redefine the problem from an illness that happens to the girl into a struggle in which she is an active participant. Minuchin and his colleagues often relabelled the anorexic girl as "stubborn" rather than "sick", because a family can do something about stubbornness, whereas sickness belongs to the doctors.

In Psychosomatic Families (1978) Minuchin, Rosman and Baker reported a successful outcome at follow-up for about 86 percent of the 53 anorexic patients they had treated, a remarkable figure for a disorder with a reputation for chronicity and death. But the series had no control group, the treatment combined family therapy with a behavioural programme in hospital, and the patients were mostly young adolescents with a short history of illness, the group most likely to do well. Later research did not find a single "psychosomatic" or "anorexic" family type, and the idea that families cause eating disorders is now rejected. What survived was the practice of involving the family and putting the parents in charge of refeeding. In 1987 Gerald Russell, Christopher Dare, Ivan Eisler and colleagues at the Maudsley Hospital in London showed in a controlled trial that family therapy did better than individual therapy for adolescents whose illness had begun early and was of short duration, and the family-based treatment that grew from their work is now the first-line treatment for adolescent anorexia (Section 7.7 returns to the evidence; the disorder itself is in the Psychiatric Disorders book).

Exam trap

The psychosomatic family (Minuchin, Rosman and Baker) is defined by enmeshment, overprotectiveness, rigidity and lack of conflict resolution, plus the child's involvement in parental conflict. It is not the same as high expressed emotion (critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement), which is a predictor of relapse measured with the Camberwell Family Interview (Section 7.2). The lunch session belongs to structural therapy for anorexia nervosa, not to Bowen, and its real aim is to strengthen the parental subsystem and bring the conflict into the open, not merely to get the patient to eat.

Joining and accommodation

Minuchin's therapy moves in two broad phases. First the therapist joins the family; then he restructures it. Joining comes first because no family will accept being challenged by a stranger. Joining is the therapist's entry into the family system: making contact with each member, showing that he understands and respects them, and earning the authority to lead. Minuchin called it the glue that holds the therapeutic system together. A structural therapist joins the family as its leader, but a leader who first adapts himself to the family's ways, and the process of adapting is called accommodation. Minuchin described three accommodation techniques.

  • Maintenance: deliberately supporting the family's existing structure at the start, so that the family feels respected rather than threatened. In the Iyer family the therapist might greet the grandmother first, as the eldest, and acknowledge how much Shalini does for Kavya.
  • Tracking: following the content of what the family says, the way a needle follows the groove of a record, with clarifying questions, encouragement and repetition, without challenging; and picking up the family's own words, themes and metaphors to use later. If Shalini says "In our house nobody keeps secrets", the therapist notes the phrase, because it will be useful when he wants to talk about privacy.
  • Mimesis: adopting the family's style, tempo, language and mood: speaking slowly with a slow, careful family, joking with a family that jokes, using the family's own expressions, sharing something of the therapist's own life ("I have a daughter too, and she also hates mornings").

Minuchin and Fishman also described the therapist joining from different distances: a close position, affiliating warmly with family members; a median position, listening neutrally and tracking; and a disengaged position, acting as an expert director who stands back from the family and gives instructions. A skilled therapist moves between them as the session requires.

Clinical link

Joining is not a preliminary to be hurried through; families drop out of therapy when it fails. In Indian settings joining usually means respecting the family's hierarchy before trying to change it: greeting the elders first, addressing the father as the head of the family if that is how the family sees itself, accepting tea, and using the family's language and idiom. A therapist who challenges a grandmother's authority in the first ten minutes will not see the family again; a therapist who has first earned her trust can later ask her to let the mother decide.

Enactment, unbalancing and boundary making: restructuring the family

Once joined, the therapist sets out to change the structure, and the techniques that do this are known together as restructuring. They share one principle: change happens in the session, through action, not through talk about events at home. In Family Therapy Techniques (1981) Minuchin and Fishman grouped them under three strategies: challenging the symptom, challenging the family structure and challenging the family's view of reality. Table 7.6 summarises the main techniques.

The signature technique is enactment: the therapist asks family members to deal with each other, in the session, about the problem, instead of describing it to the therapist. "Mrs Iyer, you have told me Kavya will not go to bed on time. Talk to her about it now, and see if you can agree on a time." Minuchin and Fishman described an enactment in three movements. The therapist observes the family's spontaneous transactions and decides which area to highlight; he then elicits a transaction, directing members to talk to each other about it; and finally he steps in to suggest or demand a different way of transacting ("Mr Iyer, your wife needs your help; do not let Kavya walk away"). Enactment does two things. It gives the therapist direct evidence of the structure (who interrupts, who rescues, who gives up), so that the structural map can be tested. And it gives the family the experience of doing something differently, which, Minuchin believed, changes people more than any amount of explanation. While the family enacts, the therapist resists being pulled in; if Kavya turns to him for support, he points her back to her parents.

Messages must not only be given; they must be heard. Minuchin used the word intensity for the means of making a structural message register with a family whose rules filter out anything new: repeating the same message many times, repeating it across different contents (homework, bedtime, food, phone use all turn out to be the same struggle), prolonging an interaction past the point where the family usually stops, changing the physical distance between members, and refusing to accept the family's pull to return to the old pattern.

Boundary making creates, strengthens or loosens boundaries. A structural therapist may rearrange the seating, moving Kavya away from her mother and seating the parents side by side; block interruptions ("Shalini, let Kavya answer for herself"); insist that members speak for themselves; hold part of a session with the parents alone, sending the children out to establish that some business belongs to the couple; or give homework that redraws the lines, such as the parents going out alone once a week while the grandmother looks after the children, Kavya moving back to her own room, or Prakash taking over the bedtime routine. For a disengaged pair the work runs the other way: the therapist increases contact, asking Prakash and Kavya to plan something together.

Unbalancing changes the hierarchy within a subsystem by the therapist's own deliberate partiality. Instead of remaining neutral, the therapist temporarily affiliates with one member to give that person more power, ignores another member to reduce his or her influence, or enters a coalition with some members against another. In the Iyer family the therapist backs Prakash every time he tries to set a limit, even when he does it clumsily, and ignores Kavya's attempts to draw him into her alliance with her mother. Unbalancing feels unfair, and it is meant to: it breaks a stalemate that fair treatment would leave in place, and the therapist balances it out over the course of therapy.

Reframing (defined formally with the MRI in Section 7.5) challenges the family's view of reality by offering a new, systemic definition of the problem that makes change possible. The family arrives with its own frame ("Kavya is sick"; "Kavya is lazy") and the therapist offers another ("Kavya is fighting a battle that belongs to the grown-ups"; "your daughter is stubborn, and she is winning, because you two keep disagreeing about what to do"). The new frame shifts the problem from inside one person to the relationships around her, and points to what the family can do. Closely related is complementarity: helping members see that their behaviour is part of a larger whole, that the mother's over-involvement and the father's absence fit together like the two halves of one pattern, so that neither is simply the villain or the victim. Structural therapists also point out the family's strengths, because a family is more willing to change when it feels competent.

Table 7.6 The main techniques of structural family therapy, illustrated with the Iyer family.
TechniqueWhat the therapist doesExample
Joining (maintenance, tracking, mimesis)Enters the system by respecting and adapting to its styleGreets the grandmother first; uses the family's phrase "no secrets"
EnactmentHas members deal with the problem in the session"Talk to Kavya about bedtime now"
IntensityRepeats and prolongs messages until they registerKeeps the parents negotiating past the usual breaking point
Boundary makingCreates or redraws boundaries between subsystemsMoves Kavya's chair; sees the parents alone
UnbalancingSides temporarily with one member to shift powerBacks every limit Prakash sets
Reframing and complementarityOffers a systemic definition; shows how behaviours fit together"She is stubborn, not sick"; mother's closeness and father's absence fit together
RestructuringThe umbrella aim: a new organisation in which the symptom has no functionParents lead together; Kavya returns to school

Here, in brief, is how the Iyer family might move through therapy. In the first session the therapist joins each member and tracks the story of Kavya's school refusal. When Kavya interrupts her father for the third time, he asks Prakash and Shalini to decide together, there and then, what time Kavya will leave for school tomorrow (an enactment). Shalini keeps turning to Kavya to ask her opinion; the therapist blocks this, moves Kavya to sit beside her brother, and backs Prakash when he speaks up (boundary making and unbalancing). He reframes the problem: Kavya is not ill but loyal, staying at home to keep her mother company, and only her parents together can release her. Homework puts Prakash in charge of the morning routine for a week and sends the couple out alone on Sunday evening. Over the following sessions the grandmother is thanked for her years of help and invited to support the parents' decisions rather than overrule them. As the parental team grows stronger and the mother-daughter boundary clearer, Kavya goes back to school, and, as Minuchin would have predicted, the strains in the marriage, long hidden behind Kavya, come into view and become the next focus.

Exam trap

The core sequence of structural therapy is joining, then restructuring. Mimesis (copying the family's style), tracking (following its content and metaphors) and maintenance (supporting its structure) are joining techniques. Enactment (acting out the problem in the session), boundary making, unbalancing (temporarily siding with one member) and reframing are restructuring techniques. Paradoxical directives and prescribing the symptom are strategic (Section 7.5), and circular questioning is Milan (Section 7.5); reframing is shared by structural, strategic and MRI therapists, so a question about reframing alone does not identify Minuchin.

Bowen and Minuchin compared

Bowen and Minuchin are the two pillars of classical family therapy, and they are easily confused. Both were psychiatrists trained in psychoanalysis who broke away from it, both treated the family as a system, and both cared about boundaries between selves and between generations. But they differed on almost everything else, as Table 7.7 shows. Bowen looked at the past and at three generations; Minuchin at the present and the household. Bowen's therapist was a calm coach who stayed outside the family's emotional field; Minuchin's was an active director who joined the family and then pushed it to change. Bowen worked on the differentiation of individuals, often seeing one person; Minuchin worked on the organisation of the whole family, and wanted everyone in the room.

Table 7.7 Bowen's family systems therapy and Minuchin's structural family therapy.
FeatureBowenMinuchin
Time focusPast and present; three or more generationsPresent; the current household
Key conceptsDifferentiation, triangles, projection, transmission, cutoffStructure, subsystems, hierarchy, boundaries, enmeshment and disengagement
Unhealthy patternFusion and chronic anxietyDiffuse or rigid boundaries; confused hierarchy; cross-generational coalitions
Therapist's stanceCalm, neutral, detriangled coachActive leader who joins, then challenges and directs
Who attendsOften the parents or one motivated personThe whole family, with subsystems seen separately at times
Signature toolsGenogram, process questions, I-position, coaching visits homeStructural map, joining, enactment, unbalancing, boundary making
Main goalHigher differentiation of self; lower anxietyA reorganised family in which the symptom has no function

Structural therapy has had a long afterlife. Its ideas run through brief strategic family therapy, developed by José Szapocznik in Miami for Hispanic adolescents with drug problems, through multisystemic therapy and functional family therapy (Section 7.7), and through the family-based treatment of adolescent anorexia. It has also drawn sharp criticism. Some critics found the therapist's power and manipulation troubling, and asked whose idea of a "normal" family the goal map expressed. Feminist family therapists pointed out that strengthening the parental hierarchy could mean strengthening a dominant father, and that the enmeshed mother and the peripheral father were too easily read as the mother's fault (Section 7.7 takes up gender and culture). And from the 1980s the postmodern therapists rejected the idea that a family has an objective structure for an expert to diagnose at all.

That last point also tells you which kind of theory a statement about reality belongs to. The statement "individuals, families and entire cultures are given a different level of power as creators of their realities" expresses social constructionism, the view that reality is constructed through language and shared stories, and it describes narrative therapy (Section 6.8 and Section 7.7). Structural and transgenerational therapies are the opposite kind of theory: they assume that a family really has a structure (Minuchin) or a multigenerational emotional process (Bowen) that a trained observer can map and change. Strategic therapy (Section 7.5) likewise treats the therapist as the expert who designs the intervention.

Exam trap

Statements about people as creators of their own realities, about the power of dominant stories, or about re-authoring lives point to narrative therapy (Michael White and David Epston), not to structural therapy (Minuchin's real family structure), transgenerational theory (Bowen's emotional processes across generations) or strategic therapy (Haley's expert directives). Structural therapy is the answer when the stem mentions subsystems, boundaries, hierarchy, enmeshment, disengagement, joining or enactment; Bowen is the answer when it mentions differentiation, triangles, emotional cutoff, multigenerational transmission or the genogram.

  4. Bowen took his ideas about sibling position from the psychologist , author of Family Constellation (1961).

7.5 Strategic, Brief and Systemic Therapies: Haley, the MRI and MilanLower yield · not asked yet

Murray Bowen and Salvador Minuchin, whom you met in Sections 7.3 and 7.4, both want to change the way a family is organised: Bowen by raising each member's differentiation over years of coaching, Minuchin by redrawing the family's boundaries in the room. The therapists in this section have a more modest and, in a way, more radical aim. They want to solve the problem the family came with, as quickly as possible, and they are not much interested in why it began. All of them grew from a single root, the Bateson project in Palo Alto that gave us the double bind (Section 7.2) and the axioms of communication in Pragmatics of Human Communication (Section 7.1). From that root grew three branches: the strategic therapy of Jay Haley and Cloe Madanes, eventually based in Washington; the brief therapy of the Mental Research Institute (MRI) in Palo Alto; and the systemic therapy of a team of four psychiatrists in Milan, Italy.

The three branches look different on the surface, but they share five convictions, and if you hold on to these you will rarely mix them up with the other schools. First, the presenting problem is to be taken seriously in its own right; it is not merely the surface sign of a deeper disorder that must be dug out. Second, problems are kept alive by what people are doing now, in the present pattern of interaction, so history and insight are unnecessary for change. Third, the therapist, not the family, is responsible for planning how change will happen. Fourth, change can be fast, often within ten sessions. Fifth, because families often resist direct advice, indirect methods, including the deliberately paradoxical, are sometimes the most effective ones. You have already met the individual version of paradox in Section 4.10 (behaviour therapy's paradoxical intention, tested on insomnia) and in Section 6.5 (Viktor Frankl's logotherapy). This section teaches the systemic version, in which the target is not one person's anxiety but the job a symptom is doing for a family.

From Palo Alto to strategic therapy: Haley and Erickson

In 1959 the psychiatrist Don Jackson (1920 to 1968), a member of Bateson's team, founded the Mental Research Institute (MRI) in Palo Alto, together with the psychiatrist Jules Riskin and the social worker Virginia Satir, whose own therapy you will meet in Section 7.6. For a few years Bateson's research project and the new institute ran side by side, sharing people and ideas, until the project ended in 1962 and Bateson turned to the communication of dolphins. The MRI went on to become the most influential training and research centre of the early family therapy movement.

The most restless mind in the Palo Alto group belonged to Jay Haley (1923 to 2007). Haley had trained not as a clinician but in theatre and communication, and he came to the project as a young man fascinated by the ways people use communication to control one another. Through the project he and John Weakland began, in the 1950s, to make repeated visits to the psychiatrist and hypnotherapist Milton Erickson (1901 to 1980) in Phoenix, Arizona. (Section 3.10 introduced Erickson's indirect, permissive style of hypnosis, and Section 6.8 traced his influence on solution-focused therapy.) What struck Haley was that Erickson cured people in a handful of sessions without ever interpreting anything. He accepted whatever the patient brought, including the resistance, and used it. Haley spent years studying Erickson's cases and in Uncommon Therapy (1973) he extracted the principles behind them, among them encouraging resistance (asking a reluctant patient to hold back even more, so that holding back becomes cooperation), providing a worse alternative (so that the desired change looks like the easier choice), communicating in metaphor, encouraging a relapse (so that a relapse, if it comes, is under the therapist's direction), seeding ideas early that would be used later, amplifying a small deviation once it appeared, and avoiding self-exploration.

In the same book Haley gave the new approach its name. Therapy can be called strategic therapy, he wrote, if the clinician initiates what happens during therapy and designs a particular approach for each problem. Notice how much this definition leaves out. It says nothing about a theory of personality, nothing about the past, nothing about insight or growth. What makes a therapy strategic is that the therapist takes charge: she sets a clear goal, plans how to reach it, and accepts responsibility for whether it is reached.

Haley's career then took him through two other centres of family therapy. In 1967 he left the MRI to join Salvador Minuchin and Braulio Montalvo at the Philadelphia Child Guidance Clinic, where he absorbed Minuchin's concern with family structure and hierarchy (Section 7.4); his own work became a blend of Erickson's strategy and Minuchin's structure. And in 1976 he and his then wife, the Argentine-born psychologist Cloe Madanes, founded the Family Therapy Institute of Washington, DC, which trained strategic therapists for the next two decades. This is why textbooks sometimes speak of the "Washington school" of strategic therapy, to distinguish Haley and Madanes from the Palo Alto brief therapists described in Section 7.5.5, who used many of the same techniques but did not share Haley's interest in hierarchy.

Exam trap

Strategic family therapy is Jay Haley and Cloe Madanes; structural is Salvador Minuchin; multigenerational or Bowen family systems is Murray Bowen; experiential is Virginia Satir and Carl Whitaker (Section 7.6). Haley worked with Minuchin in Philadelphia, but he is never the answer to "structural family therapy". Milton Erickson (1901 to 1980), the hypnotherapist who inspired Haley and the brief therapists, is not Erik Erikson, the psychoanalyst of the eight psychosocial stages. The MRI was founded by Don Jackson in 1959, not by Bateson, though it grew out of Bateson's project.

Haley's problem-solving therapy: power, hierarchy and the first interview

Haley's starting point was the Palo Alto idea that every message has a content (report) aspect and a relationship (command) aspect (Section 7.1). When a mother says to her son, "Your room is a mess," she is reporting on the room and also telling him to clean it; if he leaves it as it is, the two of them are in a quiet struggle over who defines their relationship. In Strategies of Psychotherapy (1963) Haley argued that much human behaviour, including much psychopathology, is best understood as a struggle of this kind. A symptom, on this view, is a way of controlling a relationship while denying that one is doing so. A wife who has panic attacks whenever her husband plans to go out with his friends gains enormous influence over his evenings, yet she never asks him to stay; the panic does the asking, and she can say, truthfully, that she cannot help it. Symptoms are powerful precisely because they are defined as involuntary.

From Minuchin, Haley took the idea of hierarchy: a well-functioning family has an organisation in which the parents are in charge of the children and the generations are clearly marked. Trouble arises when the hierarchy is confused, and Haley singled out one pattern as especially damaging. In a 1967 paper on pathological systems he described the perverse triangle: two people of different generations (a mother and her son, a grandmother and her grandson) form a coalition against a third person of the same generation as one of them (the father, the mother), and the coalition is denied. The boy who shouts at his father and is quietly defended by his mother is caught in such a triangle; so is the young wife whose mother-in-law sides openly with her son against her. Section 7.1 introduced coalitions and triangles in general; Haley's addition was that a cross-generational coalition that cannot be acknowledged is the most common structure behind a child's symptoms.

Haley thought in triads rather than individuals or dyads. A child's problem behaviour usually involves at least two adults: one parent who is over-involved with the child and another who is peripheral. Therapy therefore cannot work by persuading the child alone; it must change the sequence of behaviour among all three. Change the sequence, and the symptom loses its job.

Haley set out his method most clearly in Problem-Solving Therapy (1976), which opens with the sentence: "If therapy is to end properly, it must begin properly." By "properly" he meant negotiating a solvable problem and discovering the social situation that makes the problem necessary. He divided the first interview into five stages, summarised in Table 7.8. They give a strategic therapist's first session its recognisable shape, and they are worth knowing because examiners occasionally list them.

Table 7.8 The five stages of Haley's first interview (Problem-Solving Therapy, 1976).
StageWhat the therapist doesWhy
1 Social stageGreets every member, helps each feel at ease, notices where they sit and who speaks for whomThe family's organisation shows before anyone mentions the problem
2 Problem stageAsks each member for his or her view of the problem, often starting with the adult who seems least involved; offers no interpretationsEvery member's view counts, and no one feels blamed
3 Interaction stageAsks members to talk to one another about the problem, in the roomThe therapist sees the sequence, the coalitions and the hierarchy in action
4 Goal-setting stageDefines the problem in concrete, solvable terms ("Manav will go to school four days a week")A clear goal makes progress measurable
5 Task-setting stageEnds the session with a directive to be carried out at homeTherapy happens between sessions; whether the task is done is itself information

Two features of the method distinguish it from almost everything you met in Chapters 3 and 6. The therapist decides on the goal, after hearing the family, and the goal is concrete: not "better communication" but "father and son will spend an hour together each Sunday without the mother present". And the therapist does not offer insight. Haley argued that pointing out to people why they behave as they do usually makes them defensive, and that people change first and understand later, if at all.

Exam trap

For Haley a symptom is a strategy for controlling a relationship while denying the control ("I cannot help it"), not an unconscious compromise (Freud) or a learned habit (behaviour therapy). The perverse triangle (Haley, 1967) is a denied cross-generational coalition. Haley's interaction stage, in which members talk to each other in the session, looks like Minuchin's enactment (Section 7.4); if the option says "enactment", think Minuchin. Strategic goals are set by the therapist and are concrete; insight is not a goal.

Directives: straightforward, paradoxical and ordeal

The core technique of strategic therapy is the directive: an instruction from the therapist about what family members are to do, in the session or at home. Directives are to strategic therapy what interpretations are to psychoanalysis, the main vehicle of change. Haley (1976) gave three reasons for relying on them. First, a task changes the way people behave, and therefore the sequence that maintains the problem. Second, because the therapist designs the task and asks for a report, she becomes an important person in the family's week, and her influence grows. Third, whether or not the task is done, and how, gives the therapist information about the family that no amount of talk would reveal. Directives come in two broad kinds.

A straightforward directive is an instruction the therapist expects the family to follow. Sometimes it is plain advice, but advice rarely works on its own, because the family has usually been given it already. More often a straightforward directive is designed to alter a sequence. A peripheral father might be asked to take his daughter on an outing that the over-involved mother has no interest in, so that father and daughter spend time together without the mother stepping in. A couple who meet only to quarrel might be sent back to a place where they were happy when they first met. Haley insisted that directives be precise (who does what, when, for how long), easy enough to complete, suited to the abilities of every member including the children, and preceded by a careful look at what the family has already tried, so that the therapist does not prescribe a failed solution all over again. Some directives are metaphorical: the family is asked to solve a small problem that has the same shape as a larger one they cannot discuss. A mother and her grown daughter who are too entangled to talk about the daughter leaving home might be set the task of separating their bank accounts; settling who pays which bills becomes a rehearsal, in miniature, for the larger separation.

A paradoxical directive is an instruction to keep or even increase the very behaviour the family wants to change, given in such a way that the family benefits whether it obeys or rebels. It is reserved for families that have resisted straightforward tasks. The family therapist Peggy Papp of the Ackerman Institute in New York described three steps in a systemic paradox. The therapist first redefines the symptom as serving a positive purpose for the family (anger is described as caring, a child's misbehaviour as a way of keeping the parents united). She then prescribes the symptom: since it does so much good, the family should continue it, often on a schedule. Finally, when the family begins to improve, she restrains change, warning them not to change too fast, because they would lose what the symptom provides. This last move is called restraining, and in its mildest form ("go slowly; your family may not be ready to change yet") it is used by almost every strategic and brief therapist.

Push a case through to see how the three steps work. Ten-year-old Kiran, the only son of a busy couple in Bengaluru, has had stomach aches every school night for four months. Doctors find nothing. Each evening at about seven, Kiran doubles over; his mother rushes to him, phones his father, and the father, who otherwise works until ten, comes home early. That evening the parents sit together by Kiran's bed, worried about him and, for once, not quarrelling with each other. After a careful first interview, the therapist sees the sequence: the aches bring father home and give the parents something to share other than their disagreements. She tells the family, with complete seriousness, that Kiran's stomach is doing an important job for them: it brings his father home and keeps his parents close (redefining). So, she says, until the family finds another way to do that job, Kiran should have his stomach ache every evening at exactly seven o'clock, and both parents must sit with him and comfort him for fifteen minutes, which means that father must be home by seven (prescribing). When, two weeks later, the family reports that the aches are fewer and that Kiran has been "forgetting" his seven o'clock appointment, she frowns and warns them not to improve too quickly: what would happen to the parents' evenings if Kiran's stomach stopped needing them (restraining)? In the next sessions the parents, now at home in the evenings for their own reasons, work directly on their quarrels with the therapist.

Why should such an instruction work? Haley's answer was relational rather than individual. A symptom is powerful because it is involuntary; it controls others while the person disowns responsibility. Once the symptom is performed on the therapist's instructions, it is no longer involuntary, and it no longer controls anyone. If Kiran has his ache on schedule, he is obeying the therapist; if he does not, he has given up the ache; either way the old sequence is broken. Section 4.10 described the two forms this takes, compliance-based paradox (the family obeys and the symptom becomes voluntary) and defiance-based paradox (the family rebels against the therapist by getting better), and the "therapeutic double bind" in which the therapist wins either way; it also reported Shoham-Salomon and Rosenthal's 1987 meta-analysis showing that paradoxical interventions are roughly as effective as other psychological treatments. The important contrast is with Frankl. In logotherapy, paradoxical intention is given openly to one person, to defuse his own anticipatory anxiety (Section 6.5). The strategic prescription is aimed at the function a symptom serves in a family, and it is often given without explaining the reasoning, because a family that understood the reasoning would simply resist it in a new way.

The third kind of directive is the most memorable. In Ordeal Therapy (1984), Haley described a technique he had learnt from watching Erickson. Its principle is simple: if you make it more difficult for a person to have a symptom than to give it up, the person will give up the symptom. In ordeal therapy the client agrees, in advance, that every time the symptom occurs he will carry out a task that is harder than the symptom itself. The ordeal has to meet strict conditions. It must cause at least as much discomfort as the symptom; it must be good for the person, or at least harmless (exercise, study, housework, a donation to a cause); it must be something he can actually do and cannot legitimately object to; it must be precisely defined; and it must be performed every time, without exception, until the symptom disappears. Because the ordeal is unpleasant, the therapist first asks for a commitment: the client must want to get over the problem badly enough to promise to do whatever is asked. A typical ordeal for insomnia, of the kind Erickson used, requires the client to get out of bed whenever he is not asleep within a set time and spend the night on some useful but dull chore, such as polishing the floors. A typical Indian version might be given to Tanvi, a seventeen-year-old preparing for her medical entrance examination, who bites her nails until they bleed: every time she bites them, she must get up and solve ten extra physics problems from the chapter she finds most boring. The ordeal is good for her (she needs the practice anyway), it is more tiresome than the habit, and she cannot reasonably refuse it.

Clinical link

Strategic directives raise real ethical questions, because some are given without telling the family the reasoning. Modern strategic therapists answer them in three ways. They never prescribe a dangerous symptom (self-harm, violence, drinking, driving while panicking; see also the warning in Section 4.10). They frame a paradox in terms that are true and respectful ("the aches are doing an important job") rather than sarcastic. And they treat paradox as a minority tool for stuck families, not as the first move. Haley's own defence was that every therapist influences clients; the honest question is not whether to influence but whether to plan the influence carefully and take responsibility for it.

Exam trap

"Paradoxical injunctions", "paradoxical directives", "strategic paradox" and "prescribing the symptom" in a family therapy question point to the strategic and systemic therapists (Haley, Madanes, the MRI, the Milan team), not to Bowen's differentiation, Minuchin's boundaries or narrative externalisation. "Paradoxical intention" belongs to Frankl (Section 6.5). Papp's three steps are redefine, prescribe, restrain. Ordeal therapy is Haley's (1984, from Erickson): a beneficial task the client performs every time the symptom occurs. It is not aversion therapy (Section 4.4), which pairs the unwanted behaviour with an unpleasant stimulus such as a nausea-inducing drug or a shock.

Cloe Madanes: protection, pretending and incongruous hierarchies

Cloe Madanes (born 1940) studied psychology in her native Buenos Aires, moved to the United States, and from 1976 directed the Washington institute together with Haley. In Strategic Family Therapy (1981) and Behind the One-Way Mirror (1984) she gave strategic therapy a gentler, more playful face. Where Haley emphasised power, Madanes emphasised the love and protectiveness that symptoms often express.

Madanes saw a symptom as a metaphor. A child's "My stomach hurts" carries an explicit message (pain in the stomach) and an implicit one (perhaps "I am worried about Mother", or "Something in this house hurts"). Often, she argued, a child's symptom is an attempt to help a parent: a boy who misbehaves gives his depressed mother something to be angry about, and anger pulls her out of her depression for a while; a girl who refuses to go to school stays at home to keep an eye on a mother she fears may harm herself. The child is protecting the parent, without either of them knowing it.

This protection creates a peculiar organisation that Madanes called an incongruous hierarchy. Officially the parents are in charge and the child is beneath them. But through the symptom the child controls the parents: they rearrange their days around his aches, his fears, his refusals. The child is at once inferior (as a child) and superior (as the one whose symptom governs the household), and the two hierarchies contradict each other. Strategic therapy aims to restore a congruous hierarchy, with the parents openly in charge and the child free to be a child.

Madanes's signature contribution was a technique that uses play instead of defiance. In a 1980 paper, "Protection, paradox and pretending", and in her 1981 book, she described the pretend technique: the therapist asks the child to pretend to have the symptom, and asks the parents to pretend to help him, or asks a parent to pretend to have a problem so that the child can pretend to help. Three things happen at once. A symptom that is pretended cannot also be real, so the real one starts to look silly. The child's wish to help or protect the parent is acted out in play, so the real symptom is no longer needed to do the job. And because the parents direct the game, the hierarchy is quietly put right. Applied to Kiran, the therapist might ask him to put on his best fake stomach ache at the dinner table, while his father plays the doctor and his mother plays the worried nurse, and ask the family to repeat the game every evening for a week. Families usually end up laughing, which is part of the point: it is hard to take an ache seriously after you have staged it twice as comedy.

Key study: Madanes's boy who set fires (1981)

The problem. A mother of five consulted Madanes because her ten-year-old son was setting fires. The father had just left the family, sent no money and moved to another city; the mother, who had come from Puerto Rico and spoke no English, was overwhelmed and would not leave the boy alone for a minute. The observation. In the first interview the therapist gave the boy matches, asked him to light one and asked the mother to do whatever she did at home when she caught him. The mother took the match and burned him with it. Madanes's hypothesis was that the fire-setting was helping the mother: by giving her someone to blame and punish, the boy made her angry instead of depressed, and anger kept her going. The intervention. The therapist taught the boy to light matches safely and to put them out properly. She then asked the mother to light some paper in an ashtray and pretend to burn herself, while the boy rescued her by putting out the fire with water; the other children could watch but not join in. The mother was to repeat this game with him every evening for a week. The result. The boy stopped setting fires after this session. He was still special in the family, but now as an expert on fires who helped his mother, not as a threat; and when he lit fires under her direction he was once more beneath her in the hierarchy. Criticism. This is a single uncontrolled case, reported by the therapist, and it shows the strengths and limits of the strategic literature: vivid, clever and persuasive case reports, with few controlled trials behind them.

In later work, especially Sex, Love and Violence (1990), Madanes applied strategic methods to violence and sexual abuse in families, and sorted family problems according to four underlying intentions: the wish to dominate and control, the wish to be loved, the wish to love and protect others, and the wish to repent and forgive. The therapist's task, she suggested, is to move a family from the first of these towards the others.

Exam trap

The pretend technique is Cloe Madanes's (1980, 1981): the child pretends to have the symptom and the parents pretend to help. Do not confuse it with Moreno's role reversal and doubling in psychodrama (Section 7.11), George Kelly's fixed-role therapy (Section 5.5), Adler's "acting as if" (Section 3.8) or the Gestalt empty chair (Section 6.3). Incongruous hierarchy (the child is both beneath and above the parents) is also Madanes's term. Ordeal therapy is Haley's.

The MRI brief therapy model: when the solution becomes the problem

Haley's departure did not end the MRI's interest in rapid change. In 1965 the psychiatrist Richard Fisch (1926 to 2011) sent Don Jackson a memo proposing a project in short-term therapy, and with Jackson's backing he, John Weakland (1919 to 1995), Paul Watzlawick (Section 7.1) and Arthur Bodin set up the MRI's Brief Therapy Center. Weakland had been the first recruit to Bateson's project; a chemical engineer by training, he had turned to anthropology after meeting Bateson. The centre met one day a week, saw whatever cases came to it, and set itself a strict rule: treatment would last a maximum of ten sessions. The team worked in pairs, one therapist in the room and the others watching through a one-way mirror, and every case was discussed before and after each session.

Key study: Brief therapy, focused problem resolution (Weakland, Fisch, Watzlawick and Bodin, 1974)

Question. Can a wide range of problems be resolved in no more than ten sessions by focusing only on the presenting complaint and on what keeps it going? Method. Over about six years the Brief Therapy Center, working one day a week, treated 97 cases of every kind that came to it, in which 236 individuals were seen. Treatment was limited to a maximum of ten sessions, and outcome was judged at follow-up by whether the main complaint had been resolved. Result. Treatment was judged a significant success in about three-quarters of the cases: 40 percent were rated successes (the main complaint resolved), another 32 percent were significantly improved, and 28 percent were failures. Conclusion. Many problems are maintained by current interaction and can be resolved quickly when that interaction is changed. Criticism. There was no control group, the cases were not randomly allocated, and the outcome was assessed by the treating team itself, so the study is better seen as an encouraging demonstration than as proof.

The theory behind the centre was set out in Change: Principles of Problem Formation and Problem Resolution (1974) by Watzlawick, Weakland and Fisch, and the practice in The Tactics of Change: Doing Therapy Briefly (1982) by Fisch, Weakland and Lynn Segal. Its central idea fits in one sentence: the attempted solution becomes the problem. Life is full of ordinary difficulties (a child's tantrums, a bad night's sleep, a teenager's moodiness, a quarrel between newlyweds). Most of them pass. A difficulty becomes a "problem" in the clinical sense when people respond to it with an attempted solution that makes it worse, and then, because the difficulty is worse, apply more of the same solution, which makes it worse still. The authors called one of their chapters "More of the same, or: when the solution becomes the problem". The therapist's job is to identify the attempted solution and stop it. Figure 7.6 shows the loop.

Take the example in the figure. After failing his twelfth-standard board examination, Varun is miserable. His parents, frightened, try to cheer him up: they list his good qualities, tell him that failure means nothing, plan outings, and urge him to look on the bright side. Every cheerful remark tells Varun that his sadness is unacceptable and that no one understands how bad he feels, so he withdraws to his room; his parents, more frightened, cheer harder. The MRI therapist would not explore Varun's childhood or his parents' marriage. She would ask the parents exactly what they have tried, and then ask them to do something that runs against all of it: to tell Varun, in their own words, that anyone would feel low after such a blow, that they do not expect him to feel better soon, and that he should take as long as he needs. Brief therapists call such a reversal a "180-degree turn". Freed from the demand to cheer up, Varun often begins to talk.

The book Change also gave family therapy one of its most quoted distinctions. First-order change is change within a system that itself stays the same: the members try harder, or try a variation of the same thing, but the rules of the system are untouched. Second-order change is a change of the system itself, a change in the rules that govern it; the authors called it "change of change". Two of their illustrations are worth remembering. A person having a nightmare can do many things inside the dream: run, hide, scream, fight. All of these are first-order changes, and none of them ends the nightmare. The only way out is to wake up, which is a change of a different order. Similarly, in a car you can press the accelerator harder (first-order change, more of the same), or you can change gear (second-order change, a different way of producing speed).
