Families stuck in a problem are usually making first-order changes: the parents of a defiant teenager first scold, then scold louder, then scold and punish, and the pattern of escalation stays exactly the same. Second-order change often looks illogical or paradoxical from inside the system, which is why it so often has to come from outside, from the therapist.

Watzlawick, Weakland and Fisch described three ways in which an ordinary difficulty gets mishandled until it becomes a problem. Action may be needed but not taken, because the difficulty is denied ("there is nothing wrong with our marriage"); they called this the terrible simplification. Action may be taken when it should not be, in pursuit of an impossible ideal (the couple who must never disagree, the parents who expect a teenager to be always cheerful); they called this the utopia syndrome. And action may be taken at the wrong level, as when a first-order solution is applied to a problem that needs second-order change, or when someone demands a behaviour that can only happen spontaneously: "Be spontaneous!", "I want you to want to help me", "Stop being so self-conscious". These are the paradoxes, and they are the everyday cousins of Bateson's double bind (Section 7.2).

In The Tactics of Change Fisch, Weakland and Segal listed five attempted solutions that they met again and again, each with its typical reversal (Table 7.9). Notice how many of them involve trying to force something that cannot be forced.

Table 7.9 Five common attempted solutions and the MRI reversal of each (after Fisch, Weakland and Segal, 1982).
Attempted solutionExampleTypical MRI reversal
Trying to force something that can only happen spontaneouslyStraining to fall asleep, to feel sexually aroused, to stop a tremorStop trying; even try to produce the opposite
Trying to master a feared event by postponing itA student avoids the library until she feels "ready"; a man avoids speaking until he is sure he will not stammerApproach the feared event while deliberately performing imperfectly
Trying to reach agreement through oppositionParents argue a rebellious son out of his views; spouses lecture each otherTake a one-down position: agree, ask for advice, admit helplessness
Trying to get compliance through voluntarism"I should not have to ask; you should want to help"Ask directly and plainly, even arbitrarily
Confirming the accuser's suspicions by defending oneselfA husband protests his fidelity to a jealous wife, which convinces her he is hiding somethingStop defending; agree with the accusation or jam the accusation game

MRI brief therapy follows a regular sequence. The therapist first defines the problem in concrete behavioural terms: who does what, to whom, when, and why it is a problem now. She then investigates the attempted solutions in great detail, because they are the target. Next she agrees on a minimal, concrete goal ("What would be the first small sign that things were getting better?"); brief therapists believe that a small change in the right place can spread through the system. She then designs an intervention that blocks the attempted solution, usually by prescribing something that runs against it, and phrases it in the client's own language and in a way that fits the client's position, that is, his values and his view of the problem. Throughout, she restrains change ("go slowly"), and she ends treatment as soon as the goal is reached. The MRI team did not insist on seeing the whole family. They worked with whoever was most concerned about the problem and most willing to do something, sometimes a single parent or spouse. In one series of cases, Lynn Segal worked only with the wives of men who had had heart attacks and refused to change their diet or exercise. The wives' attempted solution was to nag and argue; one wife was asked instead to tell her husband to live the rest of his life exactly as he pleased, however short it might be, and to start, in his presence, going over life insurance and plans for the estate. Within weeks the husband began his rehabilitation exercises.

The most widely borrowed MRI technique is reframing. To reframe, in the words of Change, is to change the conceptual or emotional setting in which a situation is experienced, placing it in another frame that fits the facts of the situation equally well or even better, and thereby changing its entire meaning. The facts do not change; their meaning does, and with it the behaviour that follows. The authors' favourite illustration came from Mark Twain: Tom Sawyer, ordered to whitewash a fence as a punishment, reframes the chore as a rare privilege, and his friends end up paying him for the chance to do it. In a family, a teenager's "disobedience" might be reframed as "growing independence that frightens everyone", a mother's "nagging" as "worry about how much she cares", a husband's silence as "his way of avoiding a fight he does not want to have with you". A good reframe must fit the facts and the family's own values, or it will be rejected. A simpler cousin, relabelling, changes only the adjective (from "stubborn" to "determined"). Reframing is now used across family therapy, by structural therapists (Section 7.4), by motivational interviewers (Section 6.9) and many others, but it entered the field through the MRI.

Exam trap

First-order change is change within an unchanged system (more of the same); second-order change is change of the system's rules ("change of change"); both terms come from Change (1974) by Watzlawick, Weakland and Fisch, not from Pragmatics of Human Communication (1967) by Watzlawick, Beavin and Jackson, the source of "one cannot not communicate" (Section 7.1). "Second-order" conditioning is Pavlov's, and has nothing to do with family therapy. MRI brief therapy is problem-focused and limited to ten sessions: it asks which attempted solution keeps the problem going and stops it. Solution-focused brief therapy (de Shazer and Berg, Milwaukee; Section 6.8) asks what is already working and does more of it. Reframing entered family therapy through the MRI and the strategic therapists.

The Milan systemic school

The third branch grew far from California. Mara Selvini Palazzoli (1916 to 1999) was a Milanese psychiatrist and psychoanalyst who had spent years treating young women with anorexia nervosa in individual psychoanalytic therapy, with results that frustrated her. In 1967 she founded a centre for the study of the family in Milan, at first with a psychoanalytic orientation. In 1971 she and three colleagues, the psychiatrists Luigi Boscolo (1932 to 2015), Gianfranco Cecchin (1932 to 2004) and Giuliana Prata, broke with the psychoanalytic approach, adopted the ideas of Bateson and the MRI, and formed the team that became known as the Milan associates. Their therapy is called Milan systemic therapy. They described their early work with families of patients with anorexia and schizophrenia in Paradox and Counterparadox (published in Italian in 1975 and in English in 1978).

The Milan team worked as a team in the most literal sense. A male and female pair of therapists conducted the interview while the other two watched through a one-way mirror, and every session had the same five-part structure, shown in Figure 7.7. The team also made a discovery about timing. At first they saw families weekly, but they found that their interventions needed time to work through the family, so they spaced sessions about a month apart and offered about ten sessions in all. Because the total number of sessions was small but the treatment lasted most of a year, the approach came to be called "long brief therapy".

What did the team see in these families? The Milan associates described rigid "games" in which every member was caught, and above all a paradox: the family came asking for the identified patient to be changed, while behaving in every way to keep the family exactly as it was. Their answer was a counterparadox: instead of pushing for change, which the family would resist, the team told the family not to change, at least for now. To make this credible, they first had to describe the family's pattern in a way that no one could object to, and for this they invented the technique for which they are best known.

Positive connotation is the team's practice of describing the symptom, and the behaviour of every member of the family around it, as positively motivated, usually as serving to preserve the family's cohesion and stability. Two features distinguish it from an ordinary compliment or a reframe. It is applied to the whole system, never to one person: if the symptom is praised but the parents are not, the parents become the villains. And it connotes positively the family's tendency to stay the same, which is exactly what allows the team then to prescribe no change. Consider the Sharma family in Delhi, whose seventeen-year-old daughter Neha has been starving herself. The father is silent and works long hours; the mother watches every mouthful Neha eats; the paternal grandmother, who lives with them, cooks rich food and complains that no one eats it. At the end of the second session the therapist returns from the team with a written message. The team, she says, is impressed by how much each member is sacrificing for the family. Neha, by not eating, keeps her mother busy and close to her at a time when the mother might otherwise feel lonely; the mother, by watching Neha, gives the father the quiet he needs after work; the father, by staying silent, avoids disagreeing with his own mother, whose cooking holds the family's traditions together; and the grandmother, by cooking, reminds everyone that the family is still one family. Because each member is doing so much to keep the family together, the team advises that no one should change anything until the next meeting in a month's time. No one has been blamed; everyone has been put on the same level; and the family has been given the problem of what to do with a symptom that is now described as an act of family loyalty.

The Milan team's other main intervention was the family ritual: a prescribed action or series of actions, with exact details of time, place, participants and words, designed to change the family's rules without discussing them. A ritual is not explained; it is performed. The best known is the ritual of odd and even days, published by the team in 1978. On odd days of the week (Monday, Wednesday and Friday) the father alone is in charge of the children, and the mother is to behave as if she were not there; on even days (Tuesday, Thursday and Saturday) the mother alone is in charge; on Sundays everyone behaves spontaneously; and each parent keeps a written record of any breach by the other. Applied to parents who undermine each other, the ritual makes the hidden competition between them visible and unworkable, and it gives the children one clear authority at a time.

Exam trap

The Milan associates are Mara Selvini Palazzoli, Luigi Boscolo, Gianfranco Cecchin and Giuliana Prata (team formed in 1971); their classic book is Paradox and Counterparadox (1975, English 1978). Positive connotation (Milan) describes the behaviour of every member and the family's wish to stay the same as positively motivated; reframing (MRI, Haley) gives any behaviour a new, more useful meaning; relabelling changes a single label. Do not confuse the Milan team with the MRI (Palo Alto), the Philadelphia Child Guidance Clinic (Minuchin) or the Milwaukee Brief Family Therapy Center (de Shazer and Berg).

Hypothesising, circularity and neutrality

In 1980 the four associates published a short paper in Family Process that became one of the most cited in the history of family therapy: "Hypothesizing, circularity, neutrality: three guidelines for the conductor of the session". By then they were relying less on dramatic prescriptions and more on the interview itself, and the paper explains how they conducted it.

Hypothesising is the formulation, by the team before each session, of a hypothesis about how the family is organised around the problem, based on what the team already knows. A Milan hypothesis has to be systemic: it must include every member of the family and explain what function the symptom serves in the family's relationships. It is neither true nor false, only more or less useful, and it guides the questions the therapist asks; as the answers come in, it is confirmed, revised or abandoned, and a new hypothesis is formed. For the Sharma family a first hypothesis might be: "Since the grandmother moved in, the father has been caught between loyalty to his mother and loyalty to his wife; Neha's not eating gives everyone something to worry about other than that divided loyalty." The therapist will then ask questions designed to test it.

Circularity is the therapist's capacity to conduct the interview on the basis of the feedback the family gives in response to the questions she asks about relationships, and therefore about difference and change. Each answer shapes the next question. The interviewing method that puts circularity into practice is circular questioning, developed especially by Boscolo and Cecchin: questions that ask family members about the relationships and differences between other members, rather than about their own feelings or about one person's symptom. Bateson had defined information as "a difference that makes a difference", and the Milan team built their questions to bring differences into view. The most characteristic form is the triadic question, in which one member is asked to comment on the relationship between two others in their presence, a kind of respectful gossip. Its effect is striking. Family members hear, often for the first time, how others see their relationships; the interview itself becomes the intervention; and the family begins to see the problem as a pattern among people rather than a defect in one of them. Table 7.10 gives the main types, applied to the Sharma family.

Table 7.10 Types of circular question, with examples from one family.
TypeWhat it asks aboutExample
TriadicOne member's view of the relationship between two others"Kunal, when Neha refuses dinner, what does your father do? And what does your mother do then?"
DifferenceDifferences between people or relationships"Who is closer to Neha, your mother or your grandmother?"
RankingAn ordering of members on one dimension"Who is most worried about Neha's eating? Who is next? Who is least?"
Before and afterChange in relationships over time"Did your parents argue more before your grandmother came to live with you, or after?"
Hypothetical or futureWhat would happen if things were different"If Neha went to college in Pune next year, which of your parents would miss her more? What would happen between them?"

Neutrality, the third guideline, was defined not as an inner attitude but as an effect. A therapist is neutral if, at the end of the session, no member of the family can say that she has sided with anyone; she may ally with one member and then another, but the net result is that she is allied with no one, and also with no particular outcome, including change itself. Neutrality protects the therapist from being drawn into the family's coalitions and keeps her curious about everyone's part in the pattern. In 1987 Cecchin revisited the idea in a paper subtitled "an invitation to curiosity", arguing that neutrality is best understood as a stance of curiosity about the many possible explanations of a family's behaviour. The Canadian psychiatrist Karl Tomm of Calgary, who did much to spread the Milan approach in North America, later classified a therapist's questions into four kinds according to their purpose: lineal (investigating, "What is the problem?"), circular (exploring patterns, "Who notices when...?"), strategic (leading the family towards a change the therapist has in mind) and reflexive (inviting the family to reflect and generate new options themselves).

Exam trap

The three Milan guidelines are hypothesising, circularity and neutrality (Selvini Palazzoli, Boscolo, Cecchin and Prata, 1980). Circular questioning (Milan, especially Boscolo and Cecchin) is an interviewing technique; circular causality (Section 7.1) is a systems idea about mutual cause and effect. Milan neutrality means siding with no one in effect; it is not Nagy's multidirected partiality (siding with everyone in turn, out of concern for fairness; Section 7.6) and not Rogers's unconditional positive regard (Section 6.2). A Milan hypothesis is judged by usefulness, not truth.

After the split: the invariant prescription and the legacy of Milan

Around 1980 the Milan team divided into two pairs. Boscolo and Cecchin turned to training and founded what became the Milan Centre for Family Therapy. Influenced by constructivism and by the idea of second-order cybernetics, which holds that the observer is always part of the system being observed, they gave up most of the dramatic prescriptions and treated the circular interview itself as the main intervention; their approach is set out in Milan Systemic Family Therapy (1987) by Boscolo, Cecchin, Lynn Hoffman and Peggy Penn. Their work fed directly into the postmodern and narrative approaches of the 1980s and 1990s (Sections 6.8 and 7.7), and into the reflecting team developed by the Norwegian psychiatrist Tom Andersen in the mid-1980s (first used in 1985 and published in 1987), in which the team behind the mirror changes places with the family and discusses its impressions openly while the family listens.

Selvini Palazzoli and Prata turned to research and went the other way, towards a single standard intervention. From the late 1970s they experimented with giving the same prescription to every family, whatever its problem, and this invariant prescription became the focus of Selvini Palazzoli's later work, reported with new colleagues in Family Games (1989). The parents are seen alone and told to keep everything said in the session secret from the rest of the family. Then, on several occasions before the next session, they are to disappear from home together without warning, leaving only a note ("We shall not be in tonight"), go somewhere they will not be recognised, and, when the children ask where they have been, reply only that it concerns the two of them. Each parent secretly writes down how each child reacts, and the notes are read out at the next session. In one reported case, a couple with three adolescent daughters, one of whom had attempted suicide, carried out the prescription after the fifth session; at the next meeting a month later the identified patient had improved, and after three more sessions with the parents alone relationships across the family were better. The aim is to draw a firm boundary around the parental couple and to break up the hidden cross-generational alliances and "dirty games" in which the children, especially the symptomatic one, have become entangled.

Table 7.11 brings the three branches of this section together.

Table 7.11 Strategic, MRI and Milan therapies compared.
FeatureStrategic (Haley, Madanes)MRI brief therapyMilan systemic
BaseWashington (earlier Palo Alto, Philadelphia)Palo AltoMilan, Italy
Key peopleJay Haley, Cloe MadanesWatzlawick, Weakland, FischSelvini Palazzoli, Boscolo, Cecchin, Prata
Why problems persistSymptoms control relationships; confused or incongruous hierarchyAttempted solutions that become the problemFamily games that the symptom preserves
Signature methodsDirectives, paradox, ordeals, pretend techniquesBlocking attempted solutions, reframing, "go slow"Positive connotation, rituals, circular questioning, invariant prescription
LengthShort; as many sessions as neededTen sessions at mostAbout ten sessions, a month apart
Who attendsUsually the familyWhoever is most concernedThe family; later often the parents alone

How should these therapies be judged? Their strengths are clear. They are brief, practical and focused on what families come for; they produced some of the most inventive techniques in all of psychotherapy; and they gave the field the team behind the one-way mirror, still standard in family therapy training. Their weaknesses are equally clear. The early literature consists mostly of vivid case reports by the therapists themselves, with few controlled trials. Directives given without explanation, and therapists who conceal their reasoning, sit uneasily with modern standards of informed consent and collaboration. And from the late 1970s feminist family therapists argued that the language of circularity and neutrality can disguise real differences of power: calling a husband's violence and a wife's fear "parts of a circular pattern" risks blaming the victim (Section 7.7 takes up the gender critique). The strategic tradition survives today mainly in evidence-based descendants, such as brief strategic family therapy for adolescent drug use, which Section 7.7 describes, and in the techniques of reframing, restraining and circular questioning, which have been absorbed by almost every school.

Indian context

Many Indian families arrive at a clinic expecting an expert to tell them what to do, and a therapist who only reflects feelings or asks open questions may seem unhelpful. Concrete directives framed in the family's own values ("for your son's sake, the two of you must decide together") often fit this expectation well, as do positive connotations that honour sacrifice and family loyalty, which are already prized. But the joint family needs care: an invariant prescription that sends the parents out of the house in secret may simply hand the children to a grandmother, and a directive that ignores the elders' place in the hierarchy will not be followed.

Exam trap

The invariant prescription (the parents' secret disappearances) is Selvini Palazzoli and Prata's, from the period after the Milan team split; Boscolo and Cecchin took the circular interview and second-order cybernetics in another direction. The reflecting team is Tom Andersen's. In any list asking which school a technique belongs to: directives and ordeals, Haley; pretending, Madanes; attempted solutions and first-order and second-order change, the MRI; positive connotation, rituals, circular questioning and neutrality, Milan.

7.6 Experiential and Contextual Family TherapiesLower yield · not asked yet

The strategic and brief therapists of Section 7.5 had little patience with feelings. Haley thought that insight was unnecessary and that talk about emotions was usually a way of avoiding change; the MRI asked only what people were doing to keep a problem alive. At the same time, and sometimes in the same buildings, another group of family therapists took almost the opposite view. For them a symptom was not a move in a power struggle but a sign that growth had been blocked. The therapist's main tool was not a clever directive but her own presence: her warmth, her spontaneity, her willingness to show what she felt. And the aim was not the removal of a symptom but a family in which every member could feel what he felt, say it, and be heard. These experiential family therapies drew on the humanistic and existential therapies of Chapter 6, especially Carl Rogers's emphasis on genuineness and congruence (Section 6.2) and the Gestalt therapists' insistence on immediate experience in the here and now (Section 6.3). Their two great figures were Virginia Satir and Carl Whitaker.

The section ends with a third approach that fits neither the strategic nor the experiential mould. Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy came from psychoanalysis, and his contextual therapy looks across generations, as Bowen's does (Section 7.3). But like Satir and Whitaker he refused to treat a family as a machine to be adjusted, and he asked a question no other school had asked: in this family, what is fair?

Virginia Satir: self-worth and the family

Virginia Satir (1916 to 1988) grew up on a farm near Neillsville, Wisconsin, trained first as a teacher and then as a social worker, and in 1951, in private practice in Chicago, saw her first family. As she later told the story, she had been treating a young woman diagnosed with schizophrenia, and when the young woman began to improve, her mother reacted with alarm; Satir invited the mother in, then the father, then the brother, and found that she could understand the young woman's difficulties only by watching all of them together. In 1959 she moved to California and joined Don Jackson and Jules Riskin in founding the MRI (Section 7.5), and from 1962 she directed its first formal training programme in family therapy. Her first book, Conjoint Family Therapy (1964), was for many years one of the most widely read introductions to the field; Peoplemaking (1972), written for ordinary families rather than professionals, became a best seller and was revised as The New Peoplemaking (1988). In 1977 she founded the Avanta Network to carry on her training work, and her approach was set out systematically after her death in The Satir Model (1991) by Satir, John Banmen, Jane Gerber and Maria Gomori.

Satir began her career at the MRI, and she never lost the Palo Alto interest in communication; some textbooks classify her with the communications therapists for this reason. But her heart was humanistic. The single idea at the centre of her work was self-worth (she also called it self-esteem): the feelings and ideas a person has about himself. In Peoplemaking she called it "the pot", after the big black iron pot that stood on the back porch of her childhood farm and was used at different times for making soap, for cooking stew and for storing manure: what matters about a pot, as about a person, is what it is filled with and how full it is. Families she worked with took up the phrase, saying they felt "high pot" or "low pot", and she argued that the most important thing a family does is to fill, or drain, its members' pots. In Conjoint Family Therapy she traced how low self-worth travels through a family. Two people with low self-worth, each hoping the other will supply what he or she lacks, marry; each is disappointed; and the child is drawn into the gap, expected to make the parents feel worthwhile. The identified patient (Section 7.1), she wrote, is the member most obviously affected by the pain of the parents' relationship, and his symptom is a signal of that pain.

In Peoplemaking Satir described four aspects of family life that every therapist should look at: the members' self-worth; their communication, the ways they work out meaning with one another; the rules they use for how they should feel and act; and their link to society, the way they relate to people and institutions outside the family. In troubled families, she wrote, self-worth is low, communication is indirect, vague and dishonest, the rules are rigid, inhuman and never discussed, and the link to society is fearful, placating or blaming. In nurturing families self-worth is high, communication is direct, clear, specific and honest, the rules are flexible, human and open to change, and the link to society is open and hopeful. The test of a family rule, for Satir, was whether it allowed what she called the "freedom to comment": whether a member could say what he saw and felt, including something about the rules themselves. She summarised what healthy families give their members as five freedoms: the freedom to see and hear what is here, instead of what should be or will be; to say what one feels and thinks, instead of what one should; to feel what one feels, instead of what one ought; to ask for what one wants, instead of always waiting for permission; and to take risks on one's own behalf, instead of always choosing to be secure.

Satir's mature model, which she called the human validation process model, rests on these ideas. Every person is born with the potential for growth; the first and most important learning takes place in the primary triad of father, mother and child, where a child learns whether he is worth something, how to communicate and how to survive; and therapy aims to raise self-worth and open the way for growth, not to cure a disease. She contrasted this growth or "seed" model with what she called the threat-and-reward model, a hierarchical way of thinking in which people are judged by whether they obey, someone is always to blame, and change is something done to people by those in charge.

Exam trap

Virginia Satir is an experiential (sometimes called humanistic or communications) family therapist. She was a co-founder of the MRI (1959) with Don Jackson and Jules Riskin, but she is not the answer to "brief strategic therapy" or "first-order and second-order change". Her central concept is self-worth (self-esteem; "the pot"); her books are Conjoint Family Therapy (1964) and Peoplemaking (1972); her model is the human validation process model. "Differentiation of self" is Bowen's (Section 7.3), not Satir's.

The five communication stances

Satir is examined above all for one idea: the ways people communicate when they are under stress and their self-worth feels threatened. In Peoplemaking she described four incongruent ways of communicating, later called survival stances, and a fifth, healthy way, and she gave each a physical posture so that families could see and feel what they were doing. In her later model each stance is defined by which of three elements a person leaves out of the communication: the self (my own feelings and needs), the other (your feelings and needs) and the context (the situation, the task, the facts). Table 7.12 sets them out.

  • Placating. The placater ignores the self. He agrees, apologises, pleases and takes the blame, whatever he really feels: "Whatever you want is fine with me; it was all my fault." Inside, Satir wrote, the placater feels like nothing, worthless without the other's approval.
  • Blaming. The blamer ignores the other. He finds fault, accuses, bosses and attacks: "You never do anything right; what is wrong with you?" Inside he feels lonely and unsuccessful, and the attack keeps others from seeing it.
  • Super-reasonable, which Satir also called computing. The super-reasonable person ignores both self and other and attends only to the context: he is correct, logical, unemotional and abstract, like a computer or a dictionary ("If one examines the situation objectively, it is clear that adolescents of this age are prone to irregular sleep"). Inside he feels vulnerable, and the calm shields him from feeling anything.
  • Irrelevant, also called distracting. The distracter ignores self, other and context altogether. Whatever he says or does has nothing to do with what anyone else is saying or doing: he changes the subject, makes a joke, fidgets, starts to tidy the room. Inside he feels that nobody cares and that there is no place for him.
  • Congruent communication, also called levelling. The congruent communicator attends to self, other and context together. Words, face, body and voice match one another and match what the person feels: "I am hurt and angry that you shouted at me in front of the children. I want us to talk about it tonight." Satir saw congruence as the goal of therapy and the hallmark of high self-worth.
Table 7.12 Satir's five communication stances.
StanceLeaves outTypical wordsSatir's posture
PlacatingSelf"Whatever you want; it is all my fault"Kneeling, one hand raised, pleading
BlamingOther"You never do anything right"Standing over others, finger pointing
Super-reasonable (computing)Self and other"Research shows that at this age..."Rigid, still, expressionless
Irrelevant (distracting)Self, other and contextChanges the subject, jokes, fidgetsOff balance, in constant motion
Congruent (levelling)Nothing"I feel hurt when you shout; I want us to talk"Balanced; words, body and tone match

Watch the stances at work in one family. The Iyers of Mysuru have come because their nineteen-year-old daughter Lakshmi has stopped eating properly and cries at night. In the first session it emerges that she wants to change her degree course from engineering to psychology. Her father, red in the face, says that she has never finished anything she started and that she is throwing away everything he worked for (blaming). Her mother says quickly that it is all her own fault for not guiding Lakshmi better, that her husband is right, and that Lakshmi will of course do whatever he wants (placating). Lakshmi's elder brother explains, in a level voice, that placement figures for engineering graduates are higher than for psychology graduates and that the family should look at the data (super-reasonable). The twelve-year-old sister asks whether anyone has seen her cricket bat (irrelevant). No one in the room has said what he or she actually feels. A Satir therapist would not argue about degree courses. She would make warm contact with each person, help each to notice the stance he takes under stress, and coach each towards a congruent statement: the father's fear that his daughter's future is not secure, the mother's fear of conflict, the brother's discomfort with emotion, the sister's distress whenever voices are raised, and Lakshmi's own wish to be taken seriously.

The stances are Satir's version of an idea that runs through this chapter. When a person's words say one thing while his face, tone or body says another, he is sending what Satir called a double-level message, a message that is incongruent. The mother who says "I'm not upset" through clenched teeth is incongruent; so is Bateson's mother in the hospital, whose words invited an embrace that her body refused (Section 7.2). Satir's congruence is close to Rogers's, but not identical: for Rogers, congruence is a quality of the therapist, a match between inner experience and outward expression that the therapist brings to the relationship (Section 6.2); for Satir it is a way of communicating that the whole family can learn.

Exam trap

Satir's five stances are placating, blaming, super-reasonable (computing), irrelevant (distracting) and congruent (levelling). Learn both names for the third and fourth: computing is the super-reasonable stance, distracting is the irrelevant one, and levelling is congruence. The four incongruent stances are survival stances, each leaving out self, other or context. An option that speaks of an "incongruent" family pattern points to Satir; Bateson's mother-and-son story is still the double bind (Section 7.2). Do not confuse the stances with Eric Berne's Parent, Adult and Child ego states (Section 3.9) or with the cognitive distortions of Beck (Chapter 5).

Satir's techniques: sculpting, family reconstruction and the life chronology

Satir's sessions were warm, active and often physical. She shook every family member's hand, children first as often as adults, got down to the eye level of small children, used touch and humour freely, and modelled congruence in everything she said. She wanted families to experience a different way of being together in the session, not to talk about one. Several of her techniques have become standard equipment for family therapists.

The family life chronology is a structured history of the family's development, gathered with the whole family present: how the parents met, what each brought from his or her own family, what they expected of marriage and of parenthood, how each child arrived, and how the children see their parents and the family's life. Satir used it to show the family, early in therapy, that its present difficulties had a history that made sense, and to find the hopes and disappointments beneath the complaints.

Family sculpting is a technique in which one member, the sculptor, arranges the others in space, placing their bodies and directing their postures and facial expressions, so that the arrangement shows how he or she experiences the family's relationships. The technique was developed in the late 1960s and early 1970s at the Boston Family Institute by David Kantor and Fred and Bunny Duhl, who described it in 1973, and Peggy Papp used a version she called family choreography; but it was Satir who made it famous, often combining it with her communication stances so that each member took up the posture of his or her own stance. The rules are simple. Closeness and distance stand for emotional closeness and distance; height stands for power (a father standing on a chair, a mother kneeling); the direction people face shows where their attention goes; and hands, arms and glances show alliances. When the sculpture is finished, each member, still in position, says how it feels to stand there; then the sculptor may be asked to make a second sculpture, showing the family as he or she would like it to be, and the family is asked what it would take to move from one to the other.

Imagine that the Iyers' twelve-year-old is asked to sculpt her family. She places her father standing on a chair, pointing down at Lakshmi, who is kneeling with her head bowed. She puts her mother beside the father, holding his arm but leaning towards Lakshmi with one hand outstretched. She puts her brother some distance away, facing a wall, and herself at the edge of the room with her hands over her ears. No one has used a word, and yet everything the family has been unable to say is in the room. Sculpting is especially useful with children, with members who are not fluent talkers, and with families in which some members, such as a young daughter-in-law in a joint family, cannot easily criticise others aloud.

Family reconstruction, which Satir developed and used mainly in training groups and workshops, takes sculpting further back in time. One person, the star (sometimes called the explorer), works with a guide (Satir herself, or a trained leader) to re-enact the history of his or her family over three generations. Before the event the star prepares a family map of the three generations, a family life chronology and a "wheel of influence" showing the important people in his or her life. Group members are then chosen to play the star's parents, grandparents and other relatives, and key scenes are brought to life: how the parents met, what their own childhoods were like, the star's birth, the crises that shaped the family. The purpose is threefold: to help the star see where his or her ways of feeling and coping were learnt, to see the parents as people with their own histories rather than only as parents, and to free the star to make new choices. Family reconstruction resembles psychodrama, which Section 7.11 describes, and it also draws on the guided fantasy and role-play of the human potential movement.

Satir used other structured exercises too. In a parts party, group members play the different inner parts of one person (her generosity, her jealousy, her sense of humour, her anger), the parts are allowed to interact and quarrel at a "party", and the person is helped to accept and integrate them. In a temperature reading, a family or a group regularly shares appreciations, new information, puzzles, complaints with recommendations, and hopes and wishes, a way of keeping communication open. And in her later model she used the image of an iceberg: visible behaviour is only the tip, beneath which lie coping stances, feelings, feelings about feelings, perceptions, expectations, yearnings and, at the base, the self.

Clinical link

Satir's methods have rarely been tested in controlled trials, but they live on in training and in practice. A short family sculpture is one of the fastest ways to assess a family's alliances without asking anyone to accuse anyone else, and asking each member "What do you feel, standing there?" turns assessment into intervention. Teaching the stances to a couple in conflict ("When you placate, he hears nothing about you; when you blame, she hears nothing about her") gives them a shared, non-blaming language for their quarrels. Emotionally focused couple therapy (Section 7.8) carries the experiential emphasis on primary emotion into work with couples.

Exam trap

Family sculpting, the family life chronology, family reconstruction, the parts party and the temperature reading are Satir's (sculpting was developed with Kantor and the Duhls, and Papp used it as "choreography"). The genogram is Bowen's three-generation diagram (Section 7.3); the family map of boundaries and subsystems is Minuchin's (Section 7.4); the sociogram is Moreno's map of group relationships (Section 7.11). A Satir-style re-enactment is not a psychodrama, although the two are close relatives.

Carl Whitaker's symbolic-experiential therapy

If Satir was family therapy's warmest presence, Carl Whitaker (1912 to 1995) was its most irreverent. He qualified in medicine at Syracuse University in 1936 and trained in obstetrics and gynaecology before turning, in 1938, to work in a psychiatric hospital, where he became interested in schizophrenia. During the Second World War he counselled workers at Oak Ridge, Tennessee, the secret site of the atomic bomb project, and it was there that he began working with a second therapist in the room. From 1946 he chaired the department of psychiatry at Emory University in Atlanta, where he and his colleagues treated patients with schizophrenia and, increasingly, their families; he described this early work with Thomas Malone in The Roots of Psychotherapy (1953). From 1965 until he retired in 1982 he was a professor of psychiatry at the University of Wisconsin Medical School in Madison. His best-known book, The Family Crucible (1978), written with his co-therapist Augustus Napier, tells the story of one family's therapy in the form of a novel; Dancing with the Family (1988), with William Bumberry, describes his symbolic-experiential approach in his later years.

Whitaker called his approach symbolic-experiential family therapy. "Experiential" meant that families change through emotional experience in the session, not through understanding or instruction. "Symbolic" meant that the therapist works with the family's inner world: the private meanings, impulses, fantasies and unspoken rules that lie beneath its words, which Whitaker believed were more powerful than anything said aloud. He distrusted theory. In a 1976 paper on the hindrance of theory in clinical work he argued that theory lets the therapist hide behind technique instead of meeting the family as a person; his own "method" was to use himself, his free associations, his boredom, his irritation, his playfulness, and to let the family affect him as much as he affected it. He spoke openly of using his countertransference, and he insisted that a good therapy changes the therapist too. The goal was growth: a family whose members had both a stronger sense of belonging and greater freedom to be separate, and in which each member could be more spontaneous, more playful and, in Whitaker's word, more "crazy", by which he meant creative rather than disturbed. Symptoms, he suggested, were often failed efforts to grow.

Whitaker almost always worked with a co-therapist, and he made co-therapy, two therapists working together with the same family, a hallmark of experiential work. He gave several reasons. A family is a powerful system that can absorb a lone therapist, drawing him into its coalitions and its way of seeing things; a partner can pull him out. With two therapists, one can move in close, become involved, even act "crazy", while the other stays outside and keeps the session safe. And the two therapists model a relationship in which people can disagree, take turns at leading and following, and stay connected, which is exactly what many families cannot do.

Napier and Whitaker described therapy as a contest with two sides. In the battle for structure, which the therapist must win, the family is required to accept the therapist's terms for therapy: who will attend, when and how. Whitaker would not begin until the whole family came, often including grandparents and other important figures, and he would refuse to see a family that insisted on sending only the "sick" child. In the battle for initiative, which the family must win, the family must take responsibility for its own life and for whether and how it changes. The therapist will not push, persuade or rescue, and a family that waits for the therapist to tell it what to do finds itself facing silence. A therapist who loses the first battle cannot help the family; a therapist who wins the second has taken over a responsibility that belongs to the family.

Key study: The Family Crucible (Napier and Whitaker, 1978)

The Brice family came to Napier and Whitaker because of their sixteen-year-old daughter Claudia, whose angry battles with her mother and late nights out had become unmanageable. The parents, David and Carolyn, expected the therapists to fix Claudia. The therapists insisted that the whole family attend, including the younger children, and won the battle for structure. Over the following months the focus moved, as it so often does in family therapy, from Claudia to the tension in her parents' marriage, which Claudia's fights with her mother had both expressed and concealed. Along the way the therapists kept nudging the parents, David especially, away from a calm, rational and authoritarian stance and towards saying what they actually felt, so that the content of the quarrels gave way to the experience beneath them. The book shows the method at its best (spontaneous, intuitive, deeply involved) and its limits: it is a single family described by its own therapists, and it would be hard to teach anyone to do what Whitaker did.

Whitaker and his colleague David Keith (1981) described three phases of therapy. The beginning is the battle for structure and initiative. In the middle phase the therapists work actively and emotionally, bringing in extended family, confronting, exaggerating and playing. In the ending phase the family and the therapists separate, with some sadness and anxiety on both sides, and the family leaves with the initiative fully its own. Among the moves Whitaker and Keith listed were redefining symptoms as efforts for growth, offering fantasy alternatives to real-life stress, augmenting the despair of a family member so that the family has to respond to it, affective confrontation, and insisting that children be treated as children rather than as their parents' peers.

Whitaker's most distinctive move was what he called the psychotherapy of the absurd, described in a 1975 paper in Family Process. The therapist takes a family's complaint or rigid belief and, instead of disputing it, pushes it further and further until its absurdity becomes obvious. Suppose a father says, in front of his family, "This boy will be the death of me." A Whitaker-style reply might be: "How long do you think you have? Should we start planning the funeral? Who will look after your wife when you are gone, the boy?" The family laughs, the father finds himself defending his own survival, and the deadly seriousness of the complaint is loosened. Absurdity of this kind is related to the strategic therapists' paradox (Section 7.5), but its purpose is different: it is not a calculated manoeuvre to block a sequence but a spontaneous, playful way of making an experience vivid and widening the family's tolerance for anxiety and for each other.

Exam trap

Carl Whitaker is symbolic-experiential family therapy: co-therapy, the battle for structure (the therapist must win) and the battle for initiative (the family must win), the psychotherapy of the absurd (1975), anti-theory, the therapist's own spontaneity and countertransference, and The Family Crucible (1978, with Augustus Napier). "Experiential family therapy" in an option list means Satir and Whitaker; "strategic" means Haley and Madanes; "structural" means Minuchin; "multigenerational" means Bowen. Joining is Minuchin's term (Section 7.4), not Whitaker's.

Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy's contextual therapy

Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy (1920 to 2007) was born in Budapest, trained in psychiatry in Hungary and emigrated to the United States in 1950. He was a psychoanalytically trained psychiatrist who worked with patients with schizophrenia, and from 1957 he worked at the Eastern Pennsylvania Psychiatric Institute in Philadelphia, first leading research on schizophrenia and then directing its department of family psychiatry, one of the early centres of family research. With the psychologist James Framo he edited Intensive Family Therapy (1965), one of the first textbooks of the field. His own approach was set out in Invisible Loyalties: Reciprocity in Intergenerational Family Therapy (1973), written with the social worker Geraldine Spark, and later in Between Give and Take (1986), written with Barbara Krasner, and Foundations of Contextual Therapy (1987). He called it contextual therapy, because a person can be understood only in the context of his relationships, and those relationships stretch across generations and carry obligations.

Boszormenyi-Nagy described four dimensions of what he called relational reality, each of which a therapist should keep in view. The first is facts: the given circumstances of a life, such as genetic inheritance, illness, adoption, migration, poverty, a death or a divorce. The second is individual psychology: each person's needs, motives, fantasies and defences, the territory of psychoanalysis. The third is systemic transactions: the patterns of power, alliance, rules and communication that the other schools of family therapy study. The fourth, and for him the most important and most neglected, is relational ethics: the balance of fairness, trust and give-and-take between family members over time. He later added a fifth, ontic dimension, concerned with the way persons come into being through their relationships. Contextual therapy did not reject the other three dimensions; it insisted that no family can be understood, or helped, if the fourth is ignored.

To describe relational ethics Boszormenyi-Nagy used the language of accounting. Every family keeps an invisible ledger, a multigenerational account of merits and debts: what each member has given to the others, what each has received, and therefore who owes what to whom. A person earns merit by giving due care and consideration to others; he incurs indebtedness by receiving. Some debts are built into existence. Parents owe their children care simply because they brought them into the world, and children owe their parents a basic loyalty and, later, consideration in return for life and care. When the ledger is roughly balanced over time, relationships are trustworthy. When it is badly out of balance, someone is always owed and someone always owing, and trust breaks down.

The merit a person has earned is his entitlement: an ethical credit that gives him freedom and a sense of worth. Entitlement earned by giving fairly is constructive; it is the reason a person who has cared well for an ageing parent can feel at peace. But entitlement can also be earned by being wronged, and this is where the trouble starts. A child who was exploited, neglected or made to carry burdens beyond his age acquires destructive entitlement: he is genuinely owed, but the people who owe him cannot or will not pay, and so he feels justified in taking from others, often from people who never harmed him, such as a spouse or his own children. Boszormenyi-Nagy called the result the revolving slate: the injustice suffered in one generation is passed on to an innocent party in the next, who acquires destructive entitlement in turn, and so on down the generations (Figure 7.8).

The concept that gave Boszormenyi-Nagy's book its title is loyalty. In his sense, loyalty is not a feeling but a structure: a preferential commitment to one's family of origin, rooted in the ledger, that binds members to each other across generations. Much of it is hidden. An invisible loyalty is an unacknowledged obligation to the family of origin that shapes a person's behaviour without his knowing it, often at the cost of his present relationships. A young woman repeatedly sabotages her own promotions, out of loyalty to a mother who was never allowed to succeed; a son cannot commit himself to his wife, because to put her first would feel like abandoning a lonely father; a couple quarrel endlessly about money, each loyal to the rules of a different family. What looks like a symptom, or like plain bad behaviour, may be an act of loyalty, and it cannot be given up until the loyalty is recognised.

Two more of Boszormenyi-Nagy's concepts appear often in examinations. Split loyalty describes the position of a child whose parents have made loyalty to one of them a betrayal of the other, as commonly happens in bitter marriages and divorces: whatever the child does for one parent is an injury to the other, and the child is forced into an impossible choice that Boszormenyi-Nagy regarded as one of the most damaging situations a child can face. Parentification, a term he and Spark made widely known, is the process by which a child is placed in the role of parent to his own parent or siblings, giving care, comfort or management far beyond his years. Some parentification is ordinary, and even a source of constructive entitlement; but when the child's giving is unacknowledged and unrepaid, it becomes the raw material of destructive entitlement and the revolving slate. Every person also inherits a legacy: the expectations that arise simply from being born into a particular family, such as caring for one's parents, continuing a family's work or making good its losses.

What does the contextual therapist do? Her central method is multidirected partiality: she sides with each member of the family in turn, crediting each person's contributions and acknowledging each person's injuries, including those of members who are absent, dead or not yet born. This is quite different from Milan neutrality (Section 7.5). The Milan therapist ensures that she is allied with no one; the contextual therapist is allied, actively and openly, with everyone, and she also holds each member accountable for the consequences of his actions for the others. Her aims are to rebalance the ledger, to restore trustworthiness, and to help members re-engage with one another in fairer ways, a process Boszormenyi-Nagy called rejunction. One of the most powerful steps on the way is exoneration: lifting the burden of blame from a parent by coming to understand the parent's own history of unfair treatment and the constraints under which he or she acted. Exoneration is not the same as forgiveness, which implies that a wrong has been excused; it is a recognition that the parent, too, was owed and unpaid, and it frees the adult child from the need to keep collecting.

Push a case through. Sunita, aged 48, comes to a clinic in Pune with depression. She is the eldest of five children; when her mother fell ill, Sunita, then twelve, left school to raise her siblings and run the house, and no one ever thanked her. Now she demands constant attention from her married daughter Nandini, who telephones three times a day, visits every evening and is quarrelling with her husband because of it. A strategic therapist might look for the sequence that keeps the quarrels going; a contextual therapist looks at the ledger. She credits Sunita's childhood giving, explicitly and repeatedly, in Nandini's presence: "You gave your whole childhood to your family, and no one has ever said so." She credits Nandini's loyalty to her mother, and acknowledges the cost to Nandini's marriage and to the husband, who is also entitled to consideration. She helps Sunita see her own parents' situation (a sick mother, a poor and overwhelmed father) so that she can begin to exonerate them, and she helps Nandini find ways of giving to her mother that are fair to her husband too. The goal is not to cut Nandini off from her mother but to stop the slate from revolving onto a third generation.

Contextual therapy has a close relative in the family-of-origin therapy of James Framo, who worked with Boszormenyi-Nagy in Philadelphia. Framo held that adults' marital difficulties are often attempts to repair, through the spouse, relationships left unfinished with their own parents, and he invited adult clients to bring their parents and siblings to one or two long sessions to address those relationships directly. Bowen's coaching of clients to return to their families of origin (Section 7.3) is a third member of the same transgenerational family.

Indian context

Indian clients often already speak the language of the ledger. Classical Hindu thought holds that every person is born owing debts (rina), among them a debt to one's ancestors (pitri rina), and many families talk openly about what parents have given, what children owe and what a son, a daughter or a daughter-in-law has or has not "done for" the family. Contextual therapy gives this vocabulary a therapeutic use: crediting giving that has gone unacknowledged (very often a woman's caregiving), and helping an adult son or daughter meet real obligations to ageing parents without passing the cost on to a spouse or to the next generation. It also guards against a risk in the opposite direction, that a Western-trained therapist dismisses filial obligation as "enmeshment" when, by the family's own ethics, it is simply fairness.

Exam trap

Contextual therapy is Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy's; Invisible Loyalties (1973) was written with Geraldine Spark. Its vocabulary is ethical and intergenerational: relational ethics, the ledger of merit and indebtedness, entitlement (constructive and destructive), the revolving slate, invisible loyalties, split loyalty, parentification, legacy, exoneration and the therapist's multidirected partiality. It is a transgenerational (intergenerational) therapy, like Bowen's and Framo's. "Emotional cutoff" and "multigenerational transmission" are Bowen's terms (Section 7.3), not Nagy's; and multidirected partiality (siding with everyone) is not Milan neutrality (siding with no one).

The schools of family therapy side by side

You have now met most of the schools of family therapy, and examiners love to test them as matching questions: a name, a technique or a key word, and four schools to choose from. Table 7.13 gathers them in one place, with pointers to the sections where each is taught. In practice the boundaries have blurred. Most family therapists today are integrative, borrowing the genogram from Bowen, joining and enactment from Minuchin, reframing from the MRI, circular questions from Milan, sculpting from Satir and multidirected partiality from Boszormenyi-Nagy, and choosing among evidence-based programmes for particular problems (Section 7.7). But the schools remain the best way to understand where each idea came from, and why it works.

Exam trap

Match the words, not the names alone. "Subsystems, boundaries (rigid, clear, diffuse) and hierarchies" is structural (Minuchin). "Differentiation of self, triangulation, multigenerational transmission" is Bowen. "Strategic paradox and prescribing the symptom" is Haley, Madanes and the MRI. "Communication stances" and "sculpting" are Satir. "Invisible loyalties" and "ledger" are Boszormenyi-Nagy. A statement that individuals, families and cultures are creators of their realities is social constructionism, the basis of narrative therapy (Section 7.7), not strategic, structural or transgenerational therapy. "Transgenerational" in an option means Bowen, Framo or Boszormenyi-Nagy.
