7.7 Behavioural, Postmodern and Evidence-Based Family WorkMedium yield · 2 Qs

The schools you met in Sections 7.3 to 7.6 were built by gifted clinicians who watched families, formed bold theories and passed them on through demonstration and live supervision. Bowen, Minuchin, Haley, the Milan team and Satir rarely tested their methods in controlled trials, and each was confident that a skilled therapist could see the family's pattern more clearly than the family could. From the 1970s that confidence came under attack from two opposite directions. Behaviour therapists asked for data: define the problem behaviour, count it, change its consequences and show that it changed. Postmodern therapists asked a more unsettling question: what gives the therapist the right to decide what the family's "real" pattern is? Out of both challenges, and out of a new demand from courts and health services for proof that treatment works, came the family programmes that are funded today. This section follows those three developments and then turns to the questions about gender, power and culture that every family therapist now has to answer.

Behavioural family therapy: the family as an exchange of reinforcers

Behavioural family therapy applies the principles of learning (operant conditioning, social learning and, later, cognitive change) to the interactions between family members. It has two roots. The first is Oregon, where Gerald R. Patterson (1926 to 2016) and his colleagues sent observers into the homes of aggressive boys and described the coercive cycle, in which a child's escalation and a parent's giving in negatively reinforce each other; Patterson's parent management training taught parents to break the cycle. Chapter 4 (Section 4.10) tells that story in full, together with the skills of parent training, so here you need only its lesson for family therapy: a child's problem behaviour is kept going by what the other members of the family do, and the people best placed to change it are those same family members.

The second root is the work of Richard B. Stuart, who in 1969 published "Operant-interpersonal treatment for marital discord". Stuart's starting point was that partners in a distressed marriage each want something from the other and each withholds it until the other gives first. His treatment made the exchange explicit: each partner's most wanted change was tied to the other's, and both were rewarded for delivering. He soon took the same logic to the families of delinquent adolescents, where parents and teenagers negotiated written contracts that traded privileges for responsibilities (Section 4.5 defines the contingency contract; couples work is taken up in Section 7.8). In 1970 the psychiatrist Robert Paul Liberman published "Behavioral approaches to family and couple therapy", arguing that many symptoms are maintained by the attention that relatives give them, and that the family therapist's job is to help relatives move their attention from maladaptive to adaptive behaviour.

Behind these methods lies a view of family life borrowed from social psychology. In social exchange theory, set out for small groups by John Thibaut and Harold Kelley in The Social Psychology of Groups (1959), people stay in a relationship to the extent that its rewards outweigh its costs, compared with the alternatives open to them. Family members are constantly exchanging rewards (praise, help, affection, company) and costs (criticism, nagging, sulking, refusals). The exchanges tend toward reciprocity: people return the kind of behaviour they receive, kindness for kindness and insult for insult. Observational studies found that distressed families exchange fewer pleasant behaviours and more aversive ones than contented families, and that in distressed families an aversive act is more likely to be answered at once with another. To a behavioural family therapist, then, a troubled family is one whose members have drifted into a low rate of mutual reward and a high rate of mutual punishment.

Treatment therefore begins with a functional analysis of family interaction: a precise description of the target behaviours of each member, what comes before them, what follows them, and who reinforces whom. It is the ABC analysis of Chapter 4 stretched across several people, so that the consequence of one person's act is the antecedent of the next person's. Consider the family of Aditya, a fifteen-year-old in Bengaluru whose parents say he is "addicted to his phone" and "disrespectful". A week of recording shows that the trouble comes almost every evening at the same point (Figure 7.9). His mother tells him to put the phone away and start his homework; Aditya argues and shouts; his father, tired after work and wanting peace, says "Leave him, he is tired"; Aditya keeps the phone and the shouting stops; his mother goes quiet, angry with her husband. Every step is being reinforced. Aditya's arguing pays, because the demand disappears. His father's rescue pays, because the noise stops at once. And his mother's requests are punished, because she is overruled in front of her son.

Notice what this analysis reveals and what an individual analysis of Aditya would miss. Early behaviour therapists were criticised by systems therapists for thinking in straight lines (the parent's behaviour shapes the child's) and for working with one dyad at a time, usually mother and child. A parent-training plan given to Aditya's mother alone would fail, because his father's rescue would keep reinforcing the arguing. Behavioural family therapists absorbed the criticism. The plan for Aditya's family starts with the parents: they meet without him, agree on a rule about the phone and homework, and agree that neither will overrule the other in front of him, which is a structural therapist's goal (Section 7.4) reached by behavioural means. Only then is the rule put to Aditya, with a contract that makes the evening phone time something he earns.

Remember

The parenting that behavioural programmes try to build is what the developmental psychologist Diana Baumrind called authoritative: high warmth and responsiveness combined with firm, consistent and reasoned control that supports the child's growing autonomy. Her authoritarian style is high in demands and low in warmth (obedience without explanation), and her permissive style is warm with few demands; Eleanor Maccoby and John Martin (1983) added a fourth, uninvolved or neglectful parenting, low on both. The styles themselves belong to the Developmental book.

Exam trap

Behavioural family therapy grew from Gerald Patterson (Oregon, the coercive cycle, parent management training), Richard Stuart (contingency contracting with couples from 1969 and with delinquents' families) and Robert Liberman (1970). Its theory of relationships is social exchange theory (Thibaut and Kelley, 1959): rewards against costs, with reciprocity of positive and negative behaviour. The coercive cycle runs on negative reinforcement for both child and parent (Section 4.10). Do not confuse a behavioural functional analysis (antecedents and consequences of behaviour) with functional family therapy, a separate programme met later in this section.

Contracts, communication training and problem solving

Three tools do most of the work in behavioural family therapy, and they are summarised in Table 7.14. The first is the contract. Family therapists distinguish two kinds. In a quid pro quo contract one person's change is made conditional on another's: "If Aditya is off the phone by eight on school nights, he may take the scooter on Saturday afternoon"; "If his father stops overruling his mother, she will stop complaining to her own sister about him." (The Latin phrase, "something for something", had entered family therapy earlier, through Don Jackson's 1965 paper "Family rules: marital quid pro quo", which used it to describe the unspoken bargain at the heart of every marriage; the behaviourists made the bargain explicit and wrote it down.) Quid pro quo contracts have a weakness: each person can wait for the other to go first, and when one fails, the other's obligation collapses too. In a good faith contract (also called a parallel contract) each person agrees to change independently and earns his or her own reward, so one person's lapse does not cancel the other's commitment. Aditya earns his data top-up by finishing homework; his father earns an evening out of his choice by not intervening for a week.

The second tool is communication training. You met its content in Section 7.2: Ian Falloon's behavioural family management taught families of people with schizophrenia to express positive feelings, make positive requests, express negative feelings constructively and listen actively. What makes it behavioural is the method of teaching. The therapist explains the skill, models it, asks family members to rehearse it on a real issue in the session, gives immediate and specific feedback, and sets homework. A mother who says "You never listen, you are just like your father" is helped to say "I feel worried when you are on the phone at midnight; I would like you to hand it to me at eleven." The second statement names a feeling, a behaviour and a request, and it leaves out the blame and the comparison that would start the next round of shouting.

The third tool is problem-solving training. Falloon's families learned a six-step method that is still the standard: (1) pinpoint the problem or goal as precisely as possible; (2) list every possible solution without judging any of them; (3) go through the likely advantages and disadvantages of each; (4) agree on the best solution or combination; (5) plan exactly how to carry it out, who does what and when; and (6) review the result at the next meeting and praise every effort. Families are encouraged to hold a short weekly family meeting for the purpose, with one member chairing and another writing down what was agreed. Working with parents and teenagers, Arthur Robin and Sharon Foster combined problem solving and communication training with the cognitive restructuring of extreme beliefs in Negotiating Parent-Adolescent Conflict (1989). A father who believes "if I let her go to the college festival she will ruin her life" (ruination), or a daughter who believes "I should be allowed to decide everything myself" (autonomy), will not solve problems well until those beliefs are softened. The addition of thoughts to behaviour produced cognitive-behavioural family therapy, developed by Norman Epstein, Frank Dattilio and others, which works with the shared assumptions, or family schemas, through which members interpret one another's actions.

Table 7.14 The main techniques of behavioural family therapy.
TechniqueWhat the family doesExample
Functional analysis of family interactionRecords each member's target behaviour, what precedes and follows it, and who reinforces whomNoting who steps in when the evening argument starts
Increasing positive exchangesEach member deliberately does small pleasing things for the others and notices those done for himA week of "catch each other being good"
Quid pro quo contractOne person's change is traded for another'sScooter on Saturday if the phone is off by eight on school nights
Good faith (parallel) contractEach person changes independently and earns his own rewardAditya earns data; his father earns an evening out
Communication trainingLearns positive requests, feelings without blame and active listening, by modelling, rehearsal and feedback"I feel worried when you are late; please message me"
Problem-solving trainingUses six fixed steps at a weekly family meetingAgreeing phone rules at the Sunday meeting

The great strength of behavioural family therapy is that it can be written into a manual, taught to ordinary clinicians and tested, and most family programmes with strong evidence today contain its ingredients: tracking behaviour, rewarding small improvements, contracts, communication and problem solving. Its weakness, in its critics' eyes, was that it treated a family as a set of individual learners rather than a system with loyalties and alliances; a contract will not hold while a grandmother quietly sides with the child against his mother. The programmes that later proved most effective joined behavioural methods to a systemic understanding of why families resist change.

Exam trap

In a quid pro quo contract each change depends on the other's ("you do this, I do that"); in a good faith or parallel contract each person changes independently for his own reward. The six problem-solving steps (pinpoint, brainstorm, evaluate, choose, plan, review) and the four communication skills belong to Ian Falloon's behavioural family management (Section 7.2). Problem-solving communication training for parent and adolescent conflict is Robin and Foster's (1989). Behavioural family therapy teaches skills by instruction, modelling, rehearsal, feedback and homework; it does not use paradoxical directives, which belong to the strategic school (Section 7.5).

The postmodern turn: from expert to collaborator

At the same time as behaviourists were demanding more precision, a different group of family therapists began to doubt that precision about families was possible at all. Their doubts came from philosophy, and you met the core of them in Section 6.8. The first generation of family therapists were, in the language of philosophers, modernists: they believed that a family has a real structure or pattern that an expert observer can discover and then correct. The structural therapist maps the family's real boundaries; the strategic therapist designs a directive that the family need not understand; the Milan team forms hypotheses behind a one-way mirror and delivers its verdict. In each case the therapist knows something about the family that the family does not.

The first crack came from within cybernetics itself. The Austrian-born physicist Heinz von Foerster (1911 to 2002) called for a "cybernetics of cybernetics", now usually called second-order cybernetics: the study of systems that includes the observer as part of what is observed. A therapist cannot stand outside a family and describe it neutrally, like an engineer inspecting a thermostat; the moment she walks in she becomes part of a new system, and her description of the family says as much about her own theories as about the family. The Chilean biologist Humberto Maturana (1928 to 2021) added that a living system can be disturbed from outside but never instructed to change in a particular way, and the family therapist Lynn Hoffman, in a 1985 essay titled "Beyond power and control: toward a 'second order' family systems therapy", urged colleagues to stop trying to control families and to work with them instead.

The second influence was social constructionism, the view that what people take to be reality, including their problems and identities, is built in language and in relationships within a culture (Section 6.8 contrasts it with George Kelly's constructivism, which stresses the individual mind). The American social psychologist Kenneth Gergen did much to bring it into psychology with his 1985 article "The social constructionist movement in modern psychology". For family therapists the consequences were radical. A diagnosis such as "enmeshed family" or "schizophrenogenic mother" stops being a discovery and becomes one more story, often a harmful one, told by an expert. The therapist's task changes from finding the truth about a family to helping its members create new and more useful meanings together. Table 7.15 sets the two generations side by side.

Table 7.15 The first-generation systemic schools and the postmodern approaches compared.
FeatureSystemic schools (structural, strategic, Milan)Postmodern approaches (collaborative, reflecting, narrative, solution-focused)
Therapist's positionExpert who assesses and intervenesCollaborator; curious and "not-knowing"
What a problem isA product of family structure or interactionA meaning or story made in language and culture
Who is in the systemThe family, defined by roles and hierarchyEveryone in conversation about the problem
Main toolsDirectives, restructuring, paradox, hypothesesQuestions, dialogue, open reflections, re-authoring
The team behind the mirrorConfers in private and sends a messageReflects aloud while the family listens
CyberneticsFirst order: the observer stands outsideSecond order: the observer is part of the system
Exam trap

Second-order cybernetics (Heinz von Foerster's "cybernetics of cybernetics": the observer is part of the observed system) is not the same as second-order change (the Mental Research Institute's term for a change in the rules of a system rather than within them, Section 7.5). Social constructionism (Gergen: meanings are built socially, in language and culture) is not the same as constructivism (George Kelly, Humberto Maturana, Ernst von Glasersfeld: each mind or organism builds its own reality). Postmodern family therapy grew out of both.

Conversations and reflections: Anderson, Goolishian and Andersen

The clearest statement of the postmodern position in family therapy came from Galveston, Texas. Harold Goolishian (1924 to 1991), known to everyone as Harry, had worked since the 1950s at the University of Texas Medical Branch in Galveston, and with Harlene Anderson and others he founded the Galveston Family Institute (later the Houston Galveston Institute). Working with families that had defeated every other treatment, they grew suspicious of the field's confident theories, and in 1988 they set out an alternative in a Family Process paper titled "Human systems as linguistic systems". Its central claim reversed one of the oldest ideas in family therapy. The system that matters in therapy, they argued, is not the family defined by its roles and structure; it is the group of people who are "in language" about a problem: the family, but also the teacher who sent the complaint, the probation officer, the psychiatrist, the worried uncle, and the therapist. The family does not create the problem; the problem creates this system. They called it a problem-organising, problem-dis-solving system, and the idea is usually referred to as the problem-determined system. Problems, on this view, are not solved so much as dissolved, when the people talking about them come to new understandings in which the problem no longer exists in the same form.

From this followed the therapist's stance. In their 1992 chapter "The client is the expert" Anderson and Goolishian described the not-knowing stance: the therapist approaches every family with genuine curiosity, assumes that she does not already know what their words mean or what their situation is like, and lets her questions arise from what has just been said rather than from a theory. The therapist is an expert in creating a space for dialogue, not an expert on the content of the family's life. The result is the collaborative language systems approach, which Anderson later called simply collaborative therapy. A session looks like an unusually respectful conversation: there are no directives, no hidden hypotheses and no interpretations delivered from above, and the therapist is as willing to be changed by the conversation as the family is.

A second innovation came from the far north of Norway. Tom Andersen (1936 to 2007) was a psychiatrist who became professor of social psychiatry at the University of Tromsø. In 1985 he and his colleagues were working in the Milan style, with a team behind a one-way mirror that conferred in private and then sent the family its conclusions. Andersen grew uncomfortable with the arrangement, which made the team's thinking invisible and its conclusions sound like verdicts. One day the team simply asked the family whether they would like to listen to the team's discussion. The result, which Andersen described in a 1987 Family Process paper and in his book The Reflecting Team (1991), is now used all over the world.

In the reflecting team the roles of watcher and watched are reversed (Figure 7.10). First the interviewer talks with the family while the team watches from behind the mirror. Then, with the family's permission, the lights and sound are switched, and the family and interviewer watch and listen while the team members talk among themselves about what they heard. Finally the family and interviewer talk about what struck them in the team's conversation. The cycle may be repeated once or several times in a session. The reflections follow strict rules. Team members speak tentatively ("I wondered whether...", "Perhaps..."), talk to one another rather than lecturing the family, comment only on what was actually said in the room, offer several possibilities rather than one verdict, and avoid criticism. Andersen held that new ideas help a stuck system only when they are "appropriately unusual": too familiar and they make no difference, too strange and the family closes up.

Andersen's ideas travelled furthest in Finland, where from the 1980s Jaakko Seikkula and his colleagues in Western Lapland developed open dialogue: in a psychiatric crisis, especially a first episode of psychosis, a team meets the person together with his family and social network within a day, usually at home, and every discussion and treatment decision takes place in that network meeting. Its reported outcomes are striking but come mainly from naturalistic follow-up studies rather than randomised trials.

Exam trap

Two similar names, two different contributions. Harlene Anderson (with Harry Goolishian, Galveston) gave us the collaborative language systems approach, the problem-determined system and the not-knowing stance. Tom Andersen (Tromsø, Norway) gave us the reflecting team. Not-knowing (genuine curiosity, the client as expert) is not the Milan team's neutrality (not siding with any member or any one view, Section 7.5), and a reflecting team, which talks openly in front of the family, is not the Milan team, which confers in private and delivers a message or prescription.

Narrative and solution-focused work with whole families

Narrative therapy and solution-focused brief therapy were both born in family therapy, although Chapter 6 (Section 6.8) taught them as approaches to individual clients. That section explained the techniques: externalising the problem, relative influence questions, unique outcomes, re-authoring and outsider witnesses in narrative therapy; the miracle, exception, scaling and coping questions, compliments and the formula first session task in solution-focused therapy. Here the question is what changes when these methods are used with a whole family in the room.

Narrative family therapy, developed by Michael White (1948 to 2008) in Adelaide and David Epston in Auckland, treats a family as a group of people who live by stories: stories about who each of them is, what the family is like and what has gone wrong. Those stories are not invented privately. They are shaped by the dominant stories of the surrounding culture (about what a good son, a good mother or a successful family should be), and following the philosopher Michel Foucault, White argued that these cultural stories carry power: they decide whose account of a family counts and which members are judged as failures. Narrative therapy therefore gives individuals, families and whole cultures an active part as the creators of their realities, with different degrees of power to author or impose a story, and it invites families to examine where their problem-saturated stories came from. This is the point of contrast with the earlier schools. A Bowenian, structural or strategic therapist looks for something wrong in the family's emotional process, structure or hierarchy; a narrative therapist rejects the idea that the symptom serves a function for the family, and sees the family as a group that the problem has recruited and divided.

The family version of externalising makes this concrete. Imagine a joint family in Mysuru: nine-year-old Ishaan, his parents and his paternal grandmother, who come because Ishaan has violent tantrums and everyone blames someone. The father blames the grandmother for spoiling him, the grandmother blames the mother for working, and the mother blames herself. The therapist invites the family to name the problem, and Ishaan calls it "the Volcano". She then asks relative influence questions of every member in turn. How does the Volcano get Ishaan into trouble at school? What has it done to the father's evenings, to the grandmother's sleep, to the mother's confidence? Then the second set: has anyone ever stopped the Volcano from erupting? The grandmother remembers that it calms when she tells him a story; the mother remembers a Sunday when Ishaan warned her that it was coming and went to his room. Each of these unique outcomes is now a family achievement, and each member hears the others describe it. The blame that was flowing between the adults is redirected at a shared opponent, and the family leaves with a plan to "outwit the Volcano together" and a letter from the therapist summarising what each of them said. Family members also serve as audience and outsider witnesses for one another's preferred stories, which is easier to arrange in a family session than in individual work.

Solution-focused family therapy grew up at the Brief Family Therapy Center in Milwaukee, founded in 1978 by Steve de Shazer (1940 to 2005) and Insoo Kim Berg (1934 to 2007) as a family therapy centre. With several people present, the standard questions take on new uses. The miracle question is asked of each member, and the therapist listens for overlap ("So all three of you would notice that the morning starts without shouting"). Relationship questions ask people to describe change through someone else's eyes: "What would your mother notice you doing differently the day after the miracle?" "If I asked your grandmother what is better this week, what would she say?" They bring the other members' perspectives into every answer, rather as the Milan school's circular questions do (Section 7.5). Scaling questions can be put to every member ("Where would each of you put the family today, from 0 to 10?"), and differences between the numbers become something to explore rather than argue about. Because family members often stand in different relationships to change, the therapist may treat a reluctant teenager as a visitor and his worried mother as a customer, complimenting each for coming. Berg carried the approach into home-based child welfare work in her book Family Based Services (1994), and in Western Australia Andrew Turnell and Steve Edwards built on it in Signs of Safety, an approach to child protection casework that asks families and workers to map dangers and existing safety together and to scale how safe the child is (Signs of Safety, 1999).

Exam trap

"Individuals, families and entire cultures are given a different level of power as creators of their realities" describes narrative therapy (White and Epston): social constructionist, attentive to how dominant cultural stories carry power, and committed to letting people author their own stories. The distractors are modernist schools in which the therapist is the expert on what is wrong: transgenerational therapy (Bowen: differentiation passed down the generations, Section 7.3), strategic therapy (Haley: directives aimed at the family's hierarchy and its problem sequence, Section 7.5) and structural therapy (Minuchin: boundaries and subsystems, Section 7.4). Relationship questions ("What would your mother notice?") are solution-focused; circular questions are Milan's.

Programmes for troubled adolescents: FFT, MST, MDFT and BSFT

By the 1980s one group of young people had become a test case for every kind of psychological treatment: adolescents who were stealing, fighting, using drugs and appearing in juvenile courts. Individual counselling, residential institutions and detention had poor results, and parent training, which works well with younger children (Section 4.10), was not enough on its own for teenagers whose lives were organised around deviant peers. Four family programmes rose to the challenge. Each combines systemic ideas with behavioural methods, each is written down in a manual with training and supervision to keep therapists faithful to it, and each was tested in randomised trials (Table 7.16).

Functional family therapy (FFT) was developed from 1969 at the University of Utah by James Alexander and Bruce Parsons. Its name reflects its central idea: a young person's problem behaviour is functional, in the sense that it serves a purpose in the family's relationships. Running away and staying out late may regulate distance from a parent; a dramatic crisis may draw a distant parent closer. FFT therapists do not try to change these relational functions of closeness and distance, which they regard as neither good nor bad; they help the family find less destructive behaviours that achieve the same ends. The work runs through three phases, usually in about twelve to fourteen sessions over three to five months. In engagement and motivation, the therapist uses reattribution and reframing (Section 7.5) to reduce blame and negativity and to build a shared, family-focused definition of the problem ("Everyone in this family is exhausted and frightened"). In behaviour change, the therapist teaches communication, parenting skills, contracts and problem solving, fitted to the family's relational functions. In generalisation, the family learns to extend its gains to the school, the court and the neighbourhood and to plan for relapse.

Key study: Alexander and Parsons (1973) and the siblings

Question. Can a short behavioural-systems family treatment reduce reoffending among delinquent adolescents? Method. Eighty-six families of young people referred to a juvenile court in Utah for status offences (offences such as truancy, running away and being "ungovernable", which are offences only because of the young person's age) were randomly assigned to FFT, to client-centred family groups, to psychodynamic family therapy or to no treatment. The therapists were graduate students supervised by the developers. Results. FFT families showed more improvement in their observed interaction, and FFT adolescents committed fewer further status offences than those in the three comparison conditions, although there was no significant difference in criminal offences. In a follow-up three years later, Klein, Alexander and Parsons (1977) found that the younger brothers and sisters of FFT adolescents had appeared in court less often than the siblings in the other groups. Conclusion. Changing the family's interaction helped the referred child and, apparently, prevented problems in children who had never been treated. Criticism. The young people were relatively minor offenders, and a much later trial in routine services (Sexton and Turner, 2010, with 917 families of young offenders in 14 counties) found the same 22 percent felony reoffending rate a year after FFT and after ordinary probation, except that the young people whose therapists followed the model closely did significantly better.

Multisystemic therapy (MST) was developed by Scott Henggeler and his colleagues, including Charles Borduin, and is now based at the Medical University of South Carolina. Its theory comes from the developmental psychologist Urie Bronfenbrenner (1917 to 2005), whose social-ecological model (The Ecology of Human Development, 1979) pictured a child at the centre of nested systems: family, peers, school and neighbourhood, within a wider society. Serious antisocial behaviour, in this view, has many causes spread across all these systems, so treatment must reach all of them (Figure 7.11). MST is delivered at home and in the community by a small team of master's-level therapists, each with only four to six families, on call 24 hours a day, 7 days a week, for three to five months (about 60 hours of contact). The parents are the main agents of change, and the therapist works with them on monitoring, discipline and warmth, and on the school, the peer group and the family's supports. Nine treatment principles guide the work; among them, the therapist must find the "fit" between the problem and its systemic context, build on strengths, focus on present, action-oriented and well-defined goals, and, unusually, accept that the team, not the family, is responsible for engagement and outcome.

MST's best-known trial, the Missouri Delinquency Project (Borduin and colleagues, 1995), randomly assigned 176 serious juvenile offenders, who had averaged almost four felony arrests each, to MST or to individual therapy. Four years later the MST group had been rearrested less often, including for violent crimes, and a follow-up when the participants were on average 37 years old (Sawyer and Borduin, 2011) still found felony reoffending of 34.8 percent after MST against 54.8 percent after individual therapy. Independent researchers have been less impressed. A Cochrane review by Julia Littell and colleagues (2005) pointed out that most trials had been run by the developers and concluded that the evidence that MST beats other services was inconclusive. In England, the large START trial (Peter Fonagy and colleagues, 2018) assigned 684 families to MST or to good usual services and found no difference in the main outcome, out-of-home placement at 18 months (13 percent against 11 percent).

Multidimensional family therapy (MDFT), developed from the 1980s by Howard Liddle at the University of Miami, targets adolescent drug use and the delinquency that goes with it. The therapist works in four interlinked domains at once: the adolescent (seen alone as well as with the family), the parents (as adults with their own stresses and as caregivers), the family's interactions, and the systems outside the family, such as the school, the peer group and the juvenile justice system. In a 2001 trial with 182 adolescents using cannabis and alcohol, MDFT produced more improvement than adolescent group therapy or a multifamily educational programme, and its advantage in school performance and family functioning was still present a year later.

Brief strategic family therapy (BSFT) began in 1972 at the Spanish Family Guidance Center in Miami, which José Szapocznik and his colleagues set up to understand drug use among Cuban adolescents. They found families locked in a struggle with a cultural flavour: the children were becoming American faster than their parents, and parents and children had become adversaries over values. BSFT, a brief treatment lasting a few months, combines Minuchin's joining and restructuring (Section 7.4) with Haley's strategic focus on the interactions that maintain the problem (Section 7.5). Its team made two original contributions. In one-person family therapy they showed that the family system could be changed by working through one member when the others would not attend, with results comparable to conjoint therapy. And in a 1988 trial with 108 families, a deliberate "strategic structural systems engagement" procedure, which treated the family's refusal to come as the first interactional pattern to be changed, brought 93 percent of families into treatment, against 42 percent with ordinary engagement.

Table 7.16 Evidence-based family programmes for young people.

Exam trap

Match programme to founder and hallmark. FFT: Alexander and Parsons (Utah, 1969), relational functions, three phases, sibling effects. MST: Henggeler, Bronfenbrenner's social-ecological model, home-based, 24-hour availability, small caseloads, serious juvenile offenders. MDFT: Liddle, four domains, adolescent drug use. BSFT: Szapocznik, Hispanic families, structural plus strategic, one-person family therapy. A fifth name sometimes offered as a distractor, multidimensional treatment foster care (Patricia Chamberlain, Oregon Social Learning Center), places the young person with specially trained foster parents rather than treating the family at home.

The Maudsley approach to adolescent anorexia

Anorexia nervosa has a special place in family therapy. Minuchin's work on "psychosomatic families", with its famous family lunch session, made it a showcase for structural therapy (Section 7.4), but his claims rested on uncontrolled case series and on the assumption that the family's structure kept the illness going. (The disorder itself is described in the Psychiatric Disorders book.) The approach that now dominates treatment came from the Maudsley Hospital in London. There the psychiatrist Gerald Russell (1928 to 2018), who described bulimia nervosa in 1979, worked with the family therapists Christopher Dare and Ivan Eisler and the psychiatrist George Szmukler on the first controlled trial of family therapy for anorexia nervosa.

In that trial (Russell, Szmukler, Dare and Eisler, 1987) 80 patients, 57 with anorexia nervosa and 23 with bulimia nervosa, first had their weight restored in a specialist inpatient unit and were then randomly assigned to a year of family therapy or of individual supportive therapy. Family therapy was more effective for patients whose illness had begun before the age of 19 and had not become chronic; for older patients, individual therapy looked tentatively better. The advantage for the young patients was still visible at a five-year follow-up. A later trial by Eisler and colleagues (2000) compared conjoint family therapy, with everyone in the room, against separated family therapy, in which the therapist saw the parents and the adolescent separately. The two did equally well overall, but where mothers were highly critical of the patient, separated therapy worked better: expressed emotion (Section 7.2) at work again.

The Maudsley method was written into a manual by James Lock and Daniel le Grange, with Stewart Agras and Dare, in Treatment Manual for Anorexia Nervosa: A Family-Based Approach (2001), and it is now called family-based treatment (FBT). Its principles break with the older schools. The therapist is agnostic about the cause of the illness and tells the parents plainly that they did not cause it. The illness is separated from the young person, borrowing the narrative idea of externalising: it is the anorexia, not the daughter, that refuses food. And the parents, rather than the hospital, are put in charge of refeeding, much as a nursing team would be, while the therapist acts as a consultant to them. Treatment runs in three phases, typically over about twenty sessions in six to twelve months. In phase one (weight restoration) the parents take full control of eating; in the second session the family brings a meal, and the therapist helps the parents persuade their child to eat a little more than she intended. In phase two, as weight returns, control over eating is handed back to the adolescent step by step. In phase three the work turns to ordinary adolescent issues, such as independence, friendships and school, and to ending therapy. In a trial at Stanford and Chicago (Lock and colleagues, 2010) with 121 adolescents aged 12 to 18, FBT and an individual adolescent-focused therapy did equally well at the end of treatment, but FBT produced significantly more full remissions at the six-month and one-year follow-ups. Guidelines in several countries now put family therapy first for young people with anorexia. The National Institute for Health and Care Excellence in Britain (2017), for example, advises considering anorexia-nervosa-focused family therapy before any other psychological treatment for children and young people, with individual cognitive behavioural therapy or adolescent-focused psychotherapy as the alternatives when family therapy is unsuitable.

Exam trap

The Maudsley approach is family-based treatment for adolescent anorexia: first trial by Russell, Szmukler, Dare and Eisler (1987), manual by Lock and le Grange (2001). It works best for younger patients with a short illness. Do not confuse its family meal (which helps parents take charge of refeeding, with no assumption that the family caused the illness) with Minuchin's lunch session (which used the meal to expose and restructure the family's enmeshed patterns, Section 7.4). And keep the Maudsley Hospital's other famous names apart: Hans Eysenck and the behaviour therapists of Chapter 4 worked there too.

What the outcome research shows

After half a century of trials, what can be said about family therapy as a whole? In 1995, in a special issue of the Journal of Marital and Family Therapy, William Pinsof and Lyman Wynne (the author of pseudomutuality, Section 7.2) reviewed the field and concluded that marital and family therapy works: it is better than no treatment and, for several problems, it does better than individual treatment, but there was little evidence that any one school outperforms the others. William Shadish and Scott Baldwin (2003), pulling together twenty meta-analyses, reached similar conclusions: couple and family interventions are effective, their effects shrink a little at follow-up but remain significant, differences between kinds of family therapy are small, and about 40 to 50 percent of those treated show clinically significant change. When Baldwin, Shadish and colleagues (2012) pooled 24 trials of BSFT, FFT, MDFT and MST for adolescent delinquency and drug use, the four programmes together did better than usual treatment and better than alternative therapies, but by a modest margin (standardised effect sizes of about 0.2 to 0.3), and there was not enough evidence to say which of the four was best. Reviews by the Irish psychologist Alan Carr (2009, updated several times since) list the problems for which systemic interventions have the strongest support: conduct problems and drug misuse in young people, adolescent anorexia, and, among adults, schizophrenia (family psychoeducation, Section 7.2), alcohol problems and relationship distress (couple therapy, Section 7.8).

Three lessons stand out. First, the strongest evidence belongs to manualised programmes aimed at particular problems, not to the classic schools, partly because the schools were simply never tested in the same way. Second, effects tend to shrink when a programme leaves its developers' hands and is compared with good usual services, as the MST and FFT stories show, and a therapist's fidelity to the model makes a large difference. Third, the ideas of the classic schools have not disappeared; they live on inside the programmes, in BSFT's joining and restructuring, FFT's reframing, FBT's externalising and the attention every programme pays to engagement. Chapter 8 takes up the general questions of how therapies are tested and why different therapies so often perform alike.

Exam trap

If asked which family intervention has the best evidence: for adolescent anorexia, family-based treatment (Maudsley); for conduct problems in young children, parent management training (Section 4.10); for serious adolescent offending, MST or FFT; for adolescent drug use, MDFT, BSFT or MST; for relapse prevention in schizophrenia, family psychoeducation (Section 7.2), not any therapy based on the double bind. Overall, family therapy beats no treatment, but no single school has been shown to beat the others.

Gender, power and violence: the feminist critique

In 1978 the American psychologist Rachel Hare-Mustin published "A feminist approach to family therapy" in Family Process. Family therapists, she pointed out, prided themselves on understanding the social context of behaviour, yet they had never examined the consequences of a traditional socialisation that mainly disadvantages women, and much of their practice reinforced stereotyped sex roles without question. She showed how a gender-aware therapist could change sexist patterns through the therapy contract, the sharing of family tasks, communication, generational boundaries, relabelling, modelling and the alliances the therapist forms. A year earlier, in 1977, four family therapists, Marianne Walters, Betty Carter, Peggy Papp and Olga Silverstein, had formed the Women's Project in Family Therapy, which ran workshops across the United States and published The Invisible Web: Gender Patterns in Family Relationships (1988). Through the 1980s a series of papers, among them Virginia Goldner's "Feminism and family therapy" (1985), turned these concerns into a broad critique of family systems theory itself, and gave rise to feminist family therapy.

The critique had four main targets. The first was circular causality and neutrality. If every behaviour in a family is both cause and effect (Section 7.1), and the therapist must side with no one, then a husband who hits his wife and the wife who is hit are equally "part of the pattern". Michele Bograd's 1984 critique of family systems approaches to wife battering argued that this language hides who is doing what to whom and can end by blaming the victim. The second target was the hidden assumption that partners have equal power, when in most families men controlled more of the money, the decisions and the physical force. The third was mother-blaming. The classic systemic picture of the over-involved mother and the peripheral father, from the schizophrenogenic mother (Section 7.2) to the enmeshed mother of structural therapy (Section 7.4), located the pathology in mothers, without asking why mothers were so involved (society had handed them all the child care) or why fathers were absent (work and custom kept them out). The fourth was technique: interventions that restore a father's "executive" position, or a paradoxical task that asks a woman to clean the kitchen again and again, can reinforce the very roles that keep her unhappy. Hare-Mustin later (1987) gave the field a useful pair of terms: an alpha bias exaggerates the differences between men and women, as she argued psychodynamic theories did, while a beta bias ignores real differences, as systemic theories did when they treated husband and wife as interchangeable parts of a system (with Jeanne Marecek she developed the idea in "The meaning of difference", 1988).

The critique changed practice, above all where there is violence. A family or couple session can be dangerous for a woman who may be punished at home for what she says in the room, so therapists now screen each partner separately for violence before any joint work, and where there is ongoing, serious violence they treat safety as the first priority and do not begin conjoint couple therapy. The person who uses violence is held responsible for it; it is not treated as one more circular pattern. Some teams, such as the Gender and Violence Project of Goldner and her colleagues at the Ackerman Institute in New York ("Love and violence", 1990), developed conjoint work for couples who wished to stay together, but only under strict conditions of safety and accountability. Suspected abuse of a child overrides the usual rules of confidentiality: in India the Protection of Children from Sexual Offences Act, 2012 makes reporting of sexual offences against children compulsory, and the Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005 gives women civil remedies against abuse at home (Chapter 1 covers the law).

Exam trap

The feminist critique of family therapy began with Rachel Hare-Mustin (1978) and the Women's Project in Family Therapy (Walters, Carter, Papp and Silverstein, 1977; The Invisible Web, 1988). Its main targets were neutrality and circular causality in cases of violence, unequal power, mother-blaming and techniques that reinforce sex roles. Alpha bias exaggerates gender differences; beta bias ignores them. Betty Carter appears twice in this chapter: as co-author of the family life cycle (Section 7.1) and as a founder of the Women's Project.

Culture, and family work in India

Gender was the first difference the field learned to take seriously; culture was the second. Every school of family therapy carries cultural assumptions: that a healthy young adult leaves home, that parents should form a strong couple separate from their own parents, that feelings should be expressed openly, that children should become autonomous. Section 7.1 showed how Carter and McGoldrick's life cycle has to be translated for an Indian family, and how a therapist who does not understand a family's culture and customary practices will make wrong assessments and misinterpret normal behaviour as pathology. Cultural competence is the remedy: awareness of one's own cultural assumptions, knowledge of the family's culture, and skill in adapting methods so that they fit. BSFT, which began by noticing the clash of values between Cuban parents and their Americanising children, is one example of a programme built around culture rather than adapted to it afterwards. Another is the idea that interdependence is not automatically enmeshment: a mother who still decides what her twenty-year-old son eats may be over-involved, or she may be doing what every mother in her community does, and only the family's own norms can say which.

Indian families raise these questions in a sharp form. The traditional ideal is the joint family, in which three or four generations (grandparents, their sons, the sons' wives and children, and often unmarried aunts and uncles) live in one household under one authority, and Indian families have generally been patriarchal, patrilineal and patrilocal, with the bride moving into her husband's family home. Even where households have become nuclear, decisions about illness, treatment, marriage and money are often taken by the wider family, so the person who brings a patient to a clinic may not be the person who decides whether treatment continues. The Indian family therapist therefore asks early who holds authority (an elder brother, a grandfather, a mother-in-law), invites them respectfully, and joins with the hierarchy rather than challenging it head on, while protecting the less powerful members, who are often daughters-in-law and young women. Separate sessions may be needed, because a daughter-in-law may not be able to speak freely in front of her mother-in-law. Many Indian families also expect the therapist to be an active, directive expert; the psychiatrist Jaswant Singh Neki argued in 1973 that the guru and disciple (guru-chela) relationship offers a culturally familiar model for therapy, which is one reason why a purely not-knowing stance may need adapting here.

India also has its own tradition of involving families in care, which began before Western family therapy reached the country. In the 1950s (1957 is the year most Indian reviews give, though some accounts say 1950) Vidya Sagar, superintendent of the Amritsar Mental Hospital, allowed relatives to stay with patients in tents pitched on the hospital grounds and to take part in their care (Section 7.2). The idea of the family ward followed: family wards were set up at the Christian Medical College, Vellore, where descriptive reports appeared from 1967, and at the National Institute of Mental Health and Neuro Sciences (NIMHANS) in Bangalore, where a family psychiatric ward and a Family Psychiatric Centre were described by R. S. Bhatti, N. Janakiramaiah and S. M. Channabasavanna in Family Process in 1980. To this day a relative commonly stays on the ward as the patient's attendant in many Indian hospitals, which gives the clinician daily access to the family. Indian research has followed the same direction. Studies of expressed emotion in Chandigarh found lower levels of high EE than in Britain (Section 7.2). In Bangalore, S. Pai and R. L. Kapur developed an interview schedule for measuring the burden that a psychiatric patient places on the family (1981), and then showed (1983) that first-episode patients with schizophrenia treated at home by a visiting nurse trained in counselling did better after six months, clinically and socially and with a lighter burden on the family, than patients admitted to a mental hospital and followed up as outpatients. At the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research in Chandigarh, a randomised trial by Kulhara and colleagues (2009) gave 76 patients with schizophrenia and their caregivers either monthly psychoeducation sessions for nine months or routine outpatient care, and found the psychoeducation group better on psychopathology, disability, caregiver support and satisfaction. At the Schizophrenia Research Foundation in Chennai, R. Thara and her colleagues found that families preferred flexible, informal education tailored to their needs over a structured research programme, and attended it better (2005).

Clinical link

For an Indian clinical psychologist the practical conclusions are clear. Most people with severe mental illness live with their families, who provide most of their care, so family work is not an optional extra. Begin with psychoeducation and the reduction of blame, because many families have been told, by relatives or even by professionals, that they caused the illness or that it is the result of fate or black magic. Use the family's own language and explanations as a starting point rather than an obstacle. Ask about caregiver burden, because exhausted caregivers become critical caregivers. And remember the rules of this section: screen for violence separately, protect the less powerful members, and never mistake a culture's ordinary closeness for pathology.

Exam trap

The consequence of poor awareness of a family's culture and familial practices is wrong assessments and misinterpretations, not accurate understanding, better communication or a stronger alliance. Indian firsts to keep straight: relatives staying with patients in tents at Amritsar (Vidya Sagar, 1950s, usually dated 1957); family wards at CMC Vellore (reports from 1967) and NIMHANS (Bhatti and colleagues, Family Process, 1980); the Chandigarh expressed emotion studies (Wig, Leff and colleagues, Section 7.2); the family burden interview schedule of Pai and Kapur (Bangalore, 1981).

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. The theory that relationships last when their rewards outweigh their costs, set out by Thibaut and Kelley in 1959, is social theory.
  2. A contract in which each person changes independently and earns his own reward, rather than trading changes, is a contract.
  3. Heinz von Foerster's "cybernetics of cybernetics", in which the observer is part of the observed system, is called cybernetics.
  4. Harlene Anderson and Harry Goolishian's stance of genuine curiosity, with the client as the expert, is the stance.
  5. Tom Andersen's team, which discusses its ideas aloud while the family listens, is the team.
  6. "What would your mother notice you doing differently?" is a solution-focused question.
  7. Functional family therapy was developed at the University of Utah by James Alexander and Bruce .
  8. Multisystemic therapy is based on Urie Bronfenbrenner's model.
  9. The Maudsley family-based treatment puts the in charge of refeeding an adolescent with anorexia.
  10. Rachel Hare-Mustin's term for a bias that ignores real differences between men and women is bias.

Answers: (1) exchange, (2) good faith (parallel), (3) second-order, (4) not-knowing, (5) reflecting, (6) relationship, (7) Parsons, (8) social-ecological, (9) parents, (10) beta.

7.8 Couples TherapyLower yield · 1 Q

Vikram and Ritika have been married for six years. Theirs was an arranged marriage: their parents met first, the two of them met three times, and the wedding followed within the year. They live with Vikram's parents in a flat in Pune, with their five-year-old son. Ritika asked for the appointment, and she does most of the talking. "He comes home, eats, and sits with his phone. When I try to talk to him, he gets up and leaves the room. I am alone in a house full of people." Vikram, when he is finally asked, speaks quietly. "Whatever I do, it is wrong. She starts complaining the moment I walk in. So I keep quiet. If I say nothing, at least there is no fight." You have met this couple before, in a different form: they are the nagging wife and the withdrawing husband of Figure 7.1, each telling a linear story in which the other started it.

Seventy years ago Vikram and Ritika might have been seen separately, each by a different counsellor, and given advice about the duties of a husband and a wife. Today a couples therapist would see them together and could draw on at least four well-researched maps of their problem. A behaviour therapist would see two people who have stopped rewarding each other and never learned to solve problems together. An integrative behaviour therapist would add that some of their differences will never change and must be accepted. An emotionally focused therapist would see two frightened people caught in a cycle that protects each of them from the hurt underneath. A cognitive-behavioural therapist would ask what each believes the other's behaviour means. And John Gottman, the researcher who spent four decades filming couples, would listen to the first three minutes of their argument and look for contempt. This section follows these maps in turn, then asks what the evidence says and how couples therapy has to bend to fit an Indian marriage.

From marriage counselling to couples therapy

Section 7.1 described the first marriage counselling centres of the 1930s. For their first few decades they offered what Alan Gurman and Peter Fraenkel, in their 2002 history of the field, call atheoretical marriage counselling: practical advice and education about the values and obligations of married life, given by people trained in many fields, usually to each partner separately. A second strand ran alongside it. Some psychoanalysts began, cautiously, to experiment with treating both spouses, at first each in a separate analysis. Then, from the 1960s, the new family therapists swallowed the field. To Don Jackson, Virginia Satir, Jay Haley and Murray Bowen, a marriage was simply one more family subsystem, to be understood with the ideas of homeostasis, triangles and communication that you met in Section 7.1 (their schools are taught in Sections 7.3 to 7.6). Gurman and Fraenkel describe family therapy as having not merely absorbed marriage counselling but "engulfed, consumed and devoured" it. Only from the mid-1980s did couple therapy re-emerge as a field in its own right, with its own theories, its own treatment manuals and its own randomised trials, and that is the period this section is mostly about.

The name changed along the way. "Marital therapy" and "marriage counselling" assumed a husband and a wife; "couple therapy" or couples therapy (both forms are used) covers any two partners in a committed relationship, married or not, of the same sex or different sexes. It can be defined as psychotherapy in which the two partners are seen together, with the relationship itself as the focus of treatment. Three formats are worth distinguishing, because they turn up as options in questions. In conjoint therapy (Section 7.1) both partners attend the same sessions with the same therapist; this is the standard format today. In concurrent therapy one therapist sees each partner separately. In collaborative therapy each partner has a different therapist, and the two therapists consult each other.

Why treat the relationship at all? Because relationship distress is common, and it is bad for people. Distressed partners are more likely to be depressed or anxious and to drink heavily, and their children are more likely to have emotional and behavioural problems. The link with depression is strong enough that couples therapy is a recognised treatment for depression when the marriage is in trouble (Section 7.8.7). Couples therapy is not, however, for every couple. A careful therapist sees each partner alone at least once during assessment and asks, in that private meeting, about violence, fear and coercion. Where one partner is afraid of the other, joint sessions can put her in danger (what she says in the room may be punished at home), and the first task is safety, not communication training. An affair that is still going on in secret, or a partner who has already decided to leave and wants the therapist to break the news, are other common reasons why conjoint therapy cannot start, or must start differently. Assessment usually combines joint and individual interviews, a watched discussion of a real disagreement, and questionnaires such as Graham Spanier's Dyadic Adjustment Scale (1976), which most of the trials in this section use as their outcome measure (the questionnaires themselves belong to the Assessment book).

Exam trap

Conjoint means both partners together with one therapist; concurrent means one therapist seeing each partner separately; collaborative means two therapists, one for each partner, who confer. "Couple therapy", "couples therapy" and the older "marital therapy" name the same field. Before conjoint work begins, each partner should be screened alone for intimate partner violence; couples therapy is not the first treatment when one partner fears the other.

Traditional behavioural couple therapy

Behaviour therapists came to marriage in the late 1960s, carrying two ideas. The first was the social learning principle you met in Chapter 4: behaviour that is rewarded becomes more frequent, behaviour that is punished or ignored becomes less frequent, and people learn new skills by watching and practising. The second came from social psychology. In The Social Psychology of Groups (1959), John Thibaut (1917 to 1986) and Harold Kelley (1921 to 2003) had proposed social exchange theory (Section 7.7): people stay in relationships to the extent that the rewards outweigh the costs, compared with what they believe they could get elsewhere. Put the two ideas together and you have a behavioural account of a failing marriage. Satisfied couples give each other many rewards (affection, help, interest, praise) and few punishments. Distressed couples give fewer rewards and more punishments, and they show negative reciprocity: a hostile remark from one is swiftly returned by the other. Worse, distressed partners try to change each other by aversive control, nagging, threatening, sulking and withdrawing until the other gives in. Each surrender rewards the coercion, so both partners learn to coerce more; the Oregon psychologist Gerald Patterson described the same coercive process between parents and aggressive children (Section 7.7). The behavioural therapist's job follows from the analysis: increase the rewards, reduce the punishments, and teach the skills that distressed couples lack.

One of the pioneers was the American therapist Richard B. Stuart, whom you met in Section 7.7. In 1969 he reported what he called the operant-interpersonal treatment of four distressed couples: each partner named the specific behaviours he or she wanted more of from the other, and the couple agreed in writing to exchange them. Stuart's later book Helping Couples Change (1980) gave the field one of its best-known exercises, caring days. Each partner writes a list of small, specific, positive actions that would make him or her feel cared for ("Ask about my day before you pick up your phone"; "Bring me tea on Sunday morning"), and each then performs several of the other's items every day, whatever the other does. Stuart's instruction was, in effect, to act as if you cared. The point is not the tea. Distressed couples have stopped noticing anything good about each other, and a daily stream of small, visible positives breaks the deadlock in which each waits for the other to change first.

Agreements between partners were usually written down as contracts, and two kinds were used (Section 7.7 introduced both for families; the general rules for behavioural contracts are in Section 4.5). In a quid pro quo contract (Latin for "something for something") each partner's change is made conditional on the other's: "If Vikram greets me without his phone in his hand, I will not raise complaints until after dinner." The phrase came from Don Jackson, whose 1965 paper on the "marital quid pro quo" described the unspoken bargains that every marriage runs on; the behaviourists simply made the bargain explicit. Quid pro quo contracts have a weakness, however: if one partner fails, the other is released, and distressed couples are experts at waiting for the other to fail first. In a good faith contract, proposed in 1973 by Robert Weiss, Hyman Hops and Gerald Patterson of the Oregon Marital Studies Program, each partner's change is independent of the other's and is rewarded separately, often by a privilege the person chooses, so that one partner's lapse does not cancel the other's effort.

The approach was systematised by Neil S. Jacobson (1949 to 1999), whose component analysis of cognitive therapy for depression you met in Chapter 5, and Gayla Margolin, in Marital Therapy: Strategies Based on Social Learning and Behavior Exchange Principles (1979). Their manual defined what is now called traditional behavioural couple therapy (TBCT; also called behavioural marital therapy or behavioural couples therapy), and it rests on three components.

  • Behaviour exchange. Each partner identifies behaviours that please the other, the couple keeps track of how often they happen, and each deliberately increases them, through caring days and similar exercises. The aim is quick relief: a rise in daily positives that makes the harder work possible.
  • Communication training. The couple learn speaker and listener skills. The speaker talks about his or her own feelings and a specific behaviour, using an "I" statement ("I feel hurt when you look at your phone while I am talking") rather than an attack on the other's character ("You never care about anyone but yourself"). The listener paraphrases what he heard and checks that he has understood before replying. The therapist teaches these skills as any behaviour therapist teaches a skill: instruction, modelling, rehearsal in the session, feedback and homework (behaviour rehearsal is described in Section 4.7).
  • Problem-solving training. The couple learn to solve one problem at a time, in two separate phases. In the definition phase they begin with something positive, describe the problem in specific behavioural terms, say how it makes them feel, admit their own part in it and keep it brief. Only then, in the solution phase, do they brainstorm solutions without criticising any, weigh them, agree on a specific change, write it down and set a time to review it.

Push Vikram and Ritika through this. In the first weeks each writes a caring-days list: Ritika asks for fifteen minutes of tea together on the balcony after dinner, and Vikram asks to be greeted without a complaint when he walks in. They practise in the session. Ritika says, "I feel lonely in the evenings when you go to your phone," and Vikram, instead of defending himself, says back what he heard. Then they tackle a real problem, the Sunday visits to Vikram's sister that Ritika dreads. They define it, brainstorm, and agree to visit on alternate Sundays and to spend the others as a family of three. For many couples this is enough, and the change can be quick.

TBCT became the most thoroughly researched of all couple therapies. A meta-analysis by William Shadish and Scott Baldwin (2005) pooled 30 randomised trials and found behavioural marital therapy clearly better than no treatment, with a moderate effect size (d of about 0.59). But Jacobson, to his great credit, looked harder at his own results. In a 1984 reanalysis of outcome data from several trials, he and his colleagues asked not whether couples improved on average but how many actually ended up in the range of happy, non-distressed couples. The answer was sobering. About half of the couples improved, but only about a third moved into the non-distressed range, and in later follow-ups roughly a third of the couples who had improved relapsed within two years. A study from Texas sharpened the worry.

Key study: behavioural versus insight-oriented marital therapy (Snyder, Wills and Grady-Fletcher, 1991)

Question. Does a behavioural or an insight-oriented couple therapy produce more lasting change? Method. At Texas A&M University, Douglas Snyder and Robert Wills (1989) had randomly assigned distressed couples to behavioural marital therapy, to insight-oriented marital therapy (IOMT), or to a waiting list. IOMT, Snyder's own approach, helps each partner understand how feelings, expectations and defences learned in earlier relationships, especially with parents, are being replayed in the marriage (the psychodynamic ideas behind it are in Chapter 3). In 1991 Snyder, Wills and Grady-Fletcher obtained four-year follow-up data on 59 of the treated couples. Results. At the end of therapy and six months later the two treatments had done equally well. At four years, 38 percent of the couples who had received behavioural therapy had divorced, compared with 3 percent of those who had received insight-oriented therapy. Conclusion. Skills training may produce change that fades, whereas working through each partner's emotional history may produce change that lasts. Criticism. Jacobson replied that the insight-oriented therapy in the study included many behavioural techniques, that the behavioural therapy was a narrow version, and that the investigators were committed to IOMT. It was a single trial, and no later study has reproduced so large a difference. Together with the reanalyses, however, it convinced Jacobson that something was missing from TBCT.

Exam trap

Jacobson and Margolin (1979) wrote the manual of traditional behavioural couple therapy; Jacobson and Christensen (1996) wrote integrative behavioural couple therapy. TBCT's three components are behaviour exchange, communication training and problem-solving training, and its theory is social learning plus social exchange (Thibaut and Kelley), not attachment (that is emotionally focused therapy). Caring days are Richard Stuart's. A quid pro quo contract makes each partner's change conditional on the other's (the phrase "marital quid pro quo" is Don Jackson's, 1965); a good faith contract rewards each partner's change independently (Weiss, Hops and Patterson, 1973). Insight-oriented marital therapy is Douglas Snyder's.

Integrative behavioural couple therapy

Jacobson went back to his failures and asked who they were. TBCT, he found, worked best for couples who were younger, less distressed, still emotionally engaged with each other and broadly agreed about how a marriage should be run. It worked worst for couples divided by deep differences of temperament or values: one who needs closeness and one who needs space, one who plans and one who improvises, one who spends and one who saves. For such couples, teaching both partners to negotiate change was rather like teaching two people to negotiate a change in their height. Worse, the push for change was itself part of the problem. Each partner's efforts to make the other change led the other to dig in, and each round of pressure and resistance drove the two further apart.

Jacobson's answer, developed with the psychologist Andrew Christensen of the University of California, Los Angeles, was integrative behavioural couple therapy (IBCT), set out in their book Integrative Couple Therapy: Promoting Acceptance and Change (1996). It is "integrative" because it integrates two goals. Change is still sought where change is possible, using the behaviour exchange and the communication and problem-solving training of TBCT. But IBCT adds, and usually puts first, acceptance: letting go of the struggle to change a partner's unwanted behaviour and coming to experience that behaviour differently, as understandable, tolerable, or even as part of what one loves in the partner, so that it stops being a battleground. Acceptance is not resignation, and it never means putting up with abuse. It means that a partner's quietness, say, stops being evidence that he does not care and becomes simply the way he is. And acceptance has a paradoxical result that IBCT therapists see again and again: when the pressure to change stops, partners often change.

IBCT begins with a formulation of the couple's problem, which in the original version had three parts. The theme is the central difference that the couple's conflicts keep returning to, such as closeness and distance, or control and responsibility. The polarisation process is the way each partner's efforts to change the other push both to more extreme positions: the more Ritika pursues closeness, the more Vikram protects his space, and the more he withdraws, the more urgently she pursues. The mutual trap is the stuck and hopeless place this leaves them in, each feeling unable to give way without losing something essential. (In later writing Christensen and his colleagues organised the formulation as a DEEP analysis: the partners' Differences, their Emotional sensitivities, the External stressors on them, and their Patterns of communication.) The formulation is shared with the couple at a feedback session and becomes the backbone of the therapy. Three groups of techniques then build acceptance.

  • Empathic joining. The therapist helps each partner voice the soft emotions (hurt, fear, sadness, loneliness) that lie beneath the hard ones (anger, blame, contempt) that the other usually sees. When Ritika stops saying "You don't care" and says "In the evenings I feel invisible," Vikram can respond with sympathy instead of self-defence. The problem is reframed as a painful difference from which both partners suffer, not a fault in one of them.
  • Unified detachment. The partners are helped to step back and describe their pattern together, calmly and without blame, as if they were watching another couple: what sets it off, who does what next, how it ends. They often give it a name and are encouraged to find it a little funny; Vikram and Ritika call theirs "the phone and the complaint". The problem becomes an "it" that they face together rather than something one does to the other, much as narrative therapists externalise a problem (Sections 6.8 and 7.7).
  • Tolerance building. For behaviour that probably will not change, the therapist helps the partners become less upset by it. The methods include pointing out the positive side of the negative behaviour (Vikram's calm silence, which maddens Ritika in an argument, is the same steadiness she relied on when her father was in hospital); rehearsing the troublesome behaviour in the session until its sting fades; asking a partner to fake the behaviour at home now and then, when he does not really feel it, and to tell the other afterwards, so that both can observe the reaction without the usual heat; and encouraging self-care, so that each partner can meet some needs in other ways when the other cannot meet them.
Key study: traditional versus integrative behavioural couple therapy (Christensen and colleagues, 2004 to 2010)

Question. Does adding acceptance to behavioural couple therapy help seriously distressed couples more than change-focused TBCT? Method. The trial began under Jacobson and Christensen; after Jacobson's sudden death in 1999, Christensen carried it through. At the University of California, Los Angeles, and the University of Washington in Seattle, 134 seriously and chronically distressed married couples were randomly assigned to about eight months of either TBCT or IBCT and were then followed for five years. Results. The two therapies took different paths. TBCT couples improved faster at first and then levelled off; IBCT couples improved steadily throughout. By the end of treatment 71 percent of IBCT couples and 59 percent of TBCT couples were reliably improved or recovered on the Dyadic Adjustment Scale. Through the two-year follow-up IBCT kept a statistically significant advantage in relationship satisfaction, although the proportions of couples clinically improved were similar (69 against 60 percent). By five years the two had converged: about half of the couples in each group (50 and 46 percent) were still clinically improved, and about a quarter in each (26 and 28 percent) had separated or divorced. Conclusion. Both therapies helped even very distressed couples substantially; the advantage of IBCT was real but modest, and it faded over time without further treatment. Criticism. There was no untreated control group, so the trial compares two active treatments; all the couples were married Americans; and the investigators included the developers of IBCT.

IBCT has since been rolled out across the United States Department of Veterans Affairs, and an online self-help programme based on it, OurRelationship, has been tested in a national trial. It is often grouped with the third-wave behaviour therapies of Chapter 5, because like them it adds acceptance to the traditional behavioural goal of change.

Exam trap

IBCT is Andrew Christensen and Neil Jacobson's (1996), and its signature idea is acceptance, built through empathic joining (expressing the soft emotions beneath the hard ones), unified detachment (describing the pattern together, without blame, as an "it") and tolerance building (becoming less reactive to behaviour that will not change), combined with TBCT's change techniques. Acceptance in IBCT means giving up the struggle to change the partner; it is not resignation and not tolerance of abuse. Do not confuse it with acceptance in Steven Hayes's acceptance and commitment therapy (Section 5.9), which is acceptance of one's own thoughts and feelings.

Emotionally focused couple therapy

While Jacobson was rethinking behaviour therapy in Seattle, a different answer was taking shape in Canada. In the early 1980s Susan Johnson (1947 to 2024), known to everyone as Sue Johnson, was a doctoral student at the University of British Columbia in Vancouver, and her supervisor was Leslie Greenberg (born 1945), whose emotion-focused therapy for individuals you met in Section 6.7. Together they built a couple therapy out of the humanistic and experiential tradition: Rogers's empathy, the Gestalt therapists' attention to emotion in the present moment, and the systems therapists' attention to circular interaction. In their first trial, Johnson and Greenberg (1985) compared this experiential treatment with a behavioural problem-solving treatment for distressed couples, and the experiential treatment produced the greater gains. They described the method in Emotionally Focused Therapy for Couples (1988).

Johnson then took the approach in her own direction, at the University of Ottawa, by grounding it in attachment theory. John Bowlby (Sections 3.5.5 and 7.1) had argued that infants are born with an attachment system that keeps them close to a caregiver who offers safety, and that separation from that figure triggers a predictable sequence of protest, despair and finally detachment. In 1987 the American social psychologists Cindy Hazan and Phillip Shaver proposed that adult romantic love is an attachment process too. Johnson drew the clinical conclusion. In emotionally focused couple therapy (EFT, sometimes written EFCT), partners are understood as each other's attachment figures, and relationship distress is understood as separation distress: the panic and protest that arise when a person can no longer count on the partner to be there. The fights are not, at bottom, about the dishes or the in-laws or the phone. They are about one question, which Johnson put in plain words: "Are you there for me?"
