Her books, beginning with The Practice of Emotionally Focused Marital Therapy: Creating Connection (1996) and including the popular Hold Me Tight (2008), spread the approach worldwide. A secure bond, she wrote, needs a partner who is accessible (can I reach you?), responsive (will you respond to me emotionally?) and engaged (will you value me and stay close?), which she abbreviated as A.R.E.

Where behaviour therapists saw skill deficits, Johnson saw emotion organising behaviour. When the bond feels threatened, each partner reacts in the way that his or her attachment history has made automatic, and the two reactions lock together into a negative interaction cycle that runs on its own. The commonest is the pursue-withdraw cycle: one partner, anxious about the connection, protests, criticises and pursues; the other, feeling attacked and afraid of failing, defends, goes quiet and withdraws, which makes the first pursue harder still. Researchers had already documented the same pattern under the name demand-withdraw. In a 1990 study of 31 couples, Andrew Christensen and Christopher Heavey found that the wife-demand, husband-withdraw sequence was more common than the reverse, but only when the couple was discussing a change the wife wanted; when they discussed a change the husband wanted, the difference disappeared. The pattern, in other words, belongs to the situation as much as to gender. The two other cycles are attack-attack, in which both partners criticise and blame, and withdraw-withdraw, in which both have given up and the relationship goes cold. In Hold Me Tight Johnson gave the three cycles memorable names: Find the Bad Guy (attack-attack), the Protest Polka (pursue-withdraw) and Freeze and Flee (withdraw-withdraw).

The key to changing the cycle lies underneath it. What each partner shows the other is a secondary, reactive emotion (anger, irritation, coldness). What each actually feels, and hides, is a primary and more vulnerable emotion (fear of being abandoned, shame at being inadequate), bound up with an unmet attachment need. Greenberg's distinction between primary and secondary emotions (Section 6.7) is used directly. Figure 7.12 shows the cycle in which Vikram and Ritika are caught.

The EFT therapist works on two levels at once. Within each partner, she tracks and deepens emotion: she reflects and validates, asks evocative questions ("What happens inside you when her voice rises?"), heightens a key phrase by repeating it slowly, and offers empathic conjectures that put words to what has not yet been said ("Maybe part of you is saying, 'I will never be good enough for her'?"). Between the partners, she tracks and names the cycle and reframes the problem: the enemy is the cycle, not the partner. Then she restructures the interaction directly through enactments, asking one partner to turn and tell the other, here and now, what he has just discovered ("Can you tell Ritika, now, what you just told me about always feeling that you fail?"). Change happens not because the couple has learned a skill but because they have had a new emotional experience of each other in the room.

The process unfolds in three stages and nine steps, summarised in Table 7.17. Stage 2 contains the two change events that Johnson's research singled out as the heart of the therapy. In withdrawer re-engagement the withdrawn partner comes out of hiding and states his own fears and needs. In blamer softening the previously critical partner, now able to trust that the other is reachable, asks for comfort and closeness from a position of vulnerability, and the other responds. Couples who complete a softening tend to do well. Johnson and her colleagues also described the attachment injury (Johnson, Makinen and Millikin, 2001): a betrayal or abandonment at a moment of urgent need, such as an affair, or a partner's absence during a miscarriage or a parent's death, which becomes an impasse because the injured partner returns to it whenever closeness is attempted. In their model of resolution, the injured partner describes the hurt and its impact, the other acknowledges it and expresses remorse, and the injured partner then asks for, and receives, the comfort that was missing at the time.

Table 7.17 The three stages and nine steps of emotionally focused couple therapy (after Sue Johnson).
StageStepsWhat happens with Vikram and Ritika
1 Cycle de-escalation(1) Build the alliance and assess the conflicts in attachment terms; (2) identify the negative cycle; (3) reach the unacknowledged emotions beneath each partner's position; (4) reframe the problem as the cycle and the underlying needsThey learn to see "the phone and the complaint" as a cycle that grips them both, driven by her fear and his shame
2 Restructuring interactional positions(5) Help each partner own disowned needs and fears; (6) help each accept the other's experience; (7) help each express needs directly, creating new bonding events (withdrawer re-engagement, then blamer softening)Vikram tells Ritika he goes silent because he feels he can never please her; Ritika, softer, asks him to hold her when she feels alone
3 Consolidation(8) Find new solutions to old practical problems; (9) consolidate the new positions and the new, secure cycleWith the bond safer, they solve the Sunday-visit problem easily and build rituals of connection

EFT has a solid, if smaller, evidence base. A 1999 meta-analysis of the early trials by Johnson, John Hunsley, Greenberg and Schindler reported a large effect, and Johnson has cited recovery from distress in about 70 percent of treated couples, though the trials were few, small and run by the therapy's developers. A larger meta-analysis by Rathgeber and colleagues at the University of Münster (2019) pooled 33 randomised trials with 2,730 participants. At the end of treatment both emotionally focused therapy (effect size g of 0.73) and behavioural couple therapy (g of 0.53) produced medium effects on relationship satisfaction, the difference between them was not significant, and the gains shrank over follow-up. The authors warned that publication bias may have inflated both.

Exam trap

Emotionally focused couple therapy is Sue Johnson's, first developed with Leslie Greenberg (Johnson and Greenberg, 1985; Greenberg and Johnson, 1988), and it rests on attachment theory (Bowlby; Hazan and Shaver, 1987): distress is separation distress, not a skill deficit. Do not confuse it with Greenberg's emotion-focused therapy for individuals (Section 6.7) or with emotion-focused coping (a stress concept). The pursue-withdraw cycle is the same pattern as the demand-withdraw pattern studied by Christensen and Heavey (1990). The two key change events, withdrawer re-engagement and blamer softening, both come in Stage 2. A.R.E. stands for accessible, responsive and engaged.

Cognitive-behavioural couple therapy

Behavioural couple therapy treated what partners do; the cognitive revolution of Chapter 5 asked what they think about what the other does. The same act can mean very different things. When Vikram picks up his phone, Ritika could think "He is exhausted after work" or "He avoids me because I bore him", and only the second makes her angry. Donald H. Baucom and Norman B. Epstein brought this idea into couple therapy in Cognitive-Behavioral Marital Therapy (1990) and later broadened it in Enhanced Cognitive-Behavioral Therapy for Couples (2002). Cognitive-behavioural couple therapy (CBCT) keeps the behaviour exchange and the communication and problem-solving training of TBCT and adds the identification and testing of five kinds of relationship cognition that Baucom, Epstein and their colleagues described:

  • Selective attention: what each partner notices and what he or she misses (Ritika notices every evening on the phone and none of the mornings he makes her tea).
  • Attributions: explanations of why the partner behaved as he or she did.
  • Expectancies: predictions about what will happen ("If I bring it up, he'll walk out").
  • Assumptions: beliefs about how partners and relationships actually are ("Men don't talk about feelings").
  • Standards: beliefs about how they should be ("If he really loved me, he would know what I need without my telling him").

Attributions have been studied most. In a 1990 review, Thomas Bradbury and Frank Fincham showed that distressed spouses make distress-maintaining attributions: the partner's negative behaviour is put down to causes that are internal to him, stable, global and intentional ("He ignores me because he is selfish; he has always been like that; it's how he treats everyone"), while his positive behaviour is put down to external, temporary and specific causes ("He was nice today only because his mother was watching"). Happy couples do the reverse, giving each other the benefit of the doubt. Distress-maintaining attributions keep a relationship miserable whatever the partner actually does, because good behaviour is discounted and bad behaviour confirms the case. CBCT therapists teach partners to catch such thoughts and test them with the methods of Chapter 5: Socratic questions, looking for alternative explanations and examining the evidence, and, for rigid standards, the kind of challenge to "musts" that Albert Ellis taught. Aaron Beck brought the same thinking to a popular audience in Love Is Never Enough (1988). In trials, CBCT works about as well as TBCT; adding cognitive restructuring changes how partners think, but it has not reliably improved the overall outcome.

Exam trap

Cognitive-behavioural couple therapy is Baucom and Epstein's (1990); Aaron Beck's couples book is Love Is Never Enough (1988). Distressed couples explain the partner's bad behaviour as internal, stable, global and intentional, and the partner's good behaviour as external, unstable and specific (Bradbury and Fincham, 1990). "Standards" are beliefs about how a relationship should be; "assumptions" are beliefs about how it is.

Gottman's research on what predicts divorce

The therapies so far began with a theory and then tested it. John Mordecai Gottman (born 1942), who studied mathematics before he turned to psychology, began at the other end. He set out to watch, measure and follow up large numbers of ordinary couples, to find out what actually distinguishes relationships that last from relationships that break. With his long-time collaborator, the psychophysiologist Robert Levenson, he developed a laboratory procedure that became famous. A couple arrived after being apart for at least eight hours. They first talked about the events of their day; then they spent 15 minutes trying to resolve a continuing disagreement; then they talked about something pleasant. Throughout, video cameras recorded their faces and bodies, and sensors recorded heart rate, skin conductance, blood flow and even how much each partner fidgeted in the chair. Afterwards each partner watched the video alone and turned a rating dial to show how he or she had felt, moment by moment. Trained coders then scored every second of the interaction for specific emotions (interest, affection, humour, anger, sadness, whining, contempt and others) using Gottman's Specific Affect Coding System. At the University of Washington in Seattle, Gottman later added a studio apartment, which reporters nicknamed the Love Lab, where couples spent a day living as normally as they could while the cameras ran. And then came the crucial step: the researchers followed the couples for years to see which stayed together and which divorced.

What they found overturned several common beliefs. Anger on its own did not predict divorce; plenty of stable, satisfied couples argued loudly. Nor did the sheer number of arguments. What mattered was the balance between positive and negative behaviour during conflict, and certain particular kinds of negativity.

The five to one ratio. In What Predicts Divorce? (1994) and its popular companion Why Marriages Succeed or Fail (1994), Gottman reported that stable couples showed at least about five positive interactions (interest, affection, humour, agreement, empathy) for every negative one while they were discussing a conflict, whereas couples heading for divorce showed a ratio of about one to one or worse. He found that stable couples came in three styles: validating couples, who listen and compromise calmly; volatile couples, who argue passionately but also laugh and love passionately; and conflict-avoiding couples, who agree to disagree and rarely confront each other. Each style can work, as long as the ratio holds. The two unstable types, hostile and hostile-detached couples, fall below it.

The four horsemen. Four kinds of negativity predicted divorce so strongly that Gottman named them, after the riders who bring the end of the world in the Book of Revelation, the four horsemen: criticism, contempt, defensiveness and stonewalling. Criticism, in Gottman's sense, is an attack on the partner's character, usually with "always" or "never" ("You never think of anyone but yourself"); it differs from a complaint, which is about a specific behaviour ("I was upset that you didn't call to say you'd be late"), and complaints are healthy. Contempt speaks from a position of superiority: sarcasm, mockery, name-calling, hostile humour, sneering, rolling the eyes. Its facial sign is a one-sided tightening of the lip corner. Contempt was the single strongest predictor of divorce in Gottman's research, because it tells the partner that he or she is beneath respect. Defensiveness is self-protection by counter-attack or by playing the innocent victim ("Yes, but you..."), which is really a way of blaming the partner. Stonewalling is the listener's withdrawal from the interaction: he stops responding, looks away, and sits like a stone wall. It tends to arrive later in a relationship's decline, often as a response to being overwhelmed, and in Gottman's samples of married couples the stonewaller was most often the husband. Gottman also described an antidote for each horseman, as Table 7.18 shows.

Table 7.18 Gottman's four horsemen and their antidotes.
HorsemanWhat it isExampleAntidote
CriticismAn attack on the partner's character, not a complaint about a behaviour"You never listen. You only care about your phone."Gentle start-up: describe the situation and your feeling, and ask for what you need
Contempt (strongest predictor)Disrespect from a position of superiority: sarcasm, mockery, eye-rolling"Oh, the great man is too tired to speak to his wife."Build a culture of appreciation and respect; express fondness and admiration
DefensivenessExcuses, counter-attack, innocent victimhood; blaming the partner"It's not my fault. You start complaining the moment I walk in."Take responsibility, even for part of the problem
StonewallingWithdrawing from the interaction; shutting down and not respondingVikram says nothing, picks up his phone and leaves the roomPhysiological self-soothing: take a break of at least 20 minutes, then return

Harsh start-up. Gottman noticed that a conflict discussion tends to end the way it begins. A harsh start-up opens with criticism or contempt; a soft or gentle start-up opens with a complaint stated without blame. The key study below showed how much the opening matters.

Key study: the first three minutes (Carrère and Gottman, 1999)

Question. Can the way a marital conflict discussion begins predict divorce? Method. Sybil Carrère and John Gottman videotaped 124 newlywed couples in Seattle during a 15-minute discussion of a continuing disagreement, coded the emotions shown with the Specific Affect Coding System, and divided each discussion into five three-minute segments. They then followed the couples for six years. Results. The balance of positive and negative emotion in the first three minutes alone, for both husbands and wives, predicted which couples would divorce over the six years. For wives, the first three minutes predicted as well as the remaining twelve. Conclusion. A harsh start-up sets the course of the whole conversation, and repeated over thousands of conversations, the course of the marriage. Criticism. Like most divorce-prediction studies, it fitted statistical models to couples whose outcomes were already known, which inflates apparent accuracy (see below).

Flooding. When a conflict turns hostile, a partner may experience flooding, becoming so physiologically aroused (Gottman's marker was a heart rate climbing above about 100 beats a minute, with a surge of stress hormones) that he can no longer listen, think or respond with humour, and feels only the urge to fight or flee. Flooding is what usually lies behind stonewalling. Its remedy is not to push on but to stop: Gottman advised a break of at least 20 minutes spent doing something calming, rather than rehearsing grievances, before returning to the discussion.

Repair attempts and bids. Stable couples are not couples who never slip; they are couples who recover. A repair attempt is any statement or action, silly or serious, that stops negativity from escalating: "Can we start again?", a joke, a touch on the arm, "I'm sorry, that came out wrong." In stable couples repair attempts are made and, crucially, accepted. Gottman also studied the small moments of everyday life. A bid is any attempt by one partner to get the other's attention, interest or affection ("Look at that bird"; "Guess who called today"). The other can turn toward the bid (respond with interest), turn away (ignore it) or turn against it (respond with irritation). In Gottman's six-year follow-up of newlywed couples, those who stayed married had turned toward each other's bids about 86 percent of the time, and those who divorced only about 33 percent of the time.

Perpetual problems and accepting influence. Gottman reported that most couples' conflicts (69 percent, in his estimate) are perpetual problems, rooted in lasting differences of personality or values, which never get solved. Happy couples do not solve them either; they keep talking about them with humour and affection instead of becoming gridlocked, a conclusion strikingly close to IBCT's acceptance. And in a study of newlyweds he found that husbands who accepted influence from their wives, letting their wives' views shape decisions rather than resisting them, were less likely to divorce.

Gottman and his wife, the clinical psychologist Julie Schwartz Gottman, turned these findings into Gottman Method Couples Therapy, and in 1996 they founded the Gottman Institute in Seattle to train therapists. Its model of a healthy relationship is the Sound Relationship House, shown in Figure 7.13 and popularised in The Seven Principles for Making Marriage Work (1999, with Nan Silver). The house has seven floors and two load-bearing walls. The ground floor is love maps: detailed knowledge of the partner's inner world, his worries, hopes, friends and daily stresses. Above it come sharing fondness and admiration (the antidote to contempt), turning towards instead of away (responding to bids), and the positive perspective, in which a store of goodwill leads each partner to read the other's neutral or ambiguous acts charitably (Robert Weiss had called this positive sentiment override). Only then comes managing conflict, which means not solving every problem but using gentle start-up, repair, self-soothing and compromise, and living with perpetual problems through dialogue. The top floors are making life dreams come true (Gottman found that gridlocked conflicts often hide an unspoken dream) and creating shared meaning through rituals, roles, goals and symbols. The walls holding the house up are trust and commitment.

How good is the research? Its observational detail is unmatched, and many of its findings, especially the corrosive effect of contempt and the importance of positive interaction, have held up. But the most famous claim, that Gottman can predict divorce with over 90 percent accuracy, needs care. In 2001 Richard Heyman and Amy Smith Slep pointed out that these accuracy figures came from fitting a statistical model to couples whose outcomes were already known. A model built and tested on the same small sample always looks better than it is; true prediction requires cross-validation, applying the model to a new sample, and that had not been done. The research is also correlational: contempt predicts divorce, but that does not prove that teaching couples to avoid contempt will prevent it. Gottman Method Couples Therapy itself has fewer randomised trials behind it than behavioural or emotionally focused couple therapy.

Exam trap

The four horsemen are criticism, contempt, defensiveness and stonewalling, and contempt is the strongest single predictor of divorce. "Anger", "complaint" and "conflict" are not horsemen: anger and complaints occur in happy marriages too. The 5 to 1 ratio of positive to negative interactions refers to behaviour during conflict in stable couples. Flooding (overwhelming physiological arousal) usually precedes stonewalling, and its antidote is self-soothing during a break. The Sound Relationship House has seven floors, from love maps at the bottom to shared meaning at the top, and two walls, trust and commitment. The research came from John Gottman and Robert Levenson; the therapy and the Gottman Institute (1996) are John and Julie Schwartz Gottman's.

The evidence, and couples therapy in India

Taken together, the trials give a consistent picture. Couples therapy works for relationship distress, with moderate effects; the main research-based approaches (behavioural, integrative behavioural, cognitive-behavioural and emotionally focused) produce broadly similar results when they are compared directly; many couples improve without becoming fully happy; and gains tend to fade over the years after therapy ends unless they are maintained. Table 7.19 sets the approaches side by side.

Table 7.19 The main approaches to couples therapy compared.
ApproachKey names and dateWhy couples are distressedMain methods
Traditional behavioural (TBCT)Jacobson and Margolin, 1979; StuartToo few rewards, too much coercion, poor skillsBehaviour exchange, caring days, contracts, communication and problem-solving training
Integrative behavioural (IBCT)Christensen and Jacobson, 1996Real differences, made worse by each partner's push to change the otherAcceptance (empathic joining, unified detachment, tolerance building) plus change
Emotionally focused (EFT)Johnson and Greenberg, 1985 and 1988; JohnsonThreatened attachment bond; a negative cycle driven by hidden emotionsTracking the cycle, accessing primary emotion, enactments; three stages, nine steps
Cognitive-behavioural (CBCT)Baucom and Epstein, 1990Distorted attributions, expectancies, assumptions and standardsTBCT methods plus cognitive restructuring
Gottman MethodJohn and Julie Gottman; research with LevensonThe four horsemen, poor repair, weak friendshipBuilding the Sound Relationship House: love maps, turning toward, gentle start-up, repair

Couples therapy also treats individual disorders. The best evidence concerns depression. Steven Beach and K. Daniel O'Leary argued that marital discord often precedes and maintains depression, especially in women, by removing support and adding daily criticism and conflict; if so, treating the marriage should treat the depression. In 1990 O'Leary and Beach randomly assigned 36 maritally discordant couples in which the wife was depressed to behavioural marital therapy, individual cognitive therapy for the wife, or a waiting list. Both therapies reduced depression substantially, but only marital therapy also improved marital satisfaction, and the difference held at one-year follow-up. A year later, Jacobson and his colleagues (1991) found the same pattern from the other side: for depressed wives in distressed marriages, behavioural marital therapy relieved depression as well as cognitive therapy did and was the only treatment that significantly improved the relationship, whereas for depressed wives in non-distressed marriages cognitive therapy worked better. The lesson is a matching rule: when marital discord is a central part of what keeps a depression going, couples therapy is the treatment of choice. The same logic underlies behavioural couples therapy for alcohol and drug problems, developed by Timothy O'Farrell and William Fals-Stewart, in which the partner joins a daily recovery contract; it has produced better abstinence and relationship outcomes, and less partner violence, than individual treatment alone.

Exam trap

"A male adult with depressive disorder and marital discord would benefit more from" marital and couple therapy (IHBAS 2020). Cognitive behaviour therapy is a good treatment for depression, but on its own it leaves the discord, the factor maintaining the depression, untouched; psychodynamic psychotherapy (Chapter 3) and supportive therapy (Section 3.10) share that limitation, because both treat the depressed individual rather than the relationship. The trials (O'Leary and Beach, 1990; Jacobson and colleagues, 1991) studied depressed wives, but the matching principle is the same for a depressed husband: when the relationship maintains the depression, treat the relationship.

Indian context

Couples therapy in India has to work with a different kind of marriage. Most marriages are still arranged, so partners may begin married life almost as strangers, and a marriage joins two families rather than two individuals. Many couples live in joint families, as Vikram and Ritika do, and the problem they bring is often about a mother-in-law, money sent to the husband's parents, or who decides how the child is raised. The in-laws may literally be in the room: parents sometimes bring a couple to therapy or insist on attending. A therapist cannot simply shut them out, because they may hold real power over the problem, but she must protect the couple's own subsystem (Minuchin's boundary between the spouses and the older generation, Section 7.4), seeing the couple alone for at least part of the work. Goals may differ too: harmony in the household and keeping the family together may matter more to a couple than individual romantic fulfilment, and a therapist who imposes other goals will lose them. Indian law builds counselling into marital disputes: the Family Courts Act, 1984 provides for counsellors attached to family courts and requires the court, in the first instance, to try to bring the parties to a settlement. Within psychiatry, the Family Psychiatry Centre at NIMHANS in Bengaluru pioneered family and marital therapy in Indian clinical settings. Couples often arrive indirectly: through a child's problem, through a gynaecologist or a sex clinic (Section 7.9), or after a complaint of dowry harassment or domestic violence, in which case safety and the law, including the Protection of Women from Domestic Violence Act, 2005, come before any joint work.

Clinical link

In an interview you may be asked which couples therapy you would choose. A good answer starts from the formulation rather than the brand name. If the couple are fairly well connected but stuck on practical problems, behavioural skills training is quick and effective. If each is trying to change a partner who cannot or will not change, acceptance work (IBCT) is the core. If the fights are fuelled by hurt, fear and a sense that the partner is not there, emotionally focused work on the cycle is the natural fit. Whatever you choose, listen for Gottman's horsemen, especially contempt, and always screen each partner alone for violence before you begin.

7.9 Sex TherapyLower yield · not asked yet

In 1957 a gynaecologist at Washington University in St Louis, William Howell Masters (1915 to 2001), took on a research assistant. Virginia Eshelman Johnson (1925 to 2013) had no university degree, but she had a gift for putting people at ease, and Masters needed exactly that, because he intended to do something no scientist had done systematically before: to observe and measure what the human body does during sex, in a laboratory. Over the next eight years the two of them recorded the responses of several hundred volunteers. Their first book, Human Sexual Response (1966), was written in deliberately dry medical prose and became a bestseller anyway. Their second, Human Sexual Inadequacy (1970), turned the laboratory findings into a treatment, and within a decade it had changed how sexual problems were treated around the world.

Before them, sexual problems were treated, when they were treated at all, in one of two ways. Psychoanalysts saw a man's impotence or a woman's "frigidity" (an old word, now rejected) as the surface sign of a deep unconscious conflict, to be explored over years of analysis. Behaviour therapists of the 1950s and 1960s had begun to treat sexual anxieties with desensitisation; Joseph Wolpe used sexual arousal itself as one of the responses that inhibit anxiety (Section 4.2). And Alfred Kinsey's surveys of 1948 and 1953 had shown how varied ordinary sexual behaviour is. Masters and Johnson's contribution was a brief, direct, couple-based therapy aimed at the anxiety and ignorance that keep most sexual problems going. In this book sex therapy means psychological treatment for problems of sexual function or satisfaction, usually brief, usually with the couple, and built around structured homework. This section describes Masters and Johnson's model and programme, the techniques that grew from them, the revisions of Helen Singer Kaplan, the PLISSIT model that tells any clinician how far to go, and the shape sex therapy takes in India. (The sexual dysfunctions themselves, their criteria and their causes, belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book; here they are named only as the targets of each technique.)

The sexual response cycle

Key study: Human Sexual Response (Masters and Johnson, 1966)

Question. What actually happens in the human body during sexual arousal and orgasm? Method. From 1957 to 1965 Masters and Johnson recruited volunteers in St Louis and observed and measured them in the laboratory during masturbation and intercourse, recording heart rate, blood pressure, breathing, muscle tension and the changes in the genitals, including, by means of a camera inside a transparent artificial penis, changes inside the vagina. They observed 382 women and 312 men and estimated, conservatively, that they had recorded 10,000 complete cycles of sexual response. Results. Despite the obvious anatomical differences, men and women passed through the same sequence of four phases, driven by the same two physiological processes. Conclusion. Sexual response is an orderly physiological sequence that can be described, and when it goes wrong, retrained. Criticism. The volunteers were self-selected and, by design, people who could respond reliably under observation in a laboratory, and the pilot work had used sex workers; how far the findings generalise is uncertain. Critics such as the psychologist Leonore Tiefer later argued that by presenting genital physiology as a universal sequence, the model left out desire, meaning and the relationship, and set a single performance standard against which many people would then judge themselves.

The two physiological processes are worth naming. Vasocongestion is the swelling of tissues as blood flows into them faster than it flows out; it produces erection of the penis, swelling of the clitoris and labia and lubrication of the vagina. Myotonia is the rise in muscle tension throughout the body, which peaks in the involuntary contractions of orgasm. Masters and Johnson divided the sexual response cycle into four phases (Figure 7.14).

  1. Excitement. Arousal begins in response to physical or psychological stimulation. In men the penis becomes erect; in women the vagina lubricates (fluid seeps through the engorged vaginal walls), the clitoris and labia swell and the inner part of the vagina lengthens and widens. In both sexes the nipples may become erect, and heart rate and blood pressure rise.
  2. Plateau. Arousal levels off at a high level, for anything from seconds to many minutes. In women the outer third of the vagina swells to form what Masters and Johnson called the orgasmic platform, and the clitoris draws back under its hood; in men the testes rise and enlarge, and a few drops of fluid may appear at the tip of the penis.
  3. Orgasm. The shortest phase: a series of rhythmic contractions of the pelvic muscles, at first at intervals of about 0.8 seconds in both sexes, with peak heart rate and breathing and an intense release of tension. In men orgasm is accompanied by ejaculation.
  4. Resolution. The body returns to its unaroused state. Men enter a refractory period, during which further erection or orgasm is not possible; it lasts minutes in young men and can stretch to hours or longer with age. Women have no refractory period and, if stimulation continues, may return to orgasm.

Masters and Johnson described one pattern for men and three for women. In pattern A a woman may have repeated orgasms, returning to orgasm from the plateau level without dropping back; in pattern B she has a long plateau without orgasm and a slow resolution; in pattern C she rises rapidly through excitement to an intense orgasm and resolves quickly. Figure 7.14 also previews a later revision, Helen Singer Kaplan's three-phase model, which you will meet in Section 7.9.5.

Exam trap

Masters and Johnson's four phases, in order, are excitement, plateau, orgasm and resolution; there is no desire phase in their model. Desire was added by Helen Singer Kaplan, whose triphasic model is desire, excitement and orgasm. The two physiological processes are vasocongestion (blood engorgement) and myotonia (muscle tension). The refractory period belongs to the male resolution phase. Human Sexual Response (1966) is the physiology book; Human Sexual Inadequacy (1970) is the therapy book.

Masters and Johnson's therapy programme

The treatment described in Human Sexual Inadequacy rested on a handful of principles that were radical in 1970 and are now so standard that they are easy to overlook.

  • The couple is the patient. Masters and Johnson held that in a sexual problem there is no such thing as an uninvolved partner. Whatever the origin of a man's erectile difficulty, his partner is part of the pattern that keeps it going, and both must take part in treatment. They treated the relationship, not one person's body.
  • A dual-sex therapy team. Every couple was treated by a dual-sex therapy team, a male and a female co-therapist working together (Masters and Johnson were the original pair). Each partner then had a therapist of the same sex who could understand his or her experience and, when needed, interpret it to the other; neither partner faced two therapists of the opposite sex; and the therapists modelled open communication between a man and a woman.
  • A rapid, intensive format. Couples came to St Louis, often from far away, and stayed for two weeks of daily sessions, free of work, children and relatives. The first days were given to detailed histories, each partner interviewed first by the same-sex therapist and then by the other, and to a medical examination. Then, at a round-table meeting, the four of them reviewed what the therapists had understood about the problem, and the homework began.
  • Education. A great deal of time went to plain information about anatomy and sexual response, and to dismantling myths: that a man must always be ready for sex, that a woman's arousal should follow the same course and timing as a man's, that intercourse is the only "real" sex.
  • Removing performance anxiety. Most important of all, they identified the immediate cause that keeps most sexual dysfunctions going: performance anxiety, the fear of failing sexually, which itself makes failure more likely. With it goes spectatoring: during sex, the anxious person mentally steps outside and watches himself or herself like a spectator ("Is it working? Is it going to fail again?"), monitoring and judging instead of feeling. Spectatoring kills arousal, failure confirms the fear, and the cycle tightens with every attempt. Viktor Frankl had described the same trap as hyper-reflection (Section 6.5).

The results Masters and Johnson reported were remarkable. Across more than 790 people treated in their programme they reported, characteristically, failure rates rather than success rates, and overall about four in five responded within the two weeks. Almost all the men treated for premature ejaculation recovered, whereas lifelong erectile problems were the hardest, with success closer to 60 percent. Specialist clinics modelled on St Louis opened across the United States and then around the world.

Later research was less kind. In 1980 Bernie Zilbergeld and Michael Evans argued, in an article pointedly titled "The inadequacy of Masters and Johnson", that the book never clearly defined success or failure and that its couples were highly selected. Clinics elsewhere, using similar methods, never matched the St Louis figures, perhaps partly because couples who had taken two weeks off and travelled to St Louis were exceptionally motivated. Several structural features of the programme also turned out to be unnecessary: one therapist proved as effective as two (LoPiccolo, Heiman, Hogan and Roberts, 1985), and weekly sessions as effective as daily ones (Heiman and LoPiccolo, 1983). What survived were the core ideas: treat the couple, educate, remove the demand to perform, and retrain the response step by step.

Two parts of the Masters and Johnson record are now remembered as warnings. For some single men who had no partner they arranged partner surrogates, women who were not therapists and who took part in the exercises with the client; the practice drew fierce ethical criticism and remains controversial. And from 1968 to 1977 their institute ran a programme to "convert" gay men and lesbians to heterosexuality, reported in Homosexuality in Perspective (1979). Its claimed success was doubted even by people close to the institute, and conversion efforts of every kind are now condemned by professional bodies as ineffective and harmful (Section 4.4 tells this history for aversion therapy).

Clinical link

In sex therapy every touching exercise is done by the partners, in private, at home. The therapist talks, educates and assigns tasks, and never touches or watches. This is not prudishness but the most basic rule of professional boundaries. A boundary crossing is a minor departure from the usual frame that may even help the client (a brief self-disclosure, a session held somewhere other than the office); a boundary violation is a departure that exploits or harms the client to meet the therapist's needs, and a romantic or sexual relationship with a current client is the textbook example, forbidden by every professional code (ethics is covered in Chapter 1). Clients who talk about sex in therapy are unusually exposed, which is why the rule matters most here.

Sensate focus

The centrepiece of the programme was sensate focus: a graded series of touching exercises, done by the couple at home, in which the demand to perform is removed and attention is turned to sensation. It begins with a ban. In the first stages intercourse, and at first even touching of the breasts and genitals, is forbidden. The ban sounds odd, but it is the active ingredient. A man cannot fail to have an erection when an erection is not required, and a woman cannot fail to reach orgasm when orgasm is not on the agenda. With failure impossible, performance anxiety has nothing to feed on, and the spectator has nothing to watch. Because nothing is demanded of the person being touched, the exercises are often called non-demand pleasuring. The stages are summarised in Table 7.20; books number them slightly differently, and the first two are often called sensate focus I and II.

Table 7.20 The stages of sensate focus.
StageWhat the couple doesRulesPurpose
1 Non-genital touchingPartners take turns touching, stroking and massaging each other's body, except the breasts and genitals; the receiver attends to sensations and later guides the giver's handIntercourse and genital touching banned; no demand for arousalRemove performance anxiety; rediscover sensual pleasure; learn to communicate likes
2 Genital touchingTouching now includes the breasts and genitals, still taking turnsStill no intercourse and no goal of orgasm; erection or lubrication may come and goArousal without demand; learning that arousal fades and returns
3 Non-demand intercourseUsually with the woman on top, the penis is inserted slowly and held still (containment); gentle movement is added graduallyEither partner may stop and return to touching at any pointIntercourse without pressure, built up in small steps
4 Full intercourseIntercourse with continued pleasuringAttention stays on sensation, not performanceCarry the gains into ordinary sexual life

Why does it work? First, it is in vivo desensitisation of sexual anxiety (Section 4.2), with a hierarchy that climbs from non-genital touching to intercourse and with pleasure itself as the response that competes with anxiety. Second, it redirects attention from self-monitoring to sensation, which is the cure for spectatoring and close to Frankl's dereflection (Section 6.5). Third, it forces communication. Partners have to say what they like, often for the first time, and in "hand-riding" the receiver lays a hand over the giver's to show the pressure and pace he or she enjoys, so that the message can be given without words. Fourth, it is diagnostic. Couples who "forget" the homework, break the ban, or quarrel before every exercise reveal the fears or relationship conflicts that the sexual problem may be protecting, and these then become the material of therapy.

Push a case through it. Siddharth, 29, and Pooja, 25, married eight months ago in an arranged marriage and live with Siddharth's parents and grandmother in Lucknow. Intercourse has never succeeded. On the wedding night Siddharth, anxious and exhausted after days of ceremonies, lost his erection; since then he lies awake before every attempt wondering whether it will happen again, and it does. Pooja received no sex education, has heard frightening stories about pain, and has come to dread the attempts herself. Relatives have begun asking about a grandchild. After a medical check, the therapist teaches them about the sexual response and talks frankly about the myths they carry (that the first night must succeed, that a "real man" is always ready). Then she bans intercourse completely and prescribes stage 1 exercises three times a week. The first problem is practical: privacy in a crowded flat. The couple agree to use the hours when Siddharth's parents take his grandmother to the temple on Sunday mornings, and to close the bedroom door without apology. Within three weeks Siddharth reports, almost embarrassed, that he had firm erections during the exercises; because nothing was expected, nothing failed. They move through the stages over two months, with Pooja in control of every step of penetration (the dilator work described in Section 7.9.4 helped her), and they consummate the marriage without the drama they had both feared.

Exam trap

Sensate focus is Masters and Johnson's (1970), not Kaplan's, Frankl's or Wolpe's, although it resembles desensitisation and dereflection. Its active ingredient is the ban on intercourse, which removes the demand to perform. The order is non-genital touching, then genital touching, then non-demand intercourse, then full intercourse. It targets performance anxiety and spectatoring (anxious self-monitoring during sex). The other hallmarks of the Masters and Johnson programme are the couple as the patient, the dual-sex therapy team and the two-week daily format.

Techniques for specific problems

Sensate focus is the common base of sex therapy. On top of it, therapists add techniques aimed at particular problems.

Premature (early) ejaculation. The oldest behavioural technique came from the American urologist James Semans, who in 1956 published "Premature ejaculation: a new approach". In the stop-start technique the partner stimulates the man's penis until he feels that ejaculation is approaching, and he signals her to stop. They wait until the sensation subsides, then begin again, repeating the cycle several times before he allows himself to ejaculate. Over weeks the man learns to recognise the sensations that come before the point of no return and gains control over them, and the exercise then moves to intercourse, first without movement and then with it. Masters and Johnson modified the method into the squeeze technique. Instead of simply stopping, the partner firmly squeezes the penis for a few seconds just below the head, with her thumb on the underside and two fingers on the upper side; the urge to ejaculate passes and the erection partly subsides, and after a short pause stimulation resumes. A later variant, the basal squeeze, applies pressure at the base of the penis so that it can be used during intercourse. Helen Singer Kaplan preferred the simpler stop-start method, and many therapists now use it in place of the squeeze. Both work well at first, with 60 to 90 percent of men improving, but the gains often fade, to about a quarter after three years or more, and medication that delays ejaculation is now often combined with them.

Kegel exercises. The pelvic floor exercises published in 1948 by the gynaecologist Arnold Kegel (Section 4.8.5 describes his perineometer, an early form of biofeedback) train voluntary control of the pubococcygeus muscle, the sling of muscle around the vagina and urethra. Kegel exercises consist of repeatedly contracting and relaxing this muscle, which a person can locate by stopping the flow of urine midstream. In sex therapy they are used to increase a woman's awareness of genital sensation in orgasm problems, to show a woman with vaginismus that she can deliberately tighten and, more importantly, relax the muscles around the vagina, and in men as part of training in ejaculatory control.

Vaginal dilators for vaginismus. In vaginismus, now part of genito-pelvic pain/penetration disorder in DSM-5, involuntary tightening of the muscles around the vagina and fear of pain make penetration difficult or impossible. Treatment is graded exposure under the woman's own control. After education, relaxation and Kegel training, she inserts a series of vaginal dilators (smooth cylinders of graduated sizes), or her own fingers, starting with the smallest, at her own pace and only when relaxed, leaving each in place until the discomfort fades. Later her partner inserts them under her guidance, and finally, with the woman on top and in charge, the penis. All this happens within the sensate focus programme, so that arousal is maintained, and because the fear is sometimes linked to earlier sexual abuse, the therapist watches for distress and goes slowly. Success rates are high: in the studies reviewed by Barlow and Durand, 80 to 100 percent of women overcome the problem, often within a short time.

Directed masturbation for lifelong orgasm problems. For a woman who has never had an orgasm, Joseph LoPiccolo and W. Charles Lobitz (1972) developed directed masturbation, a graded programme in which she first learns about her own body alone and then brings what she has learned into the relationship. The steps move from looking at her body and genitals with a mirror, through exploring by touch to find the areas that give pleasure, to focused self-stimulation for longer and more intense periods, with fantasy, erotic material or a vibrator where needed. Then her partner watches, then he stimulates her as she has shown him, and finally the stimulation is combined with intercourse. The programme was set out for women themselves in Becoming Orgasmic (1976) by Julia Heiman, Leslie LoPiccolo and Joseph LoPiccolo, and Lonnie Barbach ran similar groups for "pre-orgasmic" women. It is among the most successful of all sex therapy techniques, with 70 to 90 percent of women benefiting. To carry the gains into intercourse, therapists use what is called the bridge manoeuvre, usually credited to Kaplan: the clitoris is stimulated manually during intercourse until the woman is close to orgasm, and the manual stimulation is withdrawn earlier and earlier, so that intercourse itself increasingly carries her the rest of the way.

Medication. Since sildenafil (Viagra) was approved for erectile dysfunction in the United States in 1998, drug treatment has transformed the management of erectile problems, and sex therapy now increasingly works alongside medicine rather than instead of it. A tablet can restore an erection; it cannot remove performance anxiety, teach a couple to talk or repair a relationship, which is why combined treatment often does best. (The drugs, and the physical causes of dysfunction, belong to the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

Exam trap

The stop-start technique is James Semans's (1956); the squeeze technique is Masters and Johnson's modification of it. Be careful: some textbooks, Barlow and Durand among them, describe "a procedure developed by Semans (1956), sometimes called the squeeze technique", so an item may credit Semans with the squeeze. If both names appear as separate options, pair stop-start with Semans and squeeze with Masters and Johnson. Both treat premature ejaculation. Directed masturbation (LoPiccolo and Lobitz, 1972) treats lifelong female orgasm problems; vaginal dilators treat vaginismus; Kegel exercises (1948) train the pubococcygeus muscle; the bridge manoeuvre is usually credited to Kaplan.

Helen Singer Kaplan and the new sex therapy

The most influential revision of Masters and Johnson came from New York. Helen Singer Kaplan (1929 to 1995) was born in Vienna, trained both as a psychologist and as a psychiatrist, and built her career in New York. At Cornell's medical college and the Payne Whitney Clinic she founded the first clinic for sexual disorders at an American medical school. In The New Sex Therapy (1974) she argued that Masters and Johnson's behavioural tasks and psychoanalytic understanding were not rivals but partners.

Kaplan distinguished two levels of cause. The immediate causes of a sexual dysfunction are what is happening in the bedroom now: performance anxiety, spectatoring, a partner's demands, poor communication, ignorance of effective technique. The deeper or remote causes are conflicts within the person (guilt about sex, fear of intimacy, conflicts left over from childhood) and conflicts in the relationship (anger, power struggles, mistrust). Her treatment, known as brief sex therapy or psychosexual therapy, worked on both levels. The couple carried out prescribed sexual tasks at home, sensate focus and the specific techniques, and came to weekly outpatient sessions, usually with a single therapist rather than a dual-sex team, while carrying on their ordinary lives. In the sessions the therapist reviewed the tasks, and when a couple avoided them or reacted to them with anxiety or anger, she used psychodynamic exploration to understand the resistance before returning to the tasks. Her principle was to use the least intensive intervention that worked: behavioural tasks first, and psychodynamic work only to the depth needed to get past an obstacle. Many couples needed only the tasks; some had to work through deep material before the tasks would work at all.

Kaplan's second great contribution concerned desire. In practice she kept meeting patients whose problem was not erection, lubrication or orgasm but a lack of interest in sex altogether, and they did not fit Masters and Johnson's model, which began with excitement. In Disorders of Sexual Desire (1979) she proposed a triphasic model of sexual response: desire, excitement and orgasm, three phases that are related but can be disturbed independently, so that each has its own family of dysfunctions. She thus added desire before arousal and treated plateau and resolution as parts of the other phases rather than as phases of their own. She also found desire problems the hardest to treat, often rooted in deeper conflict or depression. Her model shaped psychiatric classification: from DSM-III (1980) onwards the sexual dysfunctions were organised by phase of the response cycle, beginning with desire, and later editions described the cycle as desire, excitement, orgasm and resolution, joining Kaplan's first phase to Masters and Johnson's last. Table 7.21 sets the two approaches side by side.

Table 7.21 Masters and Johnson compared with Helen Singer Kaplan.
FeatureMasters and JohnsonHelen Singer Kaplan
Key booksHuman Sexual Response (1966); Human Sexual Inadequacy (1970)The New Sex Therapy (1974); Disorders of Sexual Desire (1979)
Model of responseFour phases: excitement, plateau, orgasm, resolutionThree phases: desire, excitement, orgasm
Causes addressedImmediate: performance anxiety, spectatoring, ignorance, poor communicationImmediate causes plus deeper intrapsychic and relationship causes
TherapistsDual-sex co-therapy teamUsually a single therapist
FormatDaily sessions for two weeks, away from homeWeekly outpatient sessions; the couple stays in ordinary life
MethodsEducation, sensate focus, squeeze technique, dilatorsBehavioural tasks plus psychodynamic work on resistance; stop-start technique

Later researchers questioned whether any single linear sequence fits everyone. In 2000 the physician Rosemary Basson of the University of British Columbia proposed a circular model of women's sexual response, especially in long-term relationships. Many women, she argued, do not begin with spontaneous desire. They start from sexual neutrality, choose to engage for reasons of intimacy and closeness, and experience desire responsively, once arousal has begun; a satisfying experience then strengthens the motivation for the next time. Her purpose was partly to stop clinicians from diagnosing a dysfunction when a woman's response is simply different from the traditional sequence.

Exam trap

Kaplan's triphasic model has desire, excitement and orgasm (no plateau, no resolution); Masters and Johnson's has excitement, plateau, orgasm and resolution (no desire). The DSM sequence of desire, excitement, orgasm and resolution combines the two. Kaplan's brief sex therapy combines behavioural tasks with psychodynamic exploration of resistance, usually with one therapist and weekly sessions; Masters and Johnson's was a behavioural and educational programme with a dual-sex team over two weeks. The New Sex Therapy (1974) is Kaplan's. The circular model built around responsive desire is Rosemary Basson's (2000).

Annon's PLISSIT model

Most people with sexual worries never see a sex therapist. They mention the worry, often in passing, to a general practitioner, a gynaecologist, a nurse, a counsellor or a psychologist who is seeing them for something else. How far should that professional go? In 1976 the American psychologist Jack S. Annon proposed an answer in the form of a graded model named after the initials of its four levels: the PLISSIT model, for Permission, Limited Information, Specific Suggestions and Intensive Therapy.

  1. Permission. By a calm, non-judgmental attitude and by what he says, the professional conveys that the client's thoughts, feelings, fantasies and behaviours are normal and acceptable (provided they harm no one), and that talking about sex here is allowed. For a student who is ashamed that he masturbates and fears that he is damaging himself, hearing a doctor say quietly that masturbation is a normal behaviour of most men and women may be all that he needs. Permission can also be permission not to do something: a woman does not have to want sex as often as a magazine says she should.
  2. Limited Information. The professional gives specific facts directly relevant to the concern, not a lecture: that masturbation does not cause weakness or impotence, that nocturnal emissions are normal, that penis size has little to do with a partner's satisfaction, that erections become slower with age, or that a particular medicine can affect sexual function.
  3. Specific Suggestions. After taking a brief sexual history, the professional gives specific suggestions aimed at the particular problem: sensate focus exercises, the stop-start technique, a change of position, a lubricant, setting aside unhurried time for intimacy. This level needs training in sex therapy.
  4. Intensive Therapy. When the first three levels have not helped, or the problem is bound up with deeper individual or relationship difficulties (trauma, serious conflict, depression, a complex dysfunction), the client is referred for intensive treatment by a specialist, which may be sex therapy, couples therapy, individual psychotherapy or medical care.

The model works like a funnel. Most people's needs are met at the first two levels, fewer need the third, and only a minority need the fourth; each level requires more knowledge, skill and time than the one before, so that every professional can work at the level of his or her competence and refer onwards beyond it. Its great virtue is that it makes sexual health part of ordinary care, rather than something only specialists may mention. In the 2000s Bridget Taylor and Sally Davis proposed an extended version, the Ex-PLISSIT model, for use with people living with illness or disability: permission-giving is placed at the core of every level rather than only at the start, and the professional reviews and reflects on each conversation. Table 7.22 summarises the original model.

Table 7.22 Annon's PLISSIT model (1976).
LevelWhat the professional doesExampleWho can offer it
P: PermissionConveys that the client's feelings and behaviour are normal and that sex can be discussed"Many people worry about this; it is a normal thing to ask about."Any health professional
LI: Limited InformationGives brief, specific facts relevant to the concernExplaining that nocturnal emissions do not cause weaknessAny trained professional
SS: Specific SuggestionsTakes a sexual history and gives targeted behavioural suggestionsPrescribing sensate focus or the stop-start techniqueA professional trained in sex therapy
IT: Intensive TherapyRefers for specialist treatment of complex or deep-rooted problemsReferral for sex, couples or individual therapyA specialist
Exam trap

PLISSIT is Jack Annon's (1976), and its levels, in order, are Permission, Limited Information, Specific Suggestions and Intensive Therapy. "IT" is Intensive Therapy, not "integrated treatment" or "individual therapy"; "LI" is limited information, not a full course of sex education. The levels are graded: most clients need only the first two, each level needs more expertise, and Intensive Therapy usually means referral. Do not mix it up with Kaplan's triphasic model or Masters and Johnson's four phases, which are models of the sexual response, not of how far a clinician should intervene.

Sex therapy in India

Sex therapy in India meets a particular landscape of beliefs. The best known is captured by dhat syndrome, a term introduced by the Indian psychiatrist Narendra Nath Wig in 1960 for the common presentation of young men who attribute weakness, fatigue, poor concentration, aches, worries about sexual performance and low mood to the loss of semen, which they believe they see as a whitish discharge in the urine or lose through masturbation or nocturnal emissions ("nightfall"). The belief draws on a popular reading of Ayurveda, in which semen (shukra) is the last and most concentrated of the seven dhatus, the essential bodily substances distilled from food through blood, flesh, fat, bone and marrow, so that losing it seems to drain the body's strength. DSM-5-TR lists dhat syndrome in its glossary of cultural concepts of distress and describes it not as a discrete disorder but as a cultural explanation of distress; it notes estimates that 64 percent of men attending psychiatric clinics in India for sexual complaints present in this way, and that comparable worries about white vaginal discharge occur among women. The people who present with dhat often have treatable depression or anxiety, which a clinician who simply says "there is nothing wrong with you" will miss. (Chapter 2 discusses idioms of distress and explanatory models; the syndrome itself belongs to the Psychiatric Disorders book.)

Dhat is one member of a wider family of sexual myths that Indian clinicians meet every week: that masturbation causes weakness, impotence or a bent penis; that a man's sexual power is fixed in quantity and can be used up; that a small penis cannot satisfy a woman; that a bride must bleed on the wedding night. Many men first take these fears to self-styled sexologists who advertise on walls, in newspapers and online, promising cures for "night discharge" and "weakness", and who sell remedies that reinforce the very beliefs causing the distress. Many marriages are arranged and many couples marry with little accurate sex education, so clinics regularly see couples whose marriage remains unconsummated months or years after the wedding, usually because of a combination of the husband's performance anxiety and the wife's fear of pain or vaginismus, as with Siddharth and Pooja. The standard programme of education, sensate focus and graded dilators works well for them. Practical adaptations matter: privacy for homework in a joint family, the involvement of the extended family's expectations about children, and the fact that women are less likely to raise sexual concerns directly, often presenting instead to a gynaecologist with pain or a "white discharge".

Clinical link

PLISSIT fits the Indian sexual health clinic almost perfectly. A 22-year-old man arrives convinced that years of masturbation have drained his semen and caused his fatigue. Permission: take the worry seriously, listen to his explanation without ridicule, and say that masturbation is a normal behaviour. Limited information: explain, with a simple diagram, what semen is, that the body goes on producing it, that the whitish discharge he sees is usually harmless, and that his tiredness has other causes worth exploring. Many men need nothing more. If he also has depressive symptoms, treat or refer for them; if he has a specific problem such as early ejaculation, move to specific suggestions such as the stop-start technique; and if the anxiety is part of a wider disorder or a troubled marriage, refer for intensive therapy.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

7.10 Group PsychotherapyMedium yield · 4 Qs

Picture a room on the second floor of a hospital outpatient block in Bengaluru on a Tuesday evening. Eight chairs stand in a circle. In them sit a retired bank manager who has not left his flat for a year except to come here, a college student who cuts her arms, a young father who flies into rages at work, a widow who cannot stop crying, a software engineer who has never had a friend he trusts, and three others. Two psychologists sit among them, not at the front. For the first ten minutes nobody says anything that matters. Then the student mentions, almost in passing, that she has not told anyone about her arms, and the widow says quietly, "I thought I was the only one who hides things." Something shifts in the room.

The families and couples of Sections 7.1 to 7.9 were groups that existed before the therapist arrived and would go on existing after the therapist left. A therapy group is different. Its members are strangers, brought together for the sole purpose of helping one another change, and the relationships they form inside the room are the main instrument of that change. Group psychotherapy is the treatment of psychological problems in which a small number of selected people meet regularly with one or two trained therapists, and in which the interactions among the members, not only the interventions of the therapist, are used to bring about change. Notice the distinction hidden in that definition. A lecture to thirty patients is therapy in a group; group psychotherapy proper is therapy through a group. This section traces how clinicians discovered the difference, explains Irvin Yalom's account of why groups help, and then turns to the practical questions every group leader must answer: who should be in the group, how it should be run, how groups develop and what to do with members who make the work difficult.

From tuberculosis classes to the Second World War

The story usually begins in Boston in July 1905. Joseph Hersey Pratt (1872 to 1956) was a physician, trained under William Osler at Johns Hopkins, who was troubled by the fate of poor patients with tuberculosis. The standard treatment was months of rest, fresh air and good food in a sanatorium, which the poor of Boston's tenements could not afford. With money and meeting space provided by Emmanuel Church and its rector Elwood Worcester, Pratt gathered these patients into weekly meetings that he called "classes". He taught them the rules of home treatment, checked the diaries in which they recorded their weight, temperature and hours spent resting outdoors, and asked members who were improving to describe how they had done it. Pratt called his approach the class method, or home sanatorium treatment. What struck him over the following years was that his lectures were not the most powerful part of the treatment. The patients' encouragement of one another, the hope they drew from seeing others recover, and the sense of belonging to a class with a shared purpose seemed to matter as much as the medical instruction. Pratt preferred the word class to group, and his meetings were more education than psychotherapy, but he had stumbled on three of the therapeutic factors you will meet below: hope, universality and the sharing of information.

Through the 1920s and 1930s a scattering of psychiatrists in the United States borrowed the idea for their own patients. In Washington, Edward Lazell gave lectures to groups of patients with schizophrenia at St Elizabeths Hospital. L. Cody Marsh, a minister turned psychiatrist, ran lectures, classes and group activities for hospitalised patients under a memorable motto: "By the crowd they have been broken; by the crowd they shall be healed." The American psychoanalyst Trigant Burrow (1875 to 1950) went further and, from the mid-1920s, analysed people together in groups rather than alone, describing what he called the group method of analysis; he believed that much individual disturbance reflected the distortions of society, and later renamed his approach phyloanalysis. In New York hospitals in the 1930s, Louis Wender and Paul Schilder (1886 to 1940) ran psychoanalytically oriented groups for inpatients and outpatients, interpreting the members' feelings towards one another as transference.

Two men who disliked each other intensely gave the new field its names and its institutions. Jacob Levy Moreno (1889 to 1974), the Viennese psychiatrist who invented psychodrama (Section 7.11), claimed to have coined the terms group therapy and group psychotherapy, and in 1932 he presented his work on group psychotherapy to the American Psychiatric Association in Philadelphia. In 1942 he founded the society now called the American Society of Group Psychotherapy and Psychodrama. Samuel Richard Slavson (1890 to 1981), an engineer and educator turned therapist, began working in 1934 with groups of disturbed children at the Jewish Board of Guardians in New York. Finding that troubled children would not sit and talk, he gave them a room with tools, games and materials and a permissive adult, and let them work, play and quarrel together; he called this activity group therapy. In 1943 Slavson founded the American Group Therapy Association (the association's own emblem says 1942, so both years appear in books), later renamed the American Group Psychotherapy Association, which remains the main professional body for group therapists in the United States.

What turned these scattered experiments into a profession was the Second World War. The armies of Britain and the United States suffered enormous numbers of psychiatric casualties, soldiers with what was then called war neurosis or battle exhaustion, and there were far too few psychiatrists to treat them one at a time. Group treatment began as a necessity and was soon recognised as a method in its own right. The most famous experiments took place at Northfield Military Hospital, a converted mental hospital at Northfield in Birmingham, England, which became a military psychiatric hospital in 1942. In the first Northfield experiment (1942 to 1943), the psychoanalysts Wilfred Bion and John Rickman ran the hospital's training wing as a group that was expected to take responsibility for its own discipline and morale; the army, alarmed by the apparent disorder, closed the experiment after about six weeks. The second Northfield experiment, which followed, was led by S. H. Foulkes with Tom Main and Harold Bridger, and it went further: the whole hospital was treated as a community in which every activity could be therapeutic. Main gave that idea a name, the therapeutic community, which Section 7.11 takes up. Figure 7.15 sets out the milestones of the whole group movement, including the methods taught in the next section.

Exam trap

Match each pioneer to the right first. Joseph Pratt (Boston, 1905) ran the first deliberate therapeutic groups, classes for poor patients with tuberculosis, and is often called the father of group therapy in American textbooks. Jacob Moreno coined the term group psychotherapy (presented to the American Psychiatric Association in 1932) and founded psychodrama. Samuel Slavson devised activity group therapy for children (1934) and founded the American Group Psychotherapy Association (1943; sometimes dated 1942). Trigant Burrow analysed patients in groups in the 1920s. S. H. Foulkes founded British group analysis. Irvin Yalom came much later (1970); he systematised group therapy but did not found it.

Bion and Foulkes: the group as a whole

Two of the Northfield psychoanalysts went on to build theories that treat the group itself, not just the individuals in it, as the thing to be understood. Wilfred Bion (1897 to 1979) you have met in Chapter 3 as the Kleinian analyst who described the mother as a container for her infant's unbearable feelings. After the war he ran small groups at the Tavistock Clinic in London, and the papers he wrote about them in the late 1940s were collected in Experiences in Groups (1961). Bion's groups were deliberately unstructured. He sat with them, refused to lead in the ordinary way, and commented only on what the group as a whole seemed to be doing. What he saw was that every group functions on two levels at once.

On the first level is the work group: the group that meets to do its real task (to understand itself, to make a decision, to treat patients, to plan a festival), works rationally and cooperatively, puts up with frustration and learns from experience. On the second level, operating outside awareness, the group behaves as if it had come together for an entirely different purpose. Bion called these shared unconscious fantasies basic assumptions, and he described three of them.

  • Dependency. The group behaves as if it has met to be looked after by a leader who knows everything and will provide all the answers. Members sit back, wait for the therapist to speak and treat each of the therapist's remarks as wisdom; when the leader fails to rescue them, they feel cheated and look for someone else to depend on.
  • Fight-flight. The group behaves as if it has met to fight or to run away from an enemy, and it follows whoever offers to lead the fight or the escape. Fight shows itself as quarrelling, attacks on an outsider or on one member; flight shows itself as changing the subject, joking, lateness, absence and endless talk about matters outside the room.
  • Pairing. The group behaves as if it has met so that two of its members will produce something (an idea, a plan, a new leader, almost a messiah) that will save it. The atmosphere is hopeful, and the group watches with interest as two members talk to each other. The hope must stay in the future; if the saviour actually arrived, the hope would die.

Imagine the staff of a psychiatric ward in Mysuru holding a meeting to plan a new rehabilitation programme. In dependency, everyone waits for the head of department to announce what will happen. In fight-flight, the meeting becomes a complaint session about the hospital administration, and the actual plan is never discussed. In pairing, two enthusiastic junior doctors start sketching a scheme while everyone else watches hopefully and does nothing. In each case the basic assumption serves to relieve the group's anxiety, and in each case it stops the work group from working. Bion's ideas became the foundation of the Tavistock tradition of group relations training and of much organisational consulting, and his style of commenting on the group as a whole ("the group seems to be waiting for me to tell it what to do") remains a recognisable school of group therapy.

S. H. Foulkes (born Siegmund Heinrich Fuchs; 1898 to 1976) was a German psychoanalyst who left Frankfurt for England in 1933 after the Nazis came to power. He ran his first therapy groups at Exeter, worked at Northfield during the war, founded the Group Analytic Society in London in 1952 and inspired the Institute of Group Analysis, set up in 1971 to train practitioners. His method, group analysis, is a psychoanalytic group therapy that he summed up as psychotherapy by the group, of the group, including its conductor. Several of its terms are worth knowing. Foulkes called the therapist the conductor rather than the leader, because the therapist's job is to help the group communicate, not to direct it, much as a conductor helps an orchestra to play. Members are encouraged to say whatever comes to mind in a free-floating discussion, the group's equivalent of free association (Section 3.2.3). Over time the group builds up its matrix, the shared web of communication and relationship within which every member's contribution takes on meaning; Foulkes pictured each individual as a nodal point in this network. Members see aspects of themselves reflected in others (mirroring) and are stirred by others' material that touches their own (resonance). Group-analytic groups are usually of seven or eight members, meet once or twice a week for ninety minutes, run for years and take in new members as others leave.

Exam trap

Bion's three basic assumptions are dependency, fight-flight and pairing. The work group is not a fourth basic assumption; it is the rational, task-oriented level that the basic assumptions interfere with. Do not give the basic assumptions to Foulkes, whose terms are the conductor, the matrix, mirroring and resonance, and whose method is called group analysis. And keep group analysis (Foulkes) apart from Maxwell Jones's therapeutic community (Section 7.11), a pairing examiners like to offer as distractors for each other.

Yalom's eleven therapeutic factors

Bion and Foulkes explained what groups are like. The question a practising clinician most wants answered is a different one: when group therapy works, what is it that does the work? The most influential answer came from Irvin Yalom (born 1931), the Stanford psychiatrist whose existential psychotherapy you met in Section 6.4. In 1970 he published The Theory and Practice of Group Psychotherapy, which became the standard textbook of the field and has gone through many editions, the later ones written with the Canadian psychiatrist Molyn Leszcz. Earlier writers had tried to list the mechanisms of group therapy (in 1955 Raymond Corsini and Bina Rosenberg had distilled nine, including acceptance, universalisation, ventilation and spectator therapy), but Yalom's list, drawn from clinical observation, patients' own accounts and research, is the one every exam asks about. He first called them curative factors and later therapeutic factors, and in their standard form there are eleven.

  1. Instillation of hope. Members see others who have improved, and they come to believe that the group can help them too. Hope keeps people coming long enough for the other factors to work, and leaders build it deliberately, by preparing members before the group begins and by pointing out progress when it happens. In an open group, a member who has been there for a year and is doing better is living proof for the newcomer.
  2. Universality. Members discover that they are not alone: that others have the same fears, secrets, impulses and miseries. Many people arrive convinced that they are uniquely wretched, and the relief of hearing someone else say the unsayable is enormous. Yalom sometimes asked members to write down anonymously the secret they would least like to share; the commonest, by far, was a deep conviction of being inadequate, of being a fraud who would one day be found out. This is the factor the widow in the opening scene experienced.
  3. Imparting information. This includes didactic instruction by the therapist (about the nature of an illness, its treatment, how anxiety works) and direct advice from members. It is central in psychoeducational groups and in groups for a shared problem (Section 7.10.9). In interactional groups advice itself rarely helps much, but the act of giving it shows care and interest, and early meetings are full of it.
  4. Altruism. Members help one another, and being useful to someone else raises the helper's self-esteem. Demoralised patients often believe they have nothing to offer anyone; discovering that their words have helped another person can be the most powerful experience of the group. Group therapy is one of the few treatments in which the patient is also a healer.
  5. Corrective recapitulation of the primary family group. The group resembles a family in many ways, with authority figures (the therapists, sometimes a man and a woman) and siblings (the other members) who compete for attention. Members tend to relive their early family relationships within it, and the difference is that the old patterns can now be noticed, explored and ended differently. A man who was always overshadowed by a brilliant older brother may find himself bitterly resenting the most articulate member; this time, instead of withdrawing, he can say so and see what happens.
  6. Development of socialising techniques. Members learn basic social skills, either directly (in a social skills group, by role play and practice) or indirectly, through honest feedback that is rarely available in ordinary life: "When you look at the floor while you talk, I feel you don't want me to answer."
  7. Imitative behaviour. Members model themselves on the therapist and on other members, trying out ways of speaking or of handling feelings that they have watched work for someone else. A member may also benefit from watching another member with a similar problem being helped, which earlier writers called spectator therapy. This is observational learning in Albert Bandura's sense (Chapter 4).
  8. Interpersonal learning. Yalom regarded this as the most powerful and most distinctive factor of interactional groups, and Section 7.10.4 explains it fully. In brief, members display their typical ways of relating to others inside the group, receive feedback about the effect they have, and learn to relate differently.
