It builds on Harry Stack Sullivan's interpersonal theory (Section 3.8.7): we come to know ourselves through our relationships, and our distortions of other people can be corrected only by checking them against others' perceptions.
  9. Group cohesiveness. This is the members' sense of belonging, of being accepted and valued, of "we-ness"; it is the attraction the group holds for its members. Yalom saw cohesiveness as the group equivalent of the therapeutic relationship in individual therapy, and as the condition that makes the other factors possible: people take risks, disclose and hear hard feedback only in a group they trust. Cohesive groups have better attendance and fewer dropouts, and research has repeatedly found a modest but reliable link between cohesion and outcome.
  10. Catharsis. Members express strong feelings, positive and negative, that they have held back, often for years, and feel relief. But Yalom was emphatic that catharsis is not enough on its own: the release of emotion helps only when it happens within a supportive group and is followed by some understanding of what it means. Shouting in a room full of strangers changes nothing by itself.
  11. Existential factors. Members come to recognise that life is sometimes unfair, that there is no escape from some of life's pain and from death, that however close they are to others they must face life alone, and that they are ultimately responsible for the way they live it, however much help they receive. Yalom found that patients often rated these items highly, to his surprise, and they are the bridge to his later existential psychotherapy.

Table 7.23 condenses the list, with the kind of sentence a member might say for each. It helps to see that the factors are not all of the same kind. Some are conditions for change (cohesiveness, hope), some are mechanisms of change (interpersonal learning, imitative behaviour) and some are themselves changes (relief through catharsis, a new sense of responsibility). Their importance also shifts with the type of group and with time: hope, universality and information matter most in the early weeks and in short, structured groups, while interpersonal learning and cohesiveness matter most in long-term interactional groups.

Table 7.23 Yalom's eleven therapeutic factors, with what a member might say.
FactorCore ideaA member might say
Instillation of hopeSeeing others improve; belief that the group helps"If she got better, so can I."
UniversalityOthers share my problems; I am not alone"We are all in the same boat."
Imparting informationTeaching by the therapist; advice from members"Now I know what a panic attack is."
AltruismHelping others raises my own worth"Something I said actually helped him."
Corrective recapitulation of the primary family groupReliving family patterns and ending them differently"I react to him as I did to my elder brother."
Development of socialising techniquesLearning social skills through practice and feedback"I learnt how I come across."
Imitative behaviourModelling on the therapist and other members"I tried saying no the way she does."
Interpersonal learningFeedback on my relating; trying new ways in the group"Others honestly told me what they think of me."
Group cohesivenessBelonging, acceptance, trust in the group"This is the one place I am accepted."
CatharsisExpressing feelings held in"I finally said what was bothering me."
Existential factorsFacing responsibility, aloneness, unfairness, death"In the end, my life is up to me."
Key study: which factors do members value most? (Yalom, Tinklenberg and Gilula)

Question. Which of the therapeutic factors do patients themselves think helped them? Method. Yalom, with James Tinklenberg and Marshall Gilula, studied twenty successful patients who had completed long-term outpatient group therapy (on average about sixteen months) for neurotic and characterological problems. Each sorted sixty statements, five for each of twelve categories, from "most helpful to me" to "least helpful to me". (The twelve categories split interpersonal learning into input, learning from feedback, and output, learning to relate, and included self-understanding, which is why some books list twelve factors.) Results. The categories ranked highest were interpersonal learning through feedback (input), catharsis, cohesiveness and self-understanding; the lowest were family re-enactment, guidance and identification (imitation). Conclusion. Long-term outpatients credit their improvement above all to honest feedback, emotional expression within a cohesive group and new understanding of themselves. Criticism. The patients were few, all successful and all reporting retrospectively; the questionnaire contained more interpersonal items than others; and later studies show that rankings depend on the group. Inpatients and members of brief groups rate instillation of hope, universality and existential factors more highly, and members of self-help groups such as Alcoholics Anonymous stress hope, universality and altruism.

Exam trap

"The realisation that one is not alone in one's suffering" is universality. Do not confuse it with instillation of hope (seeing that others have improved), altruism (being helped by helping) or catharsis (expressing pent-up feeling). Curative factors and therapeutic factors are the same list under its older and newer names. Yalom's eleven group factors are not his four ultimate concerns of existential psychotherapy (death, freedom, isolation and meaninglessness, 1980; Section 6.4.4); the only overlap is that "existential factors" is one of the eleven. Options such as free association and interpretation (psychoanalysis, Chapter 3), token reinforcement and shaping (behaviour therapy, Chapter 4) or schema reconstruction and mode work (schema therapy, Section 5.7) are never Yalom's factors.

The group as a social microcosm: the here-and-now

Why should a group of strangers, who will never meet outside the room, be a good place to change how one relates to the people who matter? Yalom's answer is that a therapy group becomes, given time and freedom, a social microcosm: a miniature version of each member's social world. Members do not describe their interpersonal problems so much as act them out, in front of witnesses. Mahesh, who complains that nobody at home ever listens to him, talks over everyone in the group and turns every topic back to himself. Lata, who feels exploited by her family, rushes to comfort every member who is upset and never once asks for anything for herself. Anil, who cannot understand why his colleagues avoid him, makes sarcastic jokes whenever anyone in the group becomes serious. Within a few weeks the group has a front-row view of the very patterns that bring each member to therapy.

This makes possible the sequence of interpersonal learning. A member displays a characteristic pattern; other members react to it and, if the group's norms allow, say how it affects them; with the leader's help, the member comes to see the pattern, its effect on others and his or her own part in it; the member then tries out a different way of behaving in the relative safety of the group; and the new behaviour gradually carries over to relationships outside. In Sullivan's language, members correct their parataxic distortions (seeing others through the lens of earlier relationships) by consensual validation, checking their perceptions against those of several other people. Each time a member takes a risk and the expected disaster does not follow, he or she has the kind of corrective emotional experience that Franz Alexander described for individual therapy (Section 3.6.2).

For the social microcosm to do its work, the group must attend to the here-and-now: what is happening in the room, at this moment, between these people, rather than the there-and-then of members' past lives and outside relationships. Yalom described the here-and-now as working in two tiers. The first tier is experiencing: the leader steers members towards their immediate feelings about one another, about the leader and about the group ("How are you feeling towards Lata right now?"). The second tier is process illumination: the group steps back and examines what has just happened, why it happened and what it means, in what Yalom called a self-reflective loop. Both tiers are needed. Experiencing without illumination is an emotional event that teaches nothing, the trap of many encounter groups (Section 7.11.6); illumination without experiencing is an intellectual discussion with no feeling in it.

The word process here carries the meaning that Section 7.1 gave it for families. The content of a group discussion is what it is about (a member's divorce, a quarrel with the boss); the process is how it unfolds: who speaks, who interrupts, who rescues whom, who goes quiet and why the group keeps changing the subject whenever someone begins to cry. The leader's most characteristic intervention is a process comment that turns attention from content to process: "I notice that each time Shabnam starts talking about her mother, someone makes a joke. I wonder what that is about." Leaders also learn to bring outside material into the room. When a member says that his boss makes him feel invisible, the leader might ask, "Is there anyone here who makes you feel a little like that?" The here-and-now does not forbid talk of the past or of outside life; it uses that talk as a bridge back to the present, where change can be observed and practised.

Exam trap

Yalom's emphasis on the here-and-now means using the members' immediate interactions in the group, the social microcosm, as the engine of change: working with interpersonal patterns as they appear in the session and then illuminating them (experiencing, then process illumination). It does not mean avoiding all discussion of outside life (outside material is used as a bridge to the present), replacing individual therapy permanently, bypassing group dynamics (they are the whole point), discussing only events outside the group, limiting the group to didactic teaching, or disputing irrational beliefs (Albert Ellis's REBT, Section 5.2). Gestalt therapy also speaks of the here and now (Section 6.3), but there it means one person's present awareness; Yalom's here-and-now is interpersonal.

The leader's tasks

If the members do much of the healing, what does the leader do? Yalom described three basic tasks. The first is the creation and maintenance of the group. The leader is the group's gatekeeper: he or she selects and prepares the members, fixes the time, place and length of meetings, keeps the membership stable, works to prevent dropouts, and deals with anything that threatens the group's survival, such as persistent lateness, members meeting secretly outside the group, or one member being driven out as a scapegoat.

The second task is culture building. A group's most powerful influences on its members are its norms, the unwritten rules about what is done and not done, and once norms form they are hard to change. The leader tries to shape norms that serve therapy: that members speak honestly about their feelings, including feelings about one another; that they stay with the here-and-now; that they accept one another without judging; that the members, not just the leader, are the main source of help; and that the group itself is valued. Leaders build norms in two roles at once: as a technical expert, who explains and reinforces the rules, and as a model-setting participant, who demonstrates by example the honesty, attentiveness and non-defensive acceptance of feedback that they hope members will adopt. A degree of therapist transparency, judicious disclosure of the leader's own here-and-now reactions ("I find myself feeling shut out when the group jokes like this"), is one of the most effective ways to model these norms. The third task, already described, is activation and illumination of the here-and-now.

Many groups are led by two therapists, an arrangement called co-therapy (Section 7.6 described it in family work). Co-therapists can support each other, notice more, cover for each other's absences and model a respectful relationship in which disagreement is safe; a male and female pair can evoke parental feelings that feed the corrective recapitulation of the family. The risks are rivalry between the leaders, competition for the group's affection and members playing one leader off against the other, which is why co-therapists need time together after each session to talk through what happened.

The best evidence about what effective leaders actually do came from a study you met in Section 6.2.7: Encounter Groups: First Facts (1973) by Morton Lieberman, Irvin Yalom and Matthew Miles, who followed Stanford students through encounter groups of many different schools. Observers rated the leaders' behaviour, and four basic leader functions emerged. Emotional stimulation means challenging, confronting, revealing one's own feelings and pushing members to express theirs. Caring means offering support, warmth, protection and acceptance. Meaning attribution means explaining, clarifying and interpreting, giving members a framework for understanding what is happening to them. Executive function means setting limits, rules and goals and managing time and procedure. The most effective leaders were high in caring and in meaning attribution and moderate in emotional stimulation and executive function. Leaders very high in emotional stimulation, the charismatic, aggressive "energisers", produced the most casualties, and leaders with too little executive function ran chaotic groups. Notice what this says about catharsis: members who had intense emotional experiences without any framework for understanding them did not change, which is exactly why Yalom insisted that experiencing must be followed by illumination.

Clinical link

Confidentiality is harder to promise in a group. You, the professional, are bound by your code of ethics and the law (Chapter 1); the other members are not bound in the same way, and you cannot guarantee what they will do. In the pre-group interview, explain this honestly, make "what is said here stays here" a firm group rule, agree how members will handle chance meetings outside, and ask them to report any outside contact with one another to the group. In smaller Indian towns, where members may know each other's families, these conversations matter even more.

Composition, selection and preparation

A group can be no better than its membership, and most of a leader's decisions about membership are made before the first meeting. For a heterogeneous outpatient interactional group, Yalom's advice was that selection is mainly a matter of exclusion. People are unlikely to benefit, and may disrupt the group, if they cannot take part in its primary task: those who are acutely psychotic, actively misusing alcohol or drugs, in an acute suicidal crisis, severely cognitively impaired, intensely paranoid or antisocial. Many such people do well in specialised groups designed for them; what they need is a different group, not no group. Good candidates are motivated, have problems that are at least partly interpersonal (loneliness, shyness, difficulty with intimacy or anger) and can both give and receive feedback. The best single predictor of staying and benefiting is the member's attraction to the group. Leaders also avoid creating a lone group deviant, a member who differs sharply from everyone else (the only older person, the only member with a psychotic illness), because such members are the most likely to drop out.

The first design decision is the mix of members. A homogeneous group brings together people with the same problem or situation: a group for bereaved spouses, for women with breast cancer, for adolescents with eating disorders, for men with alcohol dependence. Homogeneous groups become cohesive quickly, offer immediate universality and support, attend well and suit brief, focused work, but they can stay superficial and offer less interpersonal learning. A heterogeneous group mixes people with different problems, backgrounds and interpersonal styles, as in the Bengaluru group at the start of this section. Heterogeneity makes for a richer social microcosm, more friction and more to learn, but cohesion takes longer. Yalom's rule of thumb was to seek heterogeneity in members' conflict areas and ways of coping and homogeneity in their degree of vulnerability, so that no member is far more fragile than the rest.

The second decision is whether membership will change. A closed group starts with a fixed set of members who begin and end together, usually for a fixed number of sessions, as in a twelve-session cognitive behavioural group; it develops cohesion steadily and passes through its stages together, but it shrinks as members drop out. An open group admits new members as places become free, so its membership changes continually, as in an inpatient ward group, a self-help meeting or a long-term outpatient group (when members join only occasionally, such groups are sometimes called slow-open). Open groups offer continuity and let newcomers learn from veterans, but each arrival and departure disturbs the group. Table 7.24 summarises these choices together with size and frequency.

Table 7.24 Four design decisions for a therapy group.
DecisionOption oneOption twoUsual guidance
Mix of membersHomogeneous: same problem; quick cohesion and supportHeterogeneous: varied problems and styles; more interpersonal learningMix conflict areas, match vulnerability
Membership over timeClosed: fixed members, fixed lengthOpen: members join and leaveClosed for brief structured groups; open for wards and long-term groups
SizeSmall: fewer than five, little interactionLarge: more than ten, too little time eachSeven or eight is ideal; five to ten works
Frequency and lengthOutpatient: weekly, 75 to 90 minutesInpatient: often daily, shorter sessionsBrief groups run for weeks; interactional groups for a year or more

Finally, members need pre-group preparation. Dropouts are common in the first dozen meetings, and the commonest reasons are that members did not understand what the group was for, were frightened by its early awkwardness, or felt like outsiders. In one or more individual meetings before the group begins, the leader explains how the group works and why talking about relationships in the room helps, warns that the first weeks often feel frustrating and pointless, sets out the rules on attendance, confidentiality and outside contact, agrees each member's goals, and corrects common misconceptions (that group therapy is second-rate treatment for those who cannot get individual therapy, or that members will be forced to reveal everything). Research has consistently found that such preparation reduces dropout and speeds the group's engagement.

Exam trap

A closed group has fixed membership from start to finish; an open group takes in new members as others leave. Homogeneous means the members share a problem or characteristic; heterogeneous means they differ. Do not reverse the advantages: homogeneous groups gel faster and give more support; heterogeneous groups give more interpersonal learning. The ideal size of an interactional group is about seven or eight.

Stages of group development

Groups change as they grow older, and they change in roughly predictable ways. The most widely taught description came in 1965 from the American educational psychologist Bruce Tuckman, who reviewed some fifty studies of small groups of many kinds (therapy groups, training groups, laboratory groups and natural work groups) in a paper in the Psychological Bulletin called "Developmental sequence in small groups". Tuckman looked at two strands of development at once, the relationships among members and the way they tackle the group's task, and he named four stages with a rhyme that has made them famous.

  1. Forming. Members are polite, anxious and uncertain. They test what behaviour is acceptable, look to the leader for direction and depend on the leader for structure. The unspoken questions are "What are we here for?" and "Is it safe?"
  2. Storming. Conflict breaks out: members resist the task, compete for influence, form factions and, very often, turn against the leader, who has failed to provide the answers they wanted.
  3. Norming. The group pulls together. Members accept one another, develop shared norms and roles, and begin to express personal opinions and feelings openly. A sense of cohesion appears.
  4. Performing. The relationships the group has built become a tool for its task. Roles are flexible, energy goes into the work, and in a therapy group, members do the real work of insight and change.

In 1977 Tuckman and Mary Ann Conover Jensen reviewed the research that had appeared since, in "Stages of small-group development revisited", and added a fifth stage. Adjourning is the ending of the group: completing the task, reviewing what has been achieved and dealing with the separation, with the mixture of pride, sadness and loss that endings bring. Some writers call it mourning.

Yalom, writing about therapy groups, described a similar sequence in three stages. In the initial stage of orientation, hesitant participation and search for meaning, members wonder how the group will help them, depend heavily on the leader, give one another advice and look for similarities. In the second stage of conflict, dominance and rebellion, members struggle for control and status, criticise one another and almost always attack the leader, who has not turned out to be the all-knowing figure they hoped for. In the third stage, the development of cohesiveness, members grow close, trust one another and disclose more; at first the new closeness may suppress negative feelings, and only a mature group can hold both affection and criticism. Later comes termination, which the leader treats as an important piece of work in its own right: facing the end of the group is a small rehearsal for the losses and endings of life. A third model, from the American psychologist William Schutz (1925 to 2002), derived the sequence from his FIRO theory (Fundamental Interpersonal Relations Orientation, 1958) of three interpersonal needs: groups deal first with inclusion (am I in or out?), then with control (who has power?) and then with affection (how close will we be?). Figure 7.16 lines the three models up.

Two practical lessons follow. First, conflict is a normal and necessary stage, not a sign that the group is failing; a group that never storms seldom becomes truly cohesive, and a leader who smothers every disagreement keeps the group stuck at forming. Second, the leader's job changes with the stage: providing structure, safety and hope early on, tolerating and helping the group understand its attacks on the leader in the storming phase, supporting the new norms, stepping back when the group performs, and helping it to face its ending. Bion's basic assumptions, by contrast, are not stages at all: a group can slip into dependency or fight-flight at any point in its life.

Exam trap

The order is forming, storming, norming, performing (Bruce Tuckman, 1965), with adjourning added in 1977 by Tuckman and Mary Ann Jensen. Norming comes before performing; storming is the conflict stage. The stages are Tuckman's, not Yalom's; Yalom's own three stages are orientation, conflict and cohesiveness. Inclusion, control and affection are Schutz's (FIRO).

Problem members

Every group therapist meets members whose behaviour seems to block the group's work. Yalom's advice about all of them is the same in principle: the problem member is also a group problem, the behaviour is a here-and-now display of the member's interpersonal pattern, and the aim is to understand it, not to silence or expel the member. Three types are classic.

The monopoliser (or monopolist) talks compulsively, fills every silence, interrupts, and turns every topic back to his or her own concerns. Monopolising is usually driven by anxiety: silence feels unbearable, and talking keeps the anxiety at bay. At first the other members are relieved that someone is talking and that they need not; later they grow resentful, but they say nothing and quietly collude. A leader who simply cuts the monopoliser off, or who lets the group gang up on him, creates a scapegoat (Section 7.1.4). The more useful move is to ask the group about its part: "The group seems to let Mahesh carry the conversation every week. I wonder what it gets out of that, and what it costs?" The monopoliser can then hear, with support, how the behaviour affects others.

The silent member rarely speaks. Silence may reflect shyness, fear of exposure or of losing control, contempt for the group, or simply a person who learns by watching (and members can benefit from imitative behaviour and spectator learning without saying much). But a member who stays silent for months cannot get feedback or practise new behaviour, and the group begins to wonder what he or she is thinking. The leader invites participation gently, often by noticing non-verbal reactions ("You looked moved when Asha was speaking"), never forces disclosure, and treats the silence itself as here-and-now behaviour that can be understood.

The help-rejecting complainer is a term coined in 1952 by the American psychiatrist Jerome Frank (1909 to 2005) and his colleagues for a member who constantly asks the group for help and then rejects every piece of help offered. Every suggestion is met with "Yes, but...": he has already tried it, it would never work in his case, his situation is worse than anyone's. Eric Berne later described the same pattern as the game "Why don't you, yes but" (Section 3.9.5). Beneath it lies a painful combination of dependency and mistrust, and often hostility towards anyone in authority; the complainer needs help desperately and cannot bear to be helped. The group's usual response, ever more advice followed by exasperation, only feeds the pattern. The leader stops offering solutions, acknowledges how hard the member's situation is, and helps the group talk about the feelings that the complainer stirs in them, so that the pattern itself becomes the subject of the work. Table 7.25 sets out the three.

Table 7.25 Three classic problem members.
MemberWhat it looks likeWhat often lies beneathWhat the leader does
MonopoliserTalks compulsively, interrupts, fills every silenceAnxiety relieved by talking; the group colludesAsks the group about its part; avoids scapegoating
Silent memberRarely speaksShyness, fear of exposure, or learning by watchingInvites gently, notes non-verbal reactions, never forces
Help-rejecting complainerAsks for help, rejects every suggestion ("Yes, but")Dependency mixed with mistrust and hostilityStops advising, empathises, explores the feelings evoked

Other difficult members include the boring member (who keeps the group at a safe distance by never revealing anything personal), the "co-therapist" member (who helps everyone else in order never to be helped), and members whose psychosis or severe personality disturbance makes the group's demands too intense, for whom combined individual therapy or a more structured group is often better.

Exam trap

The help-rejecting complainer (Jerome Frank and colleagues, 1952) asks for help and rejects it all; the monopoliser dominates the talking. Berne's "Why don't you, yes but" is the transactional analysis name for the same help-rejecting pattern. In each case the leader treats the problem as the group's business, not as one member's fault.

Cognitive behavioural and psychoeducational groups, and the evidence

Not every group is an interactional group in Yalom's style. The Association for Specialists in Group Work in the United States distinguishes four types: task groups (committees and work teams, aimed at getting a job done), psychoeducational groups (structured groups that teach information and skills, often to people who are well but facing a problem or risk), counselling groups (for everyday personal and interpersonal problems) and psychotherapy groups (for more serious and lasting psychological problems). Psychoeducational groups are the descendants of Pratt's classes. Examples include the relatives' groups of family psychoeducation for schizophrenia (Section 7.2.5), stress management and diabetes education groups (Chapter 9), and Peter Lewinsohn's Coping with Depression course, which taught people with depression in a classroom format, with a textbook and homework.

Cognitive behavioural groups apply the methods of Chapters 4 and 5 in a group. They are structured and time-limited, follow an agenda, teach skills, set homework, and are usually closed and homogeneous; the leader is more teacher and coach than process commentator. The group adds things individual therapy cannot: universality and hope from others with the same problem, models to imitate, partners for practice and an audience that makes exposure real. In Richard Heimberg's cognitive behavioural group therapy for social anxiety, developed in the 1980s, members enact feared situations (giving a speech, starting a conversation) with other members playing the audience, then examine the anxious predictions that the exposure tested. Dialectical behaviour therapy teaches its skills in weekly groups (Section 5.8), social skills training is usually done in groups (Sections 4.7 and 9.8), and mindfulness-based cognitive therapy is delivered to classes of a dozen or so (Section 5.9). In these groups the information, hope, universality and cohesiveness factors do most of the work; interpersonal learning in the here-and-now is less emphasised.

Is group therapy as good as individual therapy, or merely cheaper? A 1998 meta-analysis by Chris McRoberts, Gary Burlingame and Matthew Hoag found no overall difference in outcome between group and individual formats, and later reviews by Burlingame and colleagues have reached the same broad conclusion for many conditions, including depression, social anxiety, panic disorder, eating disorders and substance misuse. Groups treat several people for the time of one therapist, and some members prefer them. Cohesion and the quality of preparation predict outcome, and dropout is the format's chief weakness. Group therapy is not second-best treatment; for problems rooted in relationships it may be the treatment of choice.

Indian context

Wilfred Bion, the theorist of basic assumptions, was born in Mathura in 1897 and spent his early childhood in India before being sent to school in England. Group methods are now a routine part of Indian inpatient wards and de-addiction programmes, and they need some adaptation. Members may expect the leader to give advice and direction like a guru, which can stall the here-and-now and tempt the group into Bion's dependency assumption (the guru-chela model of the Indian therapeutic relationship is discussed in Section 8.11). Groups are often conducted in a mix of languages, relatives may expect to attend, and members may be reluctant to criticise an older member openly. A leader who explains the method carefully in the pre-group interview, and who models respectful honesty, usually finds that the therapeutic factors travel well.

7.11 Psychodrama, Encounter Groups, Self-Help and Therapeutic CommunitiesMedium yield · 5 Qs

The groups of Section 7.10 mostly sit in a circle and talk. This section is about groups that do something else. Some act out their members' lives on a stage. Some meet to learn how groups themselves work, or to grow as people rather than to recover from illness. Some have no professional in the room at all. And some are not a meeting but a whole hospital, in which every conversation in the corridor is part of the treatment. Each of these grew from a particular person and a particular moment: a Viennese doctor watching children play make-believe in a park, a German refugee social scientist running a workshop in Connecticut, two drinkers talking through a night in Akron, Ohio, and a British psychiatrist who found that his patients learned more from one another than from his lectures. Together they make up much of what clinical psychology inherited from the twentieth century's discovery of the group.

Jacob Levy Moreno and the theatre of spontaneity

Jacob Levy Moreno, whom you met in Section 7.10.1 as the man who named group psychotherapy, was born in Bucharest in 1889 and grew up in Vienna, where he qualified in medicine in 1917. Even as a student he was drawn to action rather than talk. He liked to tell how he would join the children in Vienna's public gardens and invent stories with them that they then acted out, and how he organised groups of women working as prostitutes in the Spittelberg district to meet and discuss their common problems, an early example of the self-help group. He also liked to tell of a meeting in 1912, after one of Sigmund Freud's lectures, at which he said to Freud: "I start where you leave off. You meet people in the artificial setting of your office. I meet them on the street and in their homes... You analyse their dreams. I try to give them the courage to dream again." Moreno was a showman and an inventor of his own legend, so such stories are best read as statements of his philosophy, but the philosophy is clear: people are healed by acting spontaneously in the presence of others, not by lying on a couch.

In 1921 Moreno opened the Stegreiftheater, the Theatre of Spontaneity, in Vienna (its first public performance was in April 1921; from 1922 the company played in a rented room of its own, so some books give 1922). Its actors had no scripts. They improvised plays from suggestions shouted by the audience or from the day's newspaper headlines. The episode that Moreno later told as the birth of psychodrama concerned one of his actresses, Barbara, who was admired for playing sweet, gentle heroines. Her husband George came to Moreno in distress: at home, he said, the gentle Barbara was a shrew who flew into rages and abused him. Moreno began casting her in coarse, violent roles: a streetwalker, a woman who fights in the gutter. She played them with gusto, and George reported that her outbursts at home grew fewer. Before long Moreno had the couple act scenes from their own marriage on stage, with the audience watching, and the marriage improved further. Whether or not it happened exactly so, the story contains every ingredient of psychodrama: real life brought onto a stage, enacted rather than described, before a group that is moved by it.

Moreno emigrated to the United States in 1925. In New York he developed both psychodrama and sociometry (Section 7.11.4), and in 1936 he opened a sanatorium at Beacon, a town on the Hudson River north of New York City, with a purpose-built therapeutic theatre: a round stage on three levels, with a balcony above it. With his wife, Zerka Toeman Moreno (1917 to 2016), who refined and taught the method for decades after his death, he trained psychodramatists from all over the world. Moreno died at Beacon in 1974.

The five elements and three phases of psychodrama

Psychodrama is a group method in which one member enacts scenes from his or her life, past, present, feared or wished-for, on a stage, with other members playing the other people in those scenes, under the guidance of a trained director, in order to explore and change the way he or she feels and acts. Moreno described five instruments that every psychodrama needs.

  • The protagonist. The protagonist is the member whose life is being enacted in this session. Moreno chose the word from Greek drama: the first actor, around whom the play turns.
  • The director. The director is the trained therapist who guides the enactment. Moreno described the director as at once a producer (who stages the scenes), a therapist (who keeps the protagonist safe and moving towards change) and an analyst (who makes sense of what is revealed).
  • The auxiliary egos. Auxiliary egos, often simply called auxiliaries, are the group members (or trained staff) who play the other people in the protagonist's world: a father, a boss, a dead child, even a part of the protagonist's own self. They take their cues from the protagonist and the director.
  • The audience. The other members watch, and they are not passive. They support the protagonist, are moved by recognising their own lives in the drama, and may be drawn in as auxiliaries.
  • The stage. The stage is the space in which the action happens, a raised platform at Beacon but often just a cleared area of a room. It marks the boundary between talking about life and living it, and it allows what Moreno called surplus reality: scenes that never happened and never could, such as a conversation with someone who has died.

A session moves through three phases. In the warm-up, the director uses exercises, movement and sharing to build trust and spontaneity in the group, and a protagonist emerges, either by volunteering or by the group's choice. In the action phase, the protagonist, guided by the director, sets the scene (the room, the furniture, the time of day), chooses auxiliaries and enacts the story, typically moving from a scene in the present towards an earlier, more central one, reaching an emotional peak, and then trying out a new way of responding. In the sharing phase, the protagonist rejoins the group, and members tell what the drama touched in their own lives. Sharing is personal: members do not analyse, interpret or advise, because a protagonist who has just exposed so much needs to hear "this is my story too", not a verdict. Training groups sometimes add a fourth phase, processing, in which the director's technique is discussed.

Follow a session to see how the parts fit. Divya, a twenty-six-year-old engineer in Pune, volunteers in the warm-up. Her father died suddenly a year ago, two weeks after an angry phone call in which she told him she was leaving her government job to join a start-up. In the action phase the director asks her to set the scene of that call: her hostel room, the phone, the evening. A member she chooses plays her father. She speaks to him, stiffly at first. The director then asks her to set up a scene that never happened: her father, sitting in a chair, one last time. Now the director brings in the techniques of the next subsection, and the scene ends with Divya, in tears, saying what she never said. In the sharing, a man in the group speaks of the brother he never forgave, and a woman of her own father's silence. Divya leaves feeling not exposed but accompanied.

Techniques, spontaneity and catharsis

Psychodramatists use a large repertoire of techniques, but six are examined again and again (Table 7.26). In role reversal, the protagonist and an auxiliary swap places: Divya sits in her father's chair and speaks as him, while the auxiliary plays Divya. Role reversal lets the protagonist see the situation from the other person's side, often producing sudden empathy ("As him, I can feel how frightened he was for me"); it lets the protagonist see herself as others see her; and it shows the auxiliary how the absent person really speaks, so that the auxiliary can play the part more truly. Moreno regarded it as the heart of the method. In doubling, an auxiliary stands beside or slightly behind the protagonist, adopts her posture and voices the thoughts and feelings she is not expressing ("I'm so afraid you died ashamed of me"); the protagonist accepts, corrects or rejects what the double says, and the double helps her reach and own what lies beneath her words. In mirroring, the protagonist steps out of the scene and watches while an auxiliary replays her behaviour, her slumped shoulders and flat voice, as if in a mirror; it is used when a protagonist cannot see what she is doing or is too overwhelmed to go on, and it must be done with care so that it does not become mockery.

In the soliloquy, the protagonist speaks her private thoughts aloud, alone, often while walking slowly around the stage before entering a scene or after leaving one. The empty chair places an absent person (or a part of the self) in a chair to be addressed; psychodrama shares this device with Gestalt therapy, which made it famous (Section 6.3). In future projection, the protagonist enacts a scene from the future, a feared confrontation, a job interview, the conversation in which she tells her mother she is marrying outside the community, both to rehearse it and to discover what she hopes and fears. Behaviour therapists later developed much the same idea as behaviour rehearsal (Section 4.7.3).

Table 7.26 Six core techniques of psychodrama.
TechniqueWhat happensMain purpose
Role reversalProtagonist plays the other person; an auxiliary plays the protagonistEmpathy for the other; seeing oneself from outside
DoublingAn auxiliary beside the protagonist voices unspoken thoughts and feelingsReaching and owning what lies beneath the words
MirroringProtagonist watches an auxiliary replay her behaviourSelf-observation when the protagonist cannot see herself
SoliloquyProtagonist speaks private thoughts aloud, aloneBringing hidden thoughts into the open
Empty chairProtagonist addresses an absent person placed in a chairUnfinished business with someone absent or dead
Future projectionProtagonist enacts a scene that has not yet happenedRehearsal; clarifying hopes and fears

Behind the techniques lie three of Moreno's ideas. The first is spontaneity, which he defined as an adequate response to a new situation or a new response to an old one. Psychological problems, in his view, are failures of spontaneity: we respond to today's situations with yesterday's rigid scripts, which he called cultural conserves. The warm-up exists to raise spontaneity, and the action phase gives it room. The second is catharsis. Aristotle had used the word for the purging of pity and fear in the audience of a tragedy, and Breuer and Freud for the release of strangulated emotion by remembering (Section 3.1.2). Moreno argued that the deepest catharsis comes to the actor, through action, and he spoke of a catharsis of integration: not merely an emotional discharge but a new pulling together of the person, which is why every enactment ends with new behaviour and with sharing. The third is tele, Moreno's word for the two-way flow of feeling between people, the immediate sense of attraction, repulsion or indifference that each person has towards another. Unlike transference, which is one-way and distorted, tele is mutual and accurate, and Moreno thought it was the glue of every group. Tele is what sociometry measures.

Does psychodrama work? The research is thinner than for most methods in this book. A 2003 meta-analysis by David Kipper and Timothy Ritchie of 25 experimental studies found large average effects, with role reversal and doubling the most effective techniques, but most of the studies were small and poorly controlled. Psychodrama is better regarded as a powerful method with a modest evidence base, and its greatest influence has been indirect: role play, behaviour rehearsal, the empty chair, the here-and-now and the idea of the group as audience have passed into almost every other therapy.

Exam trap

The five elements of psychodrama are the protagonist (whose life is enacted), the director (the therapist), the auxiliary egos (members who play roles), the audience and the stage; its three phases are warm-up, action and sharing. Role reversal (swapping parts), doubling (voicing the inner self beside the protagonist) and mirroring (watching oneself replayed) are Moreno's. Do not give role reversal to interpersonal psychotherapy (Section 3.7), Kelly's fixed-role therapy (Section 5.5) or Adler's acting "as if" (Section 3.8). The empty chair is best known as Fritz Perls's Gestalt technique (Section 6.3), and behaviour rehearsal belongs to the behaviour therapists (Section 4.7). In the sharing phase members share their own experience; they do not interpret.

Sociometry and the sociogram

Moreno's second great invention was a way of measuring the invisible structure of a group. Sociometry (from the Latin socius, companion, and metrum, measure) is the study and measurement of the patterns of choice, attraction and rejection among the members of a group. Its basic tool is the sociometric test: each member is asked, on a stated criterion that matters to the group (whom would you like to share a room with, work on a project with, sit next to in class?), to name a first, second and third choice, and sometimes the people he or she would not choose. The answers can be set out in a table, the sociomatrix, or drawn as a diagram, the sociogram.

Key study: the runaways of Hudson (Moreno, 1934)

Question. In the early 1930s the New York State Training School for Girls at Hudson, an institution for delinquent adolescent girls who lived in separate cottages, suffered a sudden epidemic of runaways. Why were girls running away, and why in clusters? Method. Moreno and his colleague Helen Hall Jennings asked every girl whom she would like to live with and work with, and mapped the choices and rejections across the whole school. Results. The runaways were not random. They followed the channels of attraction in the school's network of choices, spreading along chains of friendship much as an infection spreads. When girls were reassigned to cottages on the basis of mutual choices, the runaways fell sharply. Conclusion. A group's hidden network of attraction and rejection shapes its members' behaviour, and it can be measured and deliberately rearranged. Moreno reported the work in Who Shall Survive? (1934). Criticism. Choices change over time and depend on the criterion asked, the work was not a controlled experiment, and rearranging people's lives by their choices raises its own questions. But Moreno's diagrams are now recognised as among the first drawings of social networks, the ancestors of modern network science.

A sociogram draws each member as a circle (often a circle for girls and a triangle for boys in school studies) and each choice as an arrow from the chooser to the chosen, with different lines for rejection. Several patterns can be read straight off it. A star receives many choices. An isolate neither chooses nor is chosen. A neglectee chooses others but receives few choices, and a rejectee receives many rejections. A mutual choice (a pair) links two people who chose each other, and pairs can join into chains, triangles and closed cliques; a cleavage is a gap between subgroups that choose only within themselves. Figure 7.17 shows a small example from an eight-member group.

Sociograms are used by teachers to find isolated or rejected children, by group therapists to understand the subgroups forming in their groups, and by organisations to understand who really talks to whom. In psychodrama groups the same idea is used in action: members may be asked to stand near the person they would choose for a scene, so that the group's choices become visible in the room.

Three other diagrams are easily confused with the sociogram, and Section 7.3 teaches two of them. The genogram is a family tree of at least three generations, with standard symbols for people, relationships and events, popularised by Murray Bowen; the ecomap, devised by Ann Hartman, shows a family's links with the outside systems around it (school, work, relatives, health services). The sociogram, by contrast, maps the choices and communication within a group. A cognitive map is something else again: Edward Tolman's term for a rat's mental representation of a maze (General Psychology book).

Moreno also distinguished psychodrama from sociodrama, a group method in which the group as a whole is the protagonist and the roles enacted are collective rather than personal: not Divya and her father, but "a father" and "a daughter" in a changing society. Sociodrama explores the shared social roles, conflicts and prejudices of a group: a new daughter-in-law and her mother-in-law, a police officer and a protester, a landlord and a tenant from a community that has faced discrimination. It is used in education, community work, the training of teachers and police, and the reduction of conflict between groups.

Exam trap

The tool that "captures implicit and explicit group communication and interaction patterns" is the sociogram (Jacob Moreno, sociometry, Who Shall Survive?, 1934). The genogram is a multigenerational family tree (Bowen), the ecomap shows a family's ties to outside systems (Hartman), and a cognitive map is Tolman's mental map of a maze. In a sociogram the star receives many choices and the isolate gives and receives none. Psychodrama enacts one person's life; sociodrama enacts collective social roles, with the group as protagonist.

Kurt Lewin and the T-group

The second stream of the group movement came not from a clinic but from social science. Kurt Lewin (1890 to 1947) was a German psychologist who emigrated to the United States in 1933, developed field theory (behaviour as a function of the person and the environment, taught in the General and Social Psychology books) and, more than anyone, founded the study of group dynamics, the forces that shape how groups form, decide, lead and change. In 1945 he set up the Research Center for Group Dynamics at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology. His earlier experiments with Ronald Lippitt and Ralph White on autocratic, democratic and laissez-faire leadership in boys' clubs belong to social psychology; here what matters is an accident in the summer of 1946.

That summer the Connecticut State Interracial Commission asked Lewin to run a two-week workshop in New Britain, Connecticut, to train community leaders, teachers and social workers to deal with racial and religious tensions. Lewin and his colleagues, among them Leland Bradford, Kenneth Benne and Ronald Lippitt, divided the participants into small discussion groups and placed research observers in each group to record what happened. Every evening the staff met to discuss the observers' notes. A few participants asked whether they could sit in on these staff meetings, and Lewin agreed. The result astonished everyone. When an observer described a participant's behaviour that day, the participant would often object ("That isn't what I meant at all"), others would join in, and the evening sessions became the most intense and valuable learning of the whole workshop. Within days, nearly all the participants were attending. Lewin and his colleagues realised that people learn powerfully about themselves and about groups when they receive direct feedback on their behaviour in a group, here and now, and examine it together.

Lewin died in February 1947, but that summer Bradford, Benne and Lippitt ran the first session of what became the National Training Laboratories (NTL) at Bethel, Maine. Its central method was the T-group (training group): a small, unstructured group of about a dozen people with a trainer, whose task is to study its own behaviour as it happens. With no agenda, the group must create its own structure, and in doing so its members learn how groups work, how leadership and decisions emerge, and how their own behaviour affects others. The method was called the laboratory method because the group is its own laboratory; its core tools are the here-and-now, feedback and what Lewin called unfreezing, loosening people's habitual ways of seeing and acting so that change becomes possible. Through the 1950s and 1960s T-groups spread into business, government, the churches and education, and the approach came to be known as sensitivity training: training in awareness of one's own feelings, of others' reactions and of group processes. Critics pointed out that its effects on behaviour back at work were often small, and that employees sent compulsorily by their companies could be harmed by intense confrontation. It is worth being clear about what a T-group is: an educational group for people who are not patients, aimed at learning about groups and interpersonal skills, not a treatment for disorder.

Encounter groups and the human potential movement

By the 1960s the T-group had a more emotional descendant. Section 6.2.7 described the encounter group: a small, relatively unstructured group, often meeting intensively over a weekend, whose purpose is personal growth, self-awareness and more honest relationships through open self-disclosure, the here-and-now expression of feelings and frank feedback. It also described Carl Ransom Rogers's version, the basic encounter group, which he wrote about in Carl Rogers on Encounter Groups (1970). Encounter groups were the favourite instrument of the human potential movement, a loose movement of the 1960s and 1970s built on humanistic psychology's belief that ordinary, healthy people have great untapped capacities for feeling, creativity, intimacy and growth. Its centre was the Esalen Institute at Big Sur in California, founded in 1962, where Fritz Perls ran Gestalt workshops (Section 6.3) and William Schutz, whom you met in Section 7.10.7, led "open encounter" groups that added physical exercises, touch and non-verbal games, described in his book Joy (1967). Some groups ran as marathons lasting a day or more without a break, on the theory that fatigue would wear down defences. Moreno, characteristically, claimed priority: he had used the word encounter (Begegnung) in his Viennese writings long before.

The difference between a T-group and an encounter group is mainly one of aim. The T-group studies the group in order to learn about groups, leadership and organisations; the encounter group uses the group in order to change the individual, with much more emphasis on emotional expression and intimacy. Both differ from a therapy group in that their members are not patients and are not selected or prepared for treatment. That difference proved important. Section 6.2.7 described how Morton Lieberman, Irvin Yalom and Matthew Miles (1973) found that about a third of the students in their encounter groups benefited, a similar proportion were unchanged, and about 8 percent became casualties who suffered lasting psychological harm, mostly in groups run by aggressive, charismatic leaders who pushed for emotional intensity and attacked members' defences. Section 7.10.5 drew the lesson for leaders: high caring and meaning attribution help, unrestrained emotional stimulation harms. Encounter groups faded in the late 1970s, but their legacy survives in the here-and-now focus of interpersonal group therapy, in team-building and leadership training, and in the many personal growth workshops still offered today. Table 7.27 sets the main group formats of this section and the last side by side.

Table 7.27 Group formats compared.
FormatKey names and datesMain aimWho leadsMembers
Interpersonal group therapyPratt 1905; Yalom 1970Relieve symptoms; change interpersonal patternsTrained therapist or co-therapistsSelected, prepared patients
Group analysisFoulkes, 1940s (Bion's Tavistock groups are a related tradition)Psychoanalytic change through the groupConductorPatients in long-term groups
PsychodramaMoreno, Vienna 1921; Beacon 1936Enact and change one's life storyDirectorProtagonist, auxiliaries, audience
T-groupLewin 1946; NTL Bethel 1947Learn group dynamics and interpersonal skillsTrainerManagers, teachers, professionals
Encounter groupRogers, Schutz, Esalen, 1960sPersonal growth through disclosure and feedbackFacilitatorOrdinary people seeking growth
Self-help groupAlcoholics Anonymous 1935Mutual support; abstinence or copingMembers themselvesAnyone sharing the problem
Therapeutic communityMain 1946; Maxwell Jones 1947The whole community as treatmentStaff and residents togetherInpatients or residents
Exam trap

The principal role of an encounter group is to encourage personal growth and self-change through self-disclosure and interaction. "Enabling people to interact socially" is too weak, "team building" describes organisational training, and "aiding the therapist in diagnosis" is no part of it. Keep the founders straight: T-groups come from Kurt Lewin (1946 workshop, NTL at Bethel, Maine, 1947) and teach group dynamics; encounter groups belong to Carl Rogers and the human potential movement and aim at personal growth; the casualty study is Lieberman, Yalom and Miles (1973).

Self-help and mutual-aid groups

In May 1935 a New York stockbroker named William Griffith Wilson (1895 to 1971), known to millions since as Bill W., was alone in a hotel in Akron, Ohio. A business deal he had come to close had collapsed, and he was desperate for a drink. Wilson's drinking had already wrecked his career, and he had been sober only since the previous December, after a spiritual experience during his last stay in a New York hospital for alcoholics, inspired by an old drinking friend who had stopped drinking through the Oxford Group, an evangelical Christian movement. Wilson had discovered that his own sobriety seemed to depend on trying to help other drinkers. So instead of going to the hotel bar, he telephoned around until he was put in touch with an alcoholic surgeon, Robert Holbrook Smith (1879 to 1950), Dr Bob. The two men talked for hours, one drunk to another. Smith took his last drink on 10 June 1935, the date that Alcoholics Anonymous (AA) celebrates as its founding. Within four years there were about a hundred sober members in three groups, and in 1939 they published their basic text, Alcoholics Anonymous, known as the Big Book, from which the fellowship took its name. AA now meets in about 180 countries.

The Big Book set out the twelve steps, the programme of personal recovery that each member works through. In paraphrase, the member admits being powerless over alcohol and that life has become unmanageable (the first step reads "We admitted we were powerless over alcohol, that our lives had become unmanageable"); comes to believe that a Power greater than oneself can restore sanity, and decides to turn one's will and life over to God "as we understood Him"; makes a searching moral inventory of oneself, admits one's wrongs to God, to oneself and to another person, and asks for one's defects to be removed; lists the people one has harmed and makes amends to them wherever possible; continues the self-inventory, prays and meditates; and, having had a spiritual awakening, carries the message to other alcoholics. The last step is Wilson's discovery in Akron made into a rule: helping others keeps one sober. By 1946 Wilson had also summarised the twelve traditions, which were adopted at AA's first international convention in Cleveland in 1950. Where the steps guide the individual, the traditions guide the groups: the only requirement for membership is a desire to stop drinking; each group is self-supporting through members' own contributions and refuses outside money; AA stays forever non-professional, has no opinion on outside issues, does not promote itself, and holds anonymity to be its spiritual foundation. A newcomer is encouraged to find a sponsor, an experienced member with stable sobriety who guides him through the steps and can be telephoned at any hour when the urge to drink strikes. Meetings are open (anyone may attend) or closed (only for those who want to stop drinking), and members are encouraged to take recovery "one day at a time".

AA became the model for a whole family of self-help groups, also called mutual-aid or mutual-help groups: voluntary groups of people who share a problem or situation, run by the members themselves rather than by professionals, usually free of charge, in which members help one another to cope or recover. Al-Anon was founded in 1951 by Lois Wilson, Bill's wife, and her friend Anne B., for the families and friends of alcoholics, and Alateen followed for teenagers. Narcotics Anonymous (NA), adapting the twelve steps to drug addiction, began in California in 1953. Gamblers Anonymous and Overeaters Anonymous applied them to other problems. Not all mutual-aid groups follow the twelve steps: Recovery, Inc., founded in Chicago in 1937 by the psychiatrist Abraham Low (1891 to 1954), taught former psychiatric patients a self-help method of controlling their reactions; SMART Recovery (1994) offers a secular, cognitive behavioural alternative for addiction; and there are now groups for carers of people with dementia, for parents of children with autism, for people who hear voices and for almost every illness and loss.

How does a mutual-aid group differ from a therapy group? Table 7.28 compares them. Two ideas explain why mutual aid can help. The sociologist Thomasina Borkman (1976) pointed out that self-help groups run on experiential knowledge, the truth learned by living through a problem, which members trust in a way they may not trust professional knowledge; the old-timer who says "I sat where you are sitting" carries an authority no doctor can. And the sociologist Frank Riessman (1965) described the helper therapy principle: those who help are helped, because helping raises self-esteem, makes the helper rehearse the lessons he is teaching and gives him a valued role. This is Yalom's altruism in another vocabulary. Of Yalom's factors, AA mobilises especially instillation of hope (the speaker with twenty years' sobriety), universality, altruism, imparting information and imitative behaviour; interpersonal learning in the here-and-now plays little part, because meetings discourage members from commenting on one another's contributions ("cross-talk").

Table 7.28 Therapy groups and mutual-aid groups compared.
FeatureTherapy groupMutual-aid group
LeadershipTrained professionalMembers themselves; no professional
MembershipSelected and prepared; usually closed or slow-openOpen to anyone with the problem; drop in at will
Knowledge baseProfessional theory and researchExperiential knowledge of members
Cost and durationFee; fixed course or one to two yearsFree; open-ended, often lifelong
Main mechanismsInterpersonal learning, cohesion, here-and-nowHope, universality, altruism, identification with peers
Typical focusInterpersonal patterns and symptomsAbstinence or coping with a shared problem

Does AA work? For decades the honest answer was "probably, for those who stay", because people choose whether to attend, and those who choose AA may differ from those who do not. Two kinds of evidence changed the picture. In Project MATCH (Chapter 9), a manualised twelve-step facilitation therapy, which encourages active involvement in AA, did as well as cognitive behavioural and motivational treatments. And in 2020 a Cochrane review settled much of the debate.

Key study: the Cochrane review of AA (Kelly, Humphreys and Ferri, 2020)

Question. Do Alcoholics Anonymous and clinical interventions that promote AA (twelve-step facilitation) help people with alcohol use disorder? Method. John Kelly, Keith Humphreys and Marica Ferri pooled 27 studies with more than 10,000 participants, including randomised trials, comparing AA and twelve-step facilitation with other established treatments such as cognitive behaviour therapy. Results. Manualised AA and twelve-step facilitation interventions were more effective than the other treatments in producing continuous abstinence, and about as effective on other drinking outcomes such as the intensity of drinking; they also tended to reduce health-care costs. Conclusion. Encouraging patients to engage with AA is an effective, low-cost part of treatment for alcohol use disorder. Criticism. Many studies came from the United States, the evidence for drinking outcomes other than abstinence was less certain, and the review tested professionally delivered facilitation as much as AA itself. Research on mechanisms suggests that AA works largely by changing members' social networks towards sober friends and by building their confidence and coping, not only through its spiritual content.

AA has its critics. Its spiritual language deters some people, its emphasis on powerlessness sits awkwardly with the self-efficacy that cognitive behavioural therapists try to build, it insists on total abstinence, and court-mandated attendance strains its voluntary spirit. Clinicians therefore offer it as one option among several and help each person find the group that suits them.

Indian context

AA reached India in 1957, when its message was carried to a schoolteacher in Bombay, and the city had the country's first and, for some years, largest group; meetings are now held in many Indian cities and languages. Some Indian programmes have blended the twelve steps with local traditions, as the Kripa Foundation in Mumbai did with yoga and meditation (Section 9.9.7). Because most Indian patients with alcohol dependence live with their families, Al-Anon and family groups, and family involvement in treatment generally, are particularly useful here.

Clinical link

Simply telling a patient "go to AA" achieves little. Twelve-step facilitation works because the clinician explains what meetings are like, suggests trying several different groups before deciding, links the patient with a member who can take him to his first meeting, encourages him to find a sponsor, and asks about his experience at the next session. Offer Al-Anon to the spouse, whatever the drinker decides. The same principles apply to referral to any mutual-aid group.

Exam trap

Alcoholics Anonymous was founded in 1935 in Akron, Ohio, by Bill Wilson (a stockbroker) and Dr Bob Smith (a surgeon); the Big Book appeared in 1939. The twelve steps guide the individual member; the twelve traditions guide the groups. Al-Anon (1951) is for families and friends of alcoholics, Alateen for their teenage children, Narcotics Anonymous (1953) for drug addiction. A self-help group is led by its members, not by professionals. The helper therapy principle (Riessman) is the mutual-aid version of Yalom's altruism.

Milieu therapy and the therapeutic community

The last idea of this chapter enlarges the group to the size of a whole institution. Its ancestor is the moral treatment of the late eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries (Chapter 1), when reformers such as Philippe Pinel in Paris and William Tuke at the York Retreat (1796) argued that a humane, ordered, respectful environment could itself restore the insane. By the twentieth century, though, the great mental hospitals had become custodial warehouses, and the hospital environment was more likely to cause disability than cure it (Chapter 9 describes what Erving Goffman called the total institution).

The Second World War brought the idea back. You have already met Northfield (Section 7.10.1), where the second experiment turned a whole military hospital into a community in which soldiers took responsibility for its life and work. In 1946 one of the Northfield psychiatrists, Tom Main (1911 to 1990), described the hospital as a therapeutic institution in a paper of that title, and coined the term therapeutic community for an institution organised so that the whole community, staff and patients together, and the relationships and daily life within it, become the means of treatment. Main later directed the Cassel Hospital near London for thirty years.

The name most closely linked with the therapeutic community, however, is that of Maxwell Jones (1907 to 1990), a psychiatrist born in South Africa and trained in Edinburgh. During the war Jones ran a unit at Mill Hill in London for soldiers with "effort syndrome", palpitations, breathlessness and exhaustion with no heart disease to explain them. He began by giving groups of patients lectures on the physiology of their symptoms, and noticed that the discussions that followed, and the patients' explanations to one another, did more good than his lectures. After the war he treated returned prisoners of war, and in 1947 he took charge of the Industrial Neurosis Unit at Belmont Hospital in Surrey, later renamed the Henderson Hospital. Its patients were chronically unemployed men and women with what would now be called personality disorders, people whom ordinary psychiatric wards could not help. Jones described the work in Social Psychiatry (1952), published in the United States as The Therapeutic Community.

Life at the Henderson was organised around a daily community meeting of all patients and all staff, in which anything that had happened in the community could be raised and examined. Staff did not wear uniforms, and the usual hierarchy of doctor, nurse and patient was deliberately flattened; patients sat on committees, shared the running of the unit and, at the Henderson, had a large say in decisions about admissions and discharges. Every incident, an argument in the kitchen, a broken rule, a resident who did not get up, became what Jones called a living-learning situation: something to be talked about openly by the community, so that the person involved could see how his behaviour affected others and try something different. Treatment relied on the community, not on medication or individual therapy.

In the 1950s the anthropologist Robert Rapoport and his team spent several years studying the Henderson, and in Community as Doctor (1960) they distilled its ideology into four principles that are still quoted as the definition of a democratic therapeutic community.

  • Democratisation: every member of the community, patient or staff, has an equal right to share in decisions about the community's life.
  • Permissiveness: members tolerate a wide range of behaviour, including disturbing behaviour, so that it can be expressed and examined rather than simply suppressed or punished.
  • Communalism: life is shared, with open communication, shared tasks and facilities, close relationships and a free flow of information; everything is everybody's business.
  • Reality confrontation: members are continually shown, by the reactions of the others, how their behaviour is actually seen and what effect it has.

Rapoport also noticed tensions between these ideals and the practical need to rehabilitate people for life outside, a reminder that a therapeutic community is always a balance. Jones went on in the 1960s to turn a whole mental hospital, Dingleton Hospital at Melrose in Scotland, into an open-door therapeutic community, and later took his ideas to North America. Democratic therapeutic communities spread to prisons (Grendon prison in England has run as one since 1962) and to services for people with personality disorders; the Henderson itself closed in 2008. Their research base is encouraging but methodologically weak, because residents choose to come and randomised trials are hard to run.

Milieu therapy is the broader idea of which the therapeutic community is one form: the deliberate use of the whole environment of a ward or residential unit (its routines, activities, staff attitudes, relationships and rules) as a treatment. The term is usually traced to Bruno Bettelheim and Emmy Sylvester, who wrote in 1948 about the therapeutic milieu of the Orthogenic School for disturbed children in Chicago, and its forerunners include Harry Stack Sullivan's specially staffed ward at Sheppard Pratt (Section 3.8.7). In 1978 the American psychiatrist John Gunderson described five therapeutic functions that a milieu can serve, and argued that different patients need different mixtures of them: containment (physical safety and limits, vital in acute crisis), support (comfort and reassurance), structure (a predictable timetable and clear expectations), involvement (active participation in the community, central for people with personality disorders) and validation (respect for each person's individuality). Milieu therapy did not always win when tested: in Gordon Paul and Robert Lentz's long comparison for severely disabled long-stay patients (1977), a social learning programme built on a token economy did better than milieu therapy (Sections 4.5 and 9.8.7).

One more distinction completes the picture. The concept-based or hierarchical therapeutic communities for drug addiction, which began with Synanon in California in 1958 and continued in Daytop Village in New York and many others, borrowed the name but not the democracy. They are run on a strict ladder of jobs and privileges, with senior residents in recovery as role models and confrontational encounter groups as a central ritual; Section 9.9.7 teaches them. Maxwell Jones's therapeutic community is democratic and flattened; the addiction therapeutic community is hierarchical and structured. Both use the community as the agent of change, in opposite ways.

Exam trap

The modality "pioneered by Maxwell Jones" and marked by democratic involvement of staff and patients in decisions and daily life is the therapeutic community. The distractors: encounter groups are Rogers's personal growth groups; group analysis is Foulkes's psychoanalytic group therapy; milieu therapy is the broader idea of a therapeutic ward environment (Bettelheim; Gunderson's five functions), of which the therapeutic community is one specific, democratic form, so when Maxwell Jones is named, choose therapeutic community. The token economy is Ayllon and Azrin's (Chapter 4), aversion therapy is a behaviour therapy and EMDR is Francine Shapiro's trauma treatment (both in Section 4.4), psychodrama to Moreno, logotherapy to Viktor Frankl (Section 6.5) and solution-focused brief therapy to de Shazer and Berg (Section 6.8). Tom Main coined the term therapeutic community (1946); Rapoport's four principles are democratisation, permissiveness, communalism and reality confrontation.
