The case of the young man and his mother, revisited

Go back to the hospital visit that opened this chapter. As an explanation of schizophrenia, the double bind failed: research teams could not reliably identify double binds, could not show that they were more common in the families of people with schizophrenia, and could not show that they came before the illness rather than after it (Section 7.2). Today the young man's illness would be understood through the interaction of genetic vulnerability and environment, the diathesis-stress model of Chapter 1, and nobody would tell his mother that she had caused it. Yet the scene still teaches. Bateson's group used it to illustrate the symptomatic double bind: her stiffening body said "do not come close", her words said "you should come close, or you do not love me", and her last remark forbade him to notice the contradiction, while he could neither leave nor comment. Virginia Satir would call such a message incongruent, a double-level message in which words and body disagree (Section 7.6). And the members of the Palo Alto group who recorded the scene went on to shape the Mental Research Institute that Don Jackson founded in 1959, Jay Haley's strategic therapy and the MRI's brief therapy (Section 7.5), all of which listen for the relationship message underneath the content.

What would a clinician do for this family now? The question that survived is not "what caused his illness?" but "what will make him relapse?". A researcher interviewing his mother with the Camberwell Family Interview would listen for critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement, because Vaughn and Leff (1976) showed that high expressed emotion predicts relapse, and that regular medication and less than 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact protect against it. The treatment would be family psychoeducation: telling the mother plainly that the family did not cause the illness, teaching both of them about schizophrenia and its early warning signs, practising how to express feelings and requests without criticism, and solving the practical problems of daily life together. In hospital he might join a ward group, where he could discover that he is not the only one (Yalom's universality), or live on a ward run as a therapeutic community (Sections 7.10 and 7.11). The move Bateson made with this scene, from the inside of one person's head to the space between people, is the foundation on which every therapy in this chapter, from Bowen's coaching to a couples session to a room of eight strangers, still builds.

Chapter summary

7.1 The Family as a System. Family therapy grew from child guidance clinics, marriage counselling, pioneers who saw whole families (John Bowlby in 1949, John Elderkin Bell in 1951 and Nathan Ackerman, the "grandfather of family therapy") and, above all, 1950s research on the families of patients with schizophrenia. Its ideas came from Ludwig von Bertalanffy's general systems theory (wholeness, interdependence, open systems, equifinality) and Norbert Wiener's cybernetics (1948). Don Jackson's family homeostasis (1957) is kept by family rules; negative feedback restores the old balance and positive feedback amplifies change, and neither word means good or bad. In circular causality each person's behaviour is both cause and effect, and each partner punctuates the sequence differently. The identified patient is the symptom-bearer, sometimes a scapegoat (Vogel and Bell, 1960). Subsystems, boundaries and triangulation recur in every school, and in Carter and McGoldrick's family life cycle (1980) symptoms cluster at transitions.

7.2 Families and Schizophrenia: From Blame to Expressed Emotion. Early theories blamed families: Frieda Fromm-Reichmann's schizophrenogenic mother (1948), the double bind of Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland (1956), Theodore Lidz's marital schism and marital skew (1957) and Lyman Wynne's pseudomutuality and rubber fence (1958). None survived as a cause of schizophrenia, although the double bind was seminal for the communication and strategic therapies. What survived was expressed emotion: George Brown's London research, the Camberwell Family Interview and the index of Brown, Birley and Wing (1972), whose three components are critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement (warmth is not one). Vaughn and Leff (1976) showed that high EE predicts relapse, not onset, and that medication and under 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact protect. Family psychoeducation (Leff, Falloon's behavioural family management, Anderson and Hogarty) educates without blame, trains communication and problem solving, and reduces relapse.

7.3 Bowen's Family Systems Theory. Murray Bowen (1913 to 1990) watched whole families living on an NIMH research ward and saw the family as an emotional unit driven by chronic anxiety. His eight concepts are differentiation of self (separating thinking from feeling while keeping a self in close relationships; fusion is its opposite), triangles, the nuclear family emotional process, the family projection process, the multigenerational transmission process, emotional cutoff, sibling position (from Walter Toman) and societal regression. Differentiation is not distance: the cut-off person is poorly differentiated. The Bowenian therapist is a calm, detriangled coach who asks process questions, often sees one motivated member and coaches clients to go home to their families of origin. The genogram, with symbols standardised by McGoldrick and Gerson (1985), maps three generations; Ann Hartman's ecomap maps a family's outside links, and Moreno's sociogram maps choices within a group.

7.4 Minuchin's Structural Family Therapy. Salvador Minuchin (1921 to 2017) developed structural family therapy at the Wiltwyck School (Families of the Slums, 1967) and the Philadelphia Child Guidance Clinic (Families and Family Therapy, 1974). Symptoms are maintained by the family's present structure: its subsystems, its hierarchy and its boundaries. Diffuse boundaries produce enmeshment, over-involvement with loss of autonomy; rigid boundaries produce disengagement; clear boundaries are the healthy middle. Cross-generational coalitions, triangulation and detouring use a child to manage the parents' conflict, and the structural map shows the present organisation. The psychosomatic family (enmeshment, overprotectiveness, rigidity and lack of conflict resolution) and the family lunch session came from work with diabetic and anorexic children. Therapy first joins (maintenance, tracking, mimesis) and then restructures through enactment, intensity, boundary making, unbalancing and reframing. Bowen looks across generations as a calm coach; Minuchin looks at the present household as an active director.

7.5 Strategic, Brief and Systemic Therapies: Haley, the MRI and Milan. Three branches grew from the Bateson project. Jay Haley named strategic therapy in Uncommon Therapy (1973), drawn from Milton Erickson's cases: the therapist takes charge, sets concrete goals and gives directives, straightforward, paradoxical (Peggy Papp's redefine, prescribe, restrain) or ordeals. For Haley a symptom controls a relationship while denying it, and the perverse triangle is a denied cross-generational coalition; Cloe Madanes added incongruous hierarchies and the pretend technique. The MRI Brief Therapy Center limited treatment to ten sessions and held that the attempted solution becomes the problem; Change (1974) distinguished first-order from second-order change and brought reframing into the field. The Milan team (Selvini Palazzoli, Boscolo, Cecchin and Prata) used positive connotation, rituals and counterparadox, then the guidelines of hypothesising, circularity and neutrality (1980), with circular questioning; Selvini Palazzoli and Prata later developed the invariant prescription.

7.6 Experiential and Contextual Family Therapies. Experiential family therapists put feeling and the therapist's own presence at the centre. Virginia Satir (1916 to 1988), a co-founder of the MRI, wrote Conjoint Family Therapy (1964) and Peoplemaking (1972); her central idea is self-worth, "the pot". Under stress people take incongruent survival stances: placating (leaving out the self), blaming (the other), super-reasonable or computing (self and other) and irrelevant or distracting (all three), as against congruent communication, or levelling. Her techniques include the family life chronology, family sculpting and family reconstruction. Carl Whitaker's symbolic-experiential therapy relied on co-therapy, the battle for structure and the battle for initiative, and the psychotherapy of the absurd (The Family Crucible, 1978, with Augustus Napier). Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy's contextual therapy (Invisible Loyalties, 1973, with Geraldine Spark) studies relational ethics: the ledger, entitlement, the revolving slate, invisible and split loyalties and parentification, met by multidirected partiality and exoneration.

7.7 Behavioural, Postmodern and Evidence-Based Family Work. Behavioural family therapy grew from Gerald Patterson's coercive cycle and parent training, Richard Stuart's contracts (1969) and Robert Liberman (1970), on Thibaut and Kelley's social exchange theory; its tools are functional analysis of family interaction, quid pro quo and good faith contracts, communication training and six-step problem solving. The postmodern turn (second-order cybernetics, social constructionism) made the therapist a collaborator: Anderson and Goolishian's not-knowing stance, Tom Andersen's reflecting team and Seikkula's open dialogue. Narrative family therapy (White and Epston) treats people as creators of their realities and externalises the problem; solution-focused work adds relationship questions. Evidence-based programmes include FFT (Alexander and Parsons), MST (Henggeler, after Bronfenbrenner), MDFT (Liddle), BSFT (Szapocznik) and the Maudsley family-based treatment for adolescent anorexia. Hare-Mustin's feminist critique (1978) challenged neutrality and mother-blaming, and Indian joint families demand cultural competence.

7.8 Couples Therapy. Couples therapy treats the relationship, usually conjointly, after each partner has been screened alone for violence. Traditional behavioural couple therapy (Jacobson and Margolin, 1979), built on social learning and social exchange, combines behaviour exchange (Stuart's caring days), communication training and problem solving. Integrative behavioural couple therapy (Christensen and Jacobson, 1996) adds acceptance through empathic joining, unified detachment and tolerance building. Sue Johnson's emotionally focused couple therapy, developed with Leslie Greenberg and grounded in attachment theory, treats distress as separation distress, tracks the pursue-withdraw cycle and moves through three stages and nine steps, with withdrawer re-engagement and blamer softening. Baucom and Epstein added cognition. John Gottman's research found the five-to-one ratio and the four horsemen, criticism, contempt, defensiveness and stonewalling, with contempt the strongest predictor of divorce, and built the Sound Relationship House. When marital discord maintains depression, couples therapy is the treatment of choice.

7.9 Sex Therapy. William Masters and Virginia Johnson described the sexual response cycle in Human Sexual Response (1966): excitement, plateau, orgasm and resolution, driven by vasocongestion and myotonia, with a male refractory period. Human Sexual Inadequacy (1970) set out their therapy: the couple as the patient, a dual-sex therapy team, two weeks of daily sessions, education and the removal of performance anxiety and spectatoring. Sensate focus moves from non-genital to genital touching and then to intercourse, which is banned at first. James Semans's stop-start technique (1956) and Masters and Johnson's squeeze technique treat premature ejaculation; vaginal dilators treat vaginismus and directed masturbation lifelong orgasm problems. Helen Singer Kaplan's brief sex therapy (The New Sex Therapy, 1974) combined tasks with psychodynamic work, and her triphasic model added desire. Jack Annon's PLISSIT model (1976) grades intervention from Permission to Intensive Therapy. Indian practice must address dhat syndrome and sexual myths.

7.10 Group Psychotherapy. Group psychotherapy is therapy through a group. Joseph Pratt's tuberculosis classes (Boston, 1905), Moreno's naming of group psychotherapy (1932), Slavson's activity group therapy and the wartime Northfield experiments made it a profession. Bion described the work group and three basic assumptions (dependency, fight-flight and pairing); Foulkes founded group analysis, with its conductor and matrix. Irvin Yalom (1970) listed eleven therapeutic factors, from instillation of hope and universality to group cohesiveness, catharsis and existential factors, and treated the group as a social microcosm worked in the here-and-now through experiencing and process illumination. Leaders create the group, build its culture and illuminate process; Lieberman, Yalom and Miles found caring and meaning attribution most helpful. Groups may be homogeneous or heterogeneous, open or closed, ideally of seven or eight. Tuckman's stages are forming, storming, norming, performing and adjourning; problem members include the monopoliser and the help-rejecting complainer.

7.11 Psychodrama, Encounter Groups, Self-Help and Therapeutic Communities. Jacob Levy Moreno's psychodrama, born in his Theatre of Spontaneity (Vienna, 1921), has five elements (protagonist, director, auxiliary egos, audience and stage) and three phases (warm-up, action and sharing); its techniques include role reversal, doubling, mirroring, soliloquy and future projection, resting on spontaneity, catharsis and tele. His sociometry and sociogram (Who Shall Survive?, 1934) map a group's choices, with stars and isolates. Kurt Lewin's 1946 workshop produced the T-group and sensitivity training; encounter groups (Rogers and the human potential movement) aim at personal growth through disclosure and interaction. Alcoholics Anonymous (Bill Wilson and Dr Bob Smith, 1935) gave the twelve steps and twelve traditions and became the model for self-help groups. Milieu therapy uses the whole ward environment as treatment; the therapeutic community, named by Tom Main (1946) and pioneered by Maxwell Jones, rests on democratisation, permissiveness, communalism and reality confrontation.

From the past papers

  1. Couple and family therapists who view the family as a system with homeostasis, boundaries, and feedback loops draw on: (practice mock)

    (a) Operant conditioning (b) Psychosexual stage theory (c) The medical model (d) General Systems Theory (von Bertalanffy) applied to families

    Answer: (d) General Systems Theory (von Bertalanffy) applied to families. Ludwig von Bertalanffy's general systems theory, together with Norbert Wiener's cybernetics, gave family therapy its ideas of wholeness, homeostasis, feedback and boundaries. Operant conditioning explains one organism's behaviour by its consequences, psychosexual stage theory is Freud's account of individual development, and the medical model locates the illness inside one patient, the opposite of systems thinking.

  2. One parent conveys two conflicting messages to the child at the same time is called : (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Faulty communication (b) Social skills deficits (c) Double bind communication (d) Conflict resolution

    Answer: (c) Double bind communication. The double bind (Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland, 1956) is a repeated contradiction between two levels of message, usually words against tone or body, from a person the child depends on, with no escape and no permission to comment. "Faulty communication" is too vague to be the named concept, and social skills deficits and conflict resolution have nothing to do with it.

  3. Gregory Bateson describes a classic example of the interaction between a mother and her son who had fairly well recovered from an acute schizophrenic episode. Bateson described this interaction as follows: '[The son] was glad to see her [his mother] and impulsively put his arm around her shoulders, whereupon she stiffened. He withdrew his arm and she asked, Don't you love me anymore? He then blushed, and she said, Dear, you must not be so easily embarrassed and afraid of your feelings. The patient was able to stay with her only a few minutes more, and following her departure, he assaulted an aide and was put in the isolation room.' Bateson used this interaction to illustrate which ONE of the following concepts? (RML 2021)

    (a) The cybernetic system (b) The homeostatic system (c) The incongruent family dyad (d) The symptomatic double bind

    Answer: (d) The symptomatic double bind. Bateson's group used this visit to show the double bind as a supposed cause of schizophrenic symptoms: the mother's stiffening body says "do not come close", her words say "you should", and her last remark forbids her son to notice the contradiction. Cybernetic and homeostatic systems are general systems ideas, not this pattern, and "incongruent" is Satir's and Rogers's word.

  4. In the context of schizophrenia, the term expressed emotion (EE) refers to: (RML 2021)

    (a) An individual with schizophrenia who has difficulty talking about their feelings. (b) A family environment where overwhelmingly positive comments are made. (c) A lack of emotion displayed in a family. (d) A family environment where there are emotionally loaded communication patterns of a hostile nature.

    Answer: (d) A family environment where there are emotionally loaded communication patterns of a hostile nature. Expressed emotion is the emotional climate of a home as revealed by a key relative's attitudes to the patient, rated with the Camberwell Family Interview for critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement. It is not the patient's own difficulty in talking about feelings, not a home full of positive comments (warmth is rated but is not part of the high-EE index) and not an absence of emotion.

  5. Expressed Emotion (EE) is a significant predictor of relapse in schizophrenia. Which of the following is NOT one of the three core dimensions of high Expressed Emotion? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Hostility (b) Critical Comments (c) Emotional Over-involvement (d) Warmth and Positive Regard

    Answer: (d) Warmth and Positive Regard. In the index of Brown, Birley and Wing (1972) a home is rated high in EE on critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement. The Camberwell Family Interview also rates warmth and positive remarks, but these are not part of the high-EE index.

  6. High EE (Expressed Emotion) is known to cause: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Poor treatment outcome (b) Relapse of psychotic illness (c) Negative symptoms of schizophrenia (d) Violent behavior in schizophrenic patients

    Answer: (b) Relapse of psychotic illness. In Vaughn and Leff's study (1976) about half of the patients who returned to high-EE homes relapsed within nine months, against roughly one in eight of those in low-EE homes. High EE predicts relapse, not the onset of the illness, negative symptoms or violence, and "poor treatment outcome" is too vague to be the best answer.

  7. Reducing contact hours is one of the important strategies in the management of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Interpersonal violence (b) Para suicidal risk (c) Expressed emotion (d) Stubbornness in children

    Answer: (c) Expressed emotion. Vaughn and Leff (1976) found that in high-EE homes less than 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact with the relative protected against relapse, so a day-care centre, a sheltered job or separate meal times are used when the emotional climate cannot be changed quickly. Reducing contact hours is not the strategy for interpersonal violence, parasuicidal risk or stubbornness in children.

  8. Bowen family systems therapy emphasises: (practice mock)

    (a) Narrative externalisation (b) Strategic paradox and prescribing the symptom (c) Differentiation of self, triangulation, and multigenerational transmission (d) Solution-focused exception questions

    Answer: (c) Differentiation of self, triangulation, and multigenerational transmission. These are three of Murray Bowen's eight interlocking concepts. Externalisation belongs to White and Epston's narrative therapy, paradox and prescribing the symptom to Haley, the MRI and the Milan team, and exception questions to de Shazer and Berg's solution-focused therapy.

  9. Which one of the following captures implicit and explicit group communication and interaction patterns? (RML 2020)

    (a) A genogram (b) A cognitive map (c) An ecomap (d) A sociogram

    Answer: (d) A sociogram. Jacob Moreno's sociogram (Who Shall Survive?, 1934) draws the choices and rejections among the members of a group, showing stars, isolates, pairs and cliques. A genogram maps a family across three generations (Bowen; McGoldrick and Gerson), an ecomap maps a family's links to outside systems (Ann Hartman) and a cognitive map is Edward Tolman's mental map of a maze; the word that decides the question is "group".

  10. Minuchin's structural family therapy focuses primarily on: (practice mock)

    (a) Multigenerational transmission of anxiety (b) Subsystems, boundaries (rigid, clear, diffuse), and hierarchies within the family (c) Cognitive distortions in family members (d) Paradoxical injunctions

    Answer: (b) Subsystems, boundaries (rigid, clear, diffuse), and hierarchies within the family. For Minuchin, symptoms are maintained by the family's present organisation, so the therapist maps and changes its subsystems, boundaries and hierarchy. Multigenerational transmission is Bowen's, cognitive distortions belong to cognitive therapy, and paradoxical injunctions to the strategic and Milan therapists.

  11. In Minuchin's structural family therapy, enmeshment refers to: (practice mock)

    (a) Diffuse interpersonal boundaries with over-involvement, intrusion, and loss of autonomy among family members (b) Strategic paradoxical interventions (c) Rigid boundaries with disengagement (d) A complete lack of communication between family members

    Answer: (a) Diffuse interpersonal boundaries with over-involvement, intrusion, and loss of autonomy among family members. Diffuse boundaries produce enmeshment, in which members speak for one another and distress spreads instantly; rigid boundaries produce disengagement, which is option (c). Paradoxical interventions are strategic, and "a complete lack of communication" defines neither style.

  12. Consider this statement: 'Individuals, families, and entire cultures are given a different level of power as creators of their realities.' This statement BEST describes which ONE of the following family therapies? (RML 2021)

    (a) Transgenerational theory (b) Strategic family therapy (c) Structural family therapy (d) Narrative therapy

    Answer: (d) Narrative therapy. Narrative therapy (Michael White and David Epston) is social constructionist: it treats individuals, families and cultures as creators of their realities, with different degrees of power to author or impose a story. Transgenerational (Bowen), strategic (Haley) and structural (Minuchin) therapies are modernist schools in which the expert maps a real emotional process, hierarchy or structure.

  13. What is a potential consequence of poor awareness of the culture and prevalent familial practices in family therapy? (practice mock)

    (a) Wrong assessments and misinterpretations (b) Accurate understanding of the family's dynamics (c) Effective communication with the family (d) Development of a strong therapeutic alliance

    Answer: (a) Wrong assessments and misinterpretations. A therapist who does not know a family's culture may label ordinary closeness, such as a mother who still feeds her adult son or a daughter-in-law who defers to her mother-in-law, as pathology, and the family feels judged. The other three options are what good cultural awareness produces, not poor awareness.

  14. A male adult with Depressive Disorder and marital discord would benefit more from: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Psychodynamic psychotherapy (b) Cognitive behavior therapy (c) Marital & couple therapy (d) Supportive therapy

    Answer: (c) Marital & couple therapy. When marital discord maintains a depression, treating the relationship treats the depression: O'Leary and Beach (1990) and Jacobson and colleagues (1991) found that behavioural marital therapy relieved depression as well as cognitive therapy and was the only treatment that also improved the marriage. Cognitive behaviour therapy, psychodynamic psychotherapy and supportive therapy treat the depressed individual and leave the discord untouched.

  15. The therapeutic factor in group therapy described by Yalom as 'the realisation that one is not alone in their suffering' is: (practice mock)

    (a) Catharsis (b) Universality (c) Altruism (d) Instillation of hope

    Answer: (b) Universality. Universality is discovering that others share one's fears, secrets and miseries. Instillation of hope is seeing others improve, altruism is being helped by helping, and catharsis is expressing feelings that have been held back.

  16. Yalom's therapeutic factors in group therapy include: (practice mock)

    (a) Free association and interpretation (b) Universality, instillation of hope, altruism, corrective recapitulation, cohesiveness (and others) (c) Token reinforcement and shaping (d) Schema reconstruction and mode work

    Answer: (b) Universality, instillation of hope, altruism, corrective recapitulation, cohesiveness (and others). Yalom's eleven factors (1970) are instillation of hope, universality, imparting information, altruism, corrective recapitulation of the primary family group, development of socialising techniques, imitative behaviour, interpersonal learning, group cohesiveness, catharsis and existential factors. Free association and interpretation are psychoanalytic, token reinforcement and shaping are behavioural, and schema reconstruction and mode work belong to schema therapy.

  17. Yalom's 'here-and-now' focus in group therapy refers to: (practice mock)

    (a) Discussing only events outside the group session (b) Strictly limiting the group to didactic content (c) Working with interpersonal patterns as they appear in the group session itself, then illuminating those patterns (d) Cognitive disputation of irrational beliefs

    Answer: (c) Working with interpersonal patterns as they appear in the group session itself, then illuminating those patterns. The group becomes a social microcosm in which members act out their interpersonal patterns, and the leader works in two tiers, experiencing and then process illumination. Discussing only outside events and limiting the group to teaching are the opposite of the here-and-now, and disputing irrational beliefs is Albert Ellis's REBT.

  18. What is the principal role of an "encounter group"? (RML 2022)

    (a) Enables individuals to interact with others in a social setting (b) Encourages team building (c) Aids the therapist in diagnosis (d) Encourages therapy and self-growth through disclosure and interaction

    Answer: (d) Encourages therapy and self-growth through disclosure and interaction. Encounter groups, Carl Rogers's basic encounter group and the favourite instrument of the human potential movement, aim at personal growth, self-awareness and more honest relationships through self-disclosure, here-and-now feeling and frank feedback. Social interaction is too weak an aim, team building describes organisational training, and diagnosis is no part of the method.

  19. Which of the following therapeutic modalities was pioneered by Maxwell Jones and is characterized by the democratic involvement of all community members (staff and patients) in decision-making and daily activities to promote rehabilitation? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Encounter Groups (b) Therapeutic Community (c) Group Analysis (d) Milieu Therapy

    Answer: (b) Therapeutic Community. Maxwell Jones's democratic therapeutic community (Belmont, later the Henderson Hospital, from 1947) flattened the hierarchy and made the daily community meeting and every incident part of treatment; Rapoport summed up its principles as democratisation, permissiveness, communalism and reality confrontation. Encounter groups are Rogers's personal growth groups, group analysis is S. H. Foulkes's, and milieu therapy is the broader idea of a therapeutic ward environment, of which the therapeutic community is one democratic form.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. The double bind (Bateson, Jackson, Haley and Weakland, 1956): a parent sends two conflicting messages at different levels and the child can neither escape nor comment; it was proposed as an early family-communication cause of schizophrenia, and Bateson's mother-and-son hospital visit illustrates the symptomatic double bind. asked 5 times
  2. Tuckman's stages of group development are forming, storming (conflict over influence and leadership), norming (shared norms, roles and cohesion) and performing (1965), with adjourning added by Tuckman and Mary Ann Jensen in 1977. asked 3 times
  3. The therapeutic community, with democratic involvement of staff and patients in decisions and daily life, was pioneered by Maxwell Jones (Belmont, later the Henderson Hospital, from 1947); Tom Main coined the term in 1946. asked 4 times
  4. Expressed emotion is the emotional climate of a home shown in a key relative's attitudes to the patient; its three components (Brown, Birley and Wing, 1972; Camberwell Family Interview) are critical comments, hostility and emotional over-involvement. Warmth is rated but is not one of them. asked twice
  5. High EE predicts relapse of psychotic illness, not onset, negative symptoms or violence; Vaughn and Leff (1976) found that regular medication and less than 35 hours a week of face-to-face contact protect, so reducing contact hours is a way of managing high EE. asked twice
  6. The sociogram (Jacob Moreno, Who Shall Survive?, 1934) maps the choices, rejections and interaction patterns within a group; the genogram maps a family over three generations (Bowen), the ecomap a family's links with outside systems (Ann Hartman, 1975), and the cognitive map is Tolman's. asked twice
  7. Structural family therapy is Salvador Minuchin's: it focuses on the family's present subsystems, boundaries (clear, diffuse, rigid) and hierarchy, and works by joining and then restructuring through enactment, boundary making and unbalancing. asked 3 times
  8. When Maxwell Jones is named, choose the therapeutic community: milieu therapy is the broader use of the whole ward environment as treatment (Bettelheim; Gunderson's five functions), group analysis is S. H. Foulkes's and encounter groups are Rogers's personal growth groups. asked twice
  9. "Individuals, families and entire cultures ... as creators of their realities" describes narrative therapy (Michael White and David Epston), a social constructionist approach, not transgenerational (Bowen), strategic (Haley) or structural (Minuchin) therapy. asked once
  10. The principal role of an encounter group (Carl Rogers; the human potential movement) is personal growth through self-disclosure and interaction; the T-group (Kurt Lewin's 1946 workshop) is for learning group dynamics. asked once
  11. When marital discord maintains a depression, marital and couple therapy is the treatment of choice: it relieves the depression as well as cognitive therapy and also improves the marriage (O'Leary and Beach, 1990; Jacobson and colleagues, 1991). asked once
  12. Masters and Johnson's sexual response cycle (Human Sexual Response, 1966) is excitement, plateau, orgasm, resolution, with no desire phase; Helen Singer Kaplan's triphasic model (1979) is desire, excitement, orgasm. asked once
  13. The stop-start technique (James Semans, 1956) and Masters and Johnson's squeeze technique treat premature ejaculation; both are used on top of sensate focus, Masters and Johnson's graded touching programme with intercourse banned at first (Human Sexual Inadequacy, 1970). asked once
  14. Enmeshment (Minuchin) is diffuse boundaries with over-involvement, intrusion and loss of autonomy; disengagement is rigid boundaries with little contact; clear boundaries are the healthy middle. asked twice
  15. Yalom's here-and-now: the group is a social microcosm in which members show their interpersonal patterns in the session, worked in two tiers, experiencing and then process illumination. asked twice
  16. Irvin Yalom's eleven therapeutic factors (1970): instillation of hope, universality, imparting information, altruism, corrective recapitulation of the primary family group, development of socialising techniques, imitative behaviour, interpersonal learning, group cohesiveness, catharsis and existential factors. asked twice
  17. Schools and founders: structural is Minuchin, multigenerational (Bowen family systems) is Murray Bowen, strategic is Jay Haley and Cloe Madanes, experiential is Virginia Satir and Carl Whitaker; Satir's stances are placating, blaming, super-reasonable (computing), irrelevant (distracting) and congruent (levelling). asked twice
  18. Universality is the Yalom factor of discovering that one is not alone in one's suffering; instillation of hope is seeing others improve, altruism is being helped by helping, and catharsis is expressing feeling held back. asked once
  19. Therapists who describe the family as a system with homeostasis, boundaries and feedback loops draw on Ludwig von Bertalanffy's general systems theory and Norbert Wiener's cybernetics (1948); negative feedback restores the old balance and positive feedback amplifies change. asked once
  20. Bowen family systems therapy emphasises differentiation of self (separating thinking from feeling while staying a self in close relationships), triangulation and multigenerational transmission; emotional cutoff is a sign of low differentiation. asked once
  21. Poor awareness of a family's culture and customary practices leads to wrong assessments and misinterpretations: ordinary closeness is labelled pathology and the alliance suffers. asked once
  22. Psychodrama is Jacob Levy Moreno's, not Maxwell Jones's: five elements (protagonist, director, auxiliary egos, audience, stage), three phases (warm-up, action, sharing), and techniques such as role reversal, doubling and mirroring. asked once
